| durable-execution | 5 | 1.0 | 0.8 | 2.8 | 0.0 | 0.0 | 0.4 | 0.4 | 0.4 | 2.6 | 3.2 |
| code-review | 4 | 1.2 | 0.8 | 0.5 | 0.5 | 2.5 | 1.2 | 0.2 | 1.0 | 4.0 | 2.5 |
| forge-integrated | 2 | 3.5 | 4.0 | 3.0 | 3.0 | 3.0 | 3.5 | 2.5 | 2.0 | 4.0 | 0.5 |

## Steal items, by project

### ABCA (Autonomous Background Coding Agents on AWS) (4)
- Cedar policy engine for tool calls, with a plain @tier("soft") annotation that turns a hard-deny rule into a human-in-the-loop gate, files at agent/policies/hard_deny.cedar and agent/policies/soft_deny.cedar.
- Golden-fixture parity testing between the two Cedar language bindings (cedarpy in the Python agent, @cedar-policy/cedar-wasm in the TypeScript CDK Lambdas) in contracts/cedar-parity, so the same policy cannot silently diverge across runtimes.
- Per-task IAM SessionRole via sts:AssumeRole tagged with user_id, repo and task_id, paired with dynamodb:LeadingKeys conditions on the state tables, so both compute credentials and data access are scoped to one task.
- {'CLI approval scoping': 'bgagent approve --scope tool_type:Bash | write_path:tests/** | rule:<id> | all_session, so an operator can clear a class of future gates instead of clicking through each one.'}
- bgagent nudge, a mid-run steering message the agent acknowledges (nudge_acknowledged) between tool calls, without cancelling the task.
- Fail-closed pre-flight checks (GitHub reachability, repo access) before compute is spent, with typed reasons like GITHUB_UNREACHABLE and REPO_NOT_FOUND_OR_NO_ACCESS.
- ADR-002's versioned least-privilege CDK bootstrap policy with a computeBootstrapHash() check, giving an auditable trail for the deployer's own IAM footprint.

### Cursor Cloud Agents (Background Agents, Automations, Bugbot) (4)
- Three-tier secret model, plain environment variables, Runtime Secrets whose values are scrubbed from tool output, transcripts and commit messages, and Dockerfile-only build secrets mounted with a secret mount flag that never reach the running agent
- A built-in Cloud MCP server that exposes a session transcript, run events, environment details and setup logs as queryable tool data, not only a scrollback pane
- Three named network access modes, allow all, default plus allowlist, allowlist only, with environment-level override and a team-level admin lock, plus a documented warning never to widen the artifact-upload host to a wildcard bucket
- A short-lived, Cursor-signed OIDC JWT obtainable from a local socket inside the agent and exchangeable with AWS, GCP, Azure or Vault, instead of a static cloud credential
- Follow-up messages queued for delivery at the agent next tool call instead of interrupting mid-action
- Bugbot advisory-by-default check conclusion, neutral unless fail on unresolved is turned on, incremental diff-only review, and patch identity deduplication against a local pre-push review pass
- Rollouts posts a monitoring plan as a PR comment naming which systems and telemetry signals it will watch, then reports a per-environment verdict, healthy, regression detected, inconclusive, after deploy

### Minions (Stripe) (4)
- The blueprint state machine itself, deterministic nodes (lint, push, PR creation, CI gates) and agent nodes (implement, fix CI failures) as typed graph nodes, exactly what our Temporal workflow's activities and gate activities already are, just not exposed as an authorable graph other capability classes can compose
- Per-node context engineering as an explicit mechanism, restrict tools, alter the system prompt, trim conversation context for each subtask node, rather than one session that sees everything
- A hard local lint loop before the first push, under five seconds using a caching daemon, so the branch has a real chance of passing CI on attempt one, cutting CI queue time and the token cost of a doomed first round
- The two-round CI cap and its stated reason, diminishing marginal returns from repeated full CI loops, is the same number we chose and gives us an external citation for it
- Toolshed's curation default, give each agent a small default tool subset from a shared registry (near 500 tools) and let it opt into named thematic groups, rather than exposing the whole tool catalogue to every session
- One rule-file format read by both the interactive IDE tools (Cursor, Claude Code) and the unattended agent, directory-scoped rather than global, so coding conventions are authored once
- CI-generated tickets carry a one-click "start an agent" button (their flaky-test ticket flow), a cheap signal-to-task UX pattern for our console's own alert and ticket views

### GitLab Duo Agent Platform (4)
- {'Composite identity': 'a dual-identity OAuth token that binds a service account (the agent) to the human who triggered it through a "dynamic scope", enforcing the intersection of both identities\' permissions and stamping every action with both an author_id and a human_author_id for compliance attribution.'}
- The layered reader/writer flow pattern to break the "lethal trifecta" (access to sensitive systems, exposure to untrusted content, autonomous action) split a generalist agent into a read-only agent that sees untrusted content and a write-only agent that never does, documented with full YAML flow-config examples in security_threats.md.
- {'Tool governance matrix': "every tool classified Read/Write/Delete, every rule set to Always Allow/Always Ask/Always Deny across three access types (Cloud/Local/Background), rules cascade group to project but a project can only match or tighten its group's rule, and the whole thing fails closed if the governance service errors."}
- {'Tool-response security layer in duo_workflow_service/security': "strips hidden HTML, Markdown and Mermaid comments from tool output, HTML-encodes dangerous tags such as <system> and <goal> before they re-enter the model's context, and can route responses through a third-party prompt-injection scanner (HiddenLayer) in log-only or interrupt mode."}
- Glob-based exclusion-rule files (mr-review-automated-rules.yaml) that keep whole files or diffs out of an agent's context by pattern, with group-level templates that cascade to projects, used to keep the Code Review Flow off generated files and vendored code.
- AI Gateway's AIGW_LITELLM__MODEL_METADATA_FILE mechanism registers capability metadata (context window, pricing, tool-calling support) for a self-hosted or custom model at startup so LiteLLM does not silently strip parameters it does not recognise. Directly applicable since we also run LiteLLM.

### Claude Code (3)
- The gateway compatibility contract (code.claude.com/docs/en/llm-gateway-protocol): forward anthropic-version and anthropic-beta as open, unfiltered lists rather than a pinned allowlist, since the client adds new beta strings across releases. factory-gateway should take the same posture for any header it does not actively need to inspect.
- The settings-precedence exceptions table: nine specific keys (disableClaudeAiConnectors, isolatePeerMachines, maxEffortLevel, and others) are hardcoded so the stricter value wins regardless of which scope nominally has precedence. This is a named, auditable safety ratchet, not a convention. Our policy YAML could mark a subset of keys the same way, so a project cannot loosen a cluster-wide cap even by accident.
- sandbox-runtime's resolved-address check: before dialling an allowed hostname, the egress proxy resolves it once and rejects the connection if the resolved address is loopback, link-local, or one of a hardcoded list of cloud metadata endpoints, independent of the domain allowlist. Our gateway's egress path allows Git, the module proxy, and the collector by hostname; this closes the DNS-rebinding and metadata-SSRF gap a hostname allowlist alone leaves open.
- Per-call cost and attribution headers (x-claude-code-session-id, agent-id, parent-agent-id) as first-class, forwardable metadata for a gateway to bucket spend by sub-agent, not just by task. Our gateway prices and holds per task; splitting cost by agent-id inside a task would sharpen the ledger once we run multi-agent tasks.
- The tiered hook and plugin architecture (prepend, user, append) with one privileged primitive (next.to) that the host runtime refuses outside a managed tier: loading the equivalent of sec-default through a side channel produces an inert plugin. Worth the pattern, not the code: a policy hook that only a control-plane-issued context can invoke, so a compromised or careless worker-side plugin cannot pass itself off as policy.
- The candid Security Limitations and Known Limitations sections in the sandbox-runtime README (domain fronting defeats content-blind egress filtering, allowAppleEvents removes isolation rather than weakening it, Linux proxy enforcement is env-var based and fails closed only for programs that honour it). Publishing what a sandbox does not cover is cheap and raises trust; our own sandbox docs should carry the same kind of section instead of implying default-deny egress is airtight.

### Claude Managed Agents (3)
- Vault credentials are write only and substituted only at egress, for mcp_oauth, static_bearer and environment_variable types. Tighten our own MCP tool call proxying in factory-gateway so a vault read never lands in agent visible state, even transiently in a log line.
- Outcomes give a bounded revise and regrade loop, a rubric graded definition of done checked by a separate context grader, max_iterations default 3 maximum 20, with results satisfied, needs_revision, max_iterations_reached, failed or interrupted. Worth adding as an explicit workflow node ahead of or alongside our stronger model review gate.
- Every policy flavoured event carries an evaluated_permission and a reason_code. Add equivalent structured fields to our own gateway audit log so a policy decision's reasoning is queryable after the fact, not just its outcome.
- Scheduled deployment firing is jittered, up to 15 percent of the interval, minimum 5 seconds, maximum 9 minutes, and a schedule auto pauses when a dependency is archived. Copy both details for our own cron triggered tasks to avoid thundering herd firing and dangling schedules.
- Session hours are billed to the millisecond and only while status is running, idle, rescheduling and terminated time is free. A precedent worth matching in our own cost ledger's granularity.

### Devin (3)
- devin-connect's outbound-only tunnel plus a small gateway with a declarative, hot-reloadable network allowlist, so a project's egress list changes without a Cilium policy redeploy and without opening an inbound port on the runner side
- RFC 8693 OIDC token exchange as a CLI step before a CI job calls an external control plane (setup-devin-oidc), reusable anywhere we still hand a job a static token
- A policy hierarchy with mandatory rules fixed at a higher level and recommended rules a lower level may override (Security Profiles), a concrete shape for splitting our one flat policy YAML into a floor plus a per-project narrowing file
- Playbooks as versioned, macro-invoked ("!name") instruction documents kept separate from freeform context, a workable shape for the spec-driven intake we do not have yet
- DeepWiki, an auto-generated, git-trackable wiki and diagram per repository with a maintainer-editable override file, a direct answer to our own "no project memory, no codebase index" gap
- The multi-pane live session view (plan, shell, IDE, browser) as a concrete target for the real-time stream and diff view our console admits it lacks

### Factory (Droids) (3)
- CI Steward limits from droid-action - config read only from the default branch, editing tools withheld unless the failing job is in `fix.scope`, `protected_paths` reverted after the run, three separate budgets (`max_retries`, `max_fix_attempts`, `max_runs_per_pr`)
- Two-pass model review - a candidates pass (`review_candidates_max_turns` 100) then a validator pass (`review_validator_max_turns` 40) that throws out false positives before anything is posted
- Software Factory coverage map - one console row per lifecycle stage (triage, code-gen, validate, release, document, monitor) showing automation count, health and which repositories are covered
- Mission Control layout - orchestrator, worker and validator sessions in a left rail, per-role model dropdowns, feature checklist, live progress log, time and credits in the header, pause and re-plan button
- Incident runbook as agent-maintained memory - after each RCA the agent updates `.factory/skills/incident-guidelines/SKILL.md` so the next alert of the same kind starts from learned guidance
- Agent Readiness report - score a repository on how well an agent can build, run and test it, offer a fix task, and refuse long autonomous runs below a set level
- Cost estimate before a run - `total runs ≈ features + 2 × milestones` as a floor, shown before approval
- Extension-only policy merge - arrays union and cannot be removed, scalars first-wins, object keys locked per level, and a malformed org file resolves to an empty policy that is logged, never silently skipped

### Codegen (3)
- Typed trace records per run (message_type ACTION, PLAN_EVALUATION, FINAL_ANSWER, ERROR, USER_MESSAGE, FIX_PR_ERRORS with thought, tool_input, tool_output, observation) as the shape of our normalised event stream and trace view
- Explicit terminal statuses TIMEOUT, MAX_ITERATIONS_REACHED and OUT_OF_TOKENS next to FAILED, so a budget stop is visible on the board as its own state
- Per-repo setup commands run once, then a filesystem snapshot with a 24 hour TTL and a manual invalidate button, plus an agent run that drafts the setup commands
- Check auto-fixer settings as data (check_retry_count 0 to 10, per-check retry counts, ignored_checks, per-check custom prompts) and a "ban" call that stops all agents on a PR and ignores further check events
- One agent per context, with ticket, PR and chat thread comments routed into the same running agent as interrupts
- Remote code-server editor and a web preview on port 3000, both linked from the trace page, with a per-session generated password
- Rule file discovery (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, .cursorrules and globs) under a 25,000 character budget, shown in the trace as the files that were loaded

### AI Software Factory (coleam00) (3)
- Mutation-testing calibration harness (harness/mutations/defects.json plus run.py) that seeds known bugs and measures what fraction of the gate pipeline actually catches them, instead of trusting a single pass or fail run
- A machine-checked capability contract (pack.json's capabilities map of expected CLI flags per action, read by a doctor self-check) to catch drift between a wrapper and the engine it drives before it fails live
- The evidence-scrubbing rule from Archon's SDLC pack, stripping credentials from any string before it reaches a command line or a log, written as a tested invariant rather than a hope
- Airflow-style trigger_rule join semantics (none_failed_min_one_success, all_done) for fan-in after parallel review branches, named and reused rather than reinvented per workflow
- Per-node output_format JSON-schema contracts paired with an outcome_field used for conditional branching, so every model step returns a typed result the next step consumes
- The holdout idea, hidden acceptance scenarios the coding agent never sees and that only verification checks, worth adopting even though their own version enforces it by convention only

### ESF (Engineering Software Factory) (3)
- CheckGateIntegrity: compares the working-tree content of declared gate files against their baseline git blob with git hash-object, not diff, so it survives git update-index --assume-unchanged, commit-the-tamper, and diff.mnemonicPrefix evasion. Directly portable to our own policy-file and gate-file protection.
- A per-run egress probe (public canary, cloud metadata endpoint, uid check) that runs before the agent starts and sets EGRESS_UNVERIFIED on failure, gated behind an explicit acknowledge_open_egress config flag. We only enforce egress with static NetworkPolicy and never actively measure it.
- threatmon: 40 stdlib regexp rules, one file, each with a documented false-positive and false-negative note in the source comment. Cheap, auditable, no ML. Worth copying as a first-pass detector layer feeding our existing supervisor-escalation mechanism.
- BLOCKED as a first-class workflow outcome, written by the agent itself to a JSON file, framed explicitly as removing the incentive for an agent under pressure to probe out of bounds instead of admitting it is stuck.
- The QMS/assurance risk-class schema (R0 to R4, highest-risk-wins union across path-glob rules, conflicts fail closed) and its four gate contract types (verification, independent-review, provider-snapshot, provider-ack) is a more formal vocabulary than our own path-risk-class idea and worth stealing wholesale.
- Verdict separation: a human REJECTED ends the run, but the underlying VerificationResult stays a separate recorded SUCCESS fact. Gates-passed and change-accepted are different facts. Our own approval schema should keep them equally distinct.

### Fabro (3)
- CSS-like model stylesheet with a specificity cascade (universal, shape, class, id, last-declared wins) for routing models per workflow step, instead of one flat tier per task
- Sandbox snapshot identity hashed from the image or Dockerfile text plus resources plus provider key, so an unchanged environment is built once and reused, not rebuilt per run
- Hard exclusion of the GitHub Workflows permission scope from every agent-facing token, stated as policy rather than left as an accident of the current scope list
- {'fabro doctor': 'one command that validates vault secrets, provider scopes and reachability before a run starts, a preflight check we do not have'}
- Automations as revision-checked (ETag, If-Match) objects that carry their own last_error field, so a broken schedule is visible on the object, not buried in logs
- Single static binary plus SQLite as the entire server, worth copying as a stripped-down local or demo mode of our own stack

### FactoryKit (3)
- {'Egress-time credential injection': 'platform tokens and model keys are brokered into outbound requests and scoped per repository, encrypted at rest, and never enter the sandbox process; only the repo\'s own application env vars do. Matches our "identity belongs to the task" principle almost exactly and is described as running in production.'}
- The agent never runs git. Only the control plane commits, pushes and opens the pull request (one PR per changed repo, follow-up messages stack commits). Removes an entire class of credential and integrity risk versus giving the agent a write token.
- {'Browser-regression evidence attached to every PR': "the repo's own checks run first (with up to 3 fix attempts), the agent self-reviews the diff, then a real browser session verifies the change in plain language and the recording is attached to the PR. We have no UI/runtime evidence step at all today."}
- {'Multi-model consensus assertion (their open-source QA engine)': 'Claude and Gemini both judge an assertion, a third model (Gemini 3.1 Pro) arbitrates on disagreement, with a fail-on-disagreement mode to surface ambiguity instead of hiding it. A cheap pattern for any verdict we want more than one model to agree on.'}
- {"Their QA engine's secure-script-runner.ts (src/utils/secure-script-runner.ts, over 1700 lines)": 'an acorn-based AST allowlist that restricts an AI-generated Playwright script to a fixed set of methods, blocks constructor/__proto__/prototype, and validates fetch URLs against localhost/private-IP ranges plus DNS-rebinding at resolution time. A concrete, tested pattern for constraining any code an agent is allowed to emit and run.'}
- Snapshot VM templates baked per coding agent (CLI plus Playwright and a bundled Chromium), forked per run and keyed to a bootstrap hash so bumping the hash forces a rebuild. Same warm-start idea we could apply to our per-task pod images to cut cold-start time.
- Server-owned run state with live narration streamed into a server-side buffer that the UI tails, replaying from the last seen event, so closing the laptop is "a display problem, not a lost run." Directly addresses a console gap we flagged (no real-time stream of the agent's work).

### Fusion (3)
- PROMPT.md as one committed markdown artifact per task, holding plan steps, file scope and acceptance criteria together, so plan and acceptance criteria are not two separate systems.
- A structured JSON reviewer verdict contract (verdict field APPROVE, APPROVE_WITH_NOTES or REVISE, plus a notes field) with a documented prose-fallback parser and an explicit "malformed" classification, so a review is a machine-actionable gate, not free text a human has to read to find the answer.
- {'Review depth that scales with a task complexity rating': 'light checks for trivial tasks, multi-pass review for complex ones, so review cost tracks task risk instead of a flat policy.'}
- {'Scope-checked revision routing': 'a REVISE that stays in scope goes back into the same PROMPT.md revision section, a REVISE that finds an out-of-scope problem forks a new follow-up task instead of growing the current one.'}
- Per-lane model routing (separate provider and model choice for the executor, planner, validator, merger and two summarisation lanes) with an explicit override precedence of task, then project, then global lane, then default.
- GitHub, GitLab and Linear issue import plus GitHub tracking issues as one pattern for multi-forge intake, directly against our own "one forge only" gap.

### Loki Mode (3)
- The Evidence Receipt's split between deterministic FACTS (diff base/head SHA, diff_sha256, test/build exit codes, gate verdicts) and labelled AI ASSESSMENTS (council verdict), with a headline computed only from facts (autonomy/lib/proof-generator.py, function _compute_headline). We compute similar facts but never bundle them into one portable, re-hashable file a reviewer can carry off our infrastructure.
- The command loki proof verify re-derives the diff from the recorded base SHA and recomputes the integrity hash, so tamper and drift are two independent, offline, non-LLM checks (autonomy/lib/proof-verify.py). Worth copying as a standalone script over our own timeline records.
- The receiver-signed Ed25519 JWT attestation for remote builds, with keys published unauthenticated at /.well-known/jwks.json and a kid-based rotation list for retired keys (docs/SIGNED-RECEIPTS.md). A clean, low-dependency pattern for letting an outside auditor verify anything our factory-gateway emits without an account or API key.
- Four-state verification outcomes that are never collapsed into each other, VERIFIED, FAILED, ABSENT (no attestation present) and NOT CHECKED (verifier could not run). Our own alert-verdict and gate-supervision language could borrow this precision.
- The command loki doctor --airgap names every host the engine will contact before you write a NetworkPolicy. Worth doing for our own project onboarding, list every egress target a new project's sandbox will need before the Cilium policy is authored.
- The cost and time quote before any spend, in "loki quickstart --dry-run --json", bound to the exact input by a SHA-256 digest so a stale preview cannot silently authorise a different build. A pattern for our own gateway's cost hold, quote first, bind the quote to specifics, then charge.
- docs/EVALUATING.md puts one runnable command next to every marketing claim and states outright what is not true yet (no independent benchmark placement, generation is not air-gapped). Worth stealing as a format for any comparison page we publish about our own factory.

### Machinist (3)
- the argv-template capability model: worker.toml holds a fixed argv array per executor, and the only substitution allowed is a single {{machinist.model}} placeholder that must be a whole standalone --flag=value token, never argv[0] and never a partial word (internal/config/config.go, validateCommand); untrusted prompt text only ever lands inside the piped stdin string, never in argv
- the classify-then-merge example's merge gate (examples/workflows/risk_delivery/gate.py): a trusted, non-agent script re-derives every fact needed to merge (exact head SHA, mergeability, check rollups, unresolved review threads) straight from the GitHub API instead of trusting the agent's self-report, and only auto-approves a hard-coded narrow case (low risk, at most 10 markdown or text files, at most 200 changed lines, no renames or executables)
- crash-safe state publication: write to a temp file, fsync, atomic rename, then fsync the containing directory (internal/runner/runner.go, writeResult/createDurableDirectory/syncDirectory), and a worker whose lease expires unexpectedly fails the run closed for manual reconciliation instead of silently handing it to another worker
- hard-fail config migration with no legacy shim: loadConfigFile rejects removed or renamed keys (pipelines, shepherd, agents to commands) with a specific migration message baked into the error, rather than quietly accepting the old shape (internal/config/config.go)
- a one-tick retry safeguard in the console: before letting a human retry an interrupted or cancelled job, the UI forces an explicit checkbox, 'I have verified the previous worker process has stopped' (internal/controlplane/web/src/task-detail.jsx), a cheap guard against double execution
- uniform secret redaction and strict input allow-listing in the GitHub adapter: every logged or error string is passed through a token-redaction regex, and repository slugs, actors and labels are checked against regexes before they reach gh's argv, with no shell involved at all (internal/controlplane/github_cli.go)

### Open-Inspect (3)
- A ttyd-based live terminal proxy (packages/sandbox-runtime/src/sandbox_runtime/ttyd_proxy) plus a diff library in the Next.js client (@pierre/diffs) close exactly the two gaps our own baseline names as missing, no real-time work stream and no diff view.
- Filesystem-snapshot resume on most sandbox backends (Modal, Vercel Sandbox and OpenComputer support prebuilt images plus snapshot restore, Daytona and E2B use persistent pause and resume) cuts a follow-up prompt's cold start close to zero, worth adapting for our gVisor pods.
- The per-session cost cap (packages/control-plane/src/session/budget-service.ts) derives the spend delta from the harness's own cumulative cost report in an idempotent way, a resend adds nothing and a dropped event is repaired by the next, a clean pattern for a live per-task cost readout in our console.
- Git credentials are brokered per session through an on-demand credential helper (packages/control-plane/src/source-control/github-credential-authority.ts) that mints a short-lived GitHub App installation token, never a static token at rest in the sandbox, validating our own two-per-session Forgejo token pattern.
- Pull requests are opened with the acting human's own GitHub OAuth token rather than a bot token, specifically so a bot-authored PR can never be self-approved (README security model, docs/AUTH.md). A different mechanism from our branch-protection whitelist for the same goal, worth comparing.
- The Slack repo classifier (a fast, non-reasoning model fed the message, thread context, channel name and repository descriptions, with an explicit "unknown" fallback that asks the user) is a reusable pattern for the day we take chat-originated tasks.
- Their Environments concept (a named, ordered set of up to 10 repositories with their own secret scope and optional prebuilt image, snapshotted at session creation so later edits never change an in-flight session) is a clean model for one task that must touch several repositories coherently, which our single-repo TaskBrief does not have.

### Open SWE (3)
- Workflow-file push guard (agent/middleware/workflow_push_guard.py) parses every git push, and any push touching .github/workflows/ is held for a human approval card in Slack or the dashboard before it reaches GitHub, keyed to a content fingerprint so a later commit re-opens approval.
- The /baby-sit skill (agent/bundled_skills/baby-sit/SKILL.md) watches PR checks by webhook plus a token-free 10-minute fallback poll, classifies a failure as deterministic, flaky or ambiguous before acting, and reruns only the failed jobs with gh run rerun --failed, capped at three reruns per head SHA.
- Expedited Slack review (docs/reference/expedited-slack-review.md) posts a small PR's full diff as a Slack card, needs one non-author approval, and fingerprints the exact head SHA so an unrelated later commit keeps the vote and a diff-changing one invalidates it.
- {'Per-repository review style learning with a dry_run before approve gate (agent/review/styles.py)': 'the reviewer earns write authority on a repository only after its synthesized style profile has run in observe-only mode.'}
- {'A reviewer eval harness with golden PR review comments mined from real repositories (evals/reviewer/golden_comments': 'keycloak, grafana, discourse, cal.com, sentry) scored by an LLM judge, exactly the benchmark-of-agent-success-rate our own design admits it lacks.'}
- Settings resolve through four sparse-override tiers, instance, workspace, user, thread, each unset field inheriting the one before (docs/reference/workspaces.md), a cleaner shape than one flat per-project config.
- A tiny SandboxBackendProtocol (ls, read, write, edit, glob, grep, execute, id) made six sandbox providers (LangSmith, Daytona, Runloop, E2B, Modal, local) drop-in interchangeable behind one interface, comparable to and worth benchmarking against our own Go Harness interface.

### OpenAI Symphony (3)
- One persistent "Codex Workpad" issue comment, edited in place with plan, acceptance criteria, validation and notes, as the live progress view in the forge
- Blocker-aware dispatch, only issues whose blockers are terminal get an agent, plus agents filing follow-up issues into Backlog with blockedBy links
- Reconcile on every tick, a human stops or parks work by moving the issue state, the orchestrator kills the agent and cleans the workspace
- In-worker continuation turns on the same agent thread up to max_turns, with a short continuation prompt instead of the full brief
- Host-side execution of tracker tools with the declared secret variables stripped from the child environment (unset plus Port env false)
- docs/token_accounting.md rules for Codex app-server usage events (total_token_usage over last_token_usage, delta against last reported) for our Codex harness
- A per-repo WORKFLOW.md contract, YAML front matter plus strict Liquid prompt, hot-reloaded with last-known-good fallback
- Codex approval policy in reject-object form and treating turn_input_required as a blocked state instead of a hang

### OpenHands (3)
- JMESPath webhook filters (trigger.filter evaluated by evaluate_filter() against the raw payload) decouple "which events matter" from forge-specific parsing code, worth copying for factoryd's intake regardless of language.
- {'The RemoteWorkspace interface (one abstract backend, five concrete implementations': "Docker, Apptainer, hosted API, Cloud, Kubernetes) is a clean template for letting our Harness interface's execution side vary without touching agent code."}
- The kubernetes-sigs/agent-sandbox operator (SandboxTemplate, SandboxWarmPool, pause/resume via spec.operatingMode) solves gVisor cold-start latency with warm pods, a problem our worker plane has not addressed.
- {'Monaco diff view plus an xterm.js live terminal in one conversation screen closes two gaps our own baseline names as missing': 'no diff view, no real-time work stream.'}
- The Agent Client Protocol (ACP) subprocess-relay pattern for driving a third-party CLI harness (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini) behind one UI validates our own Harness-interface plan and gives an open wire format to converge on instead of inventing one.
- The SecurityRisk enum (UNKNOWN, LOW, MEDIUM, HIGH) plus ConfirmationPolicyBase (AlwaysConfirm, NeverConfirm, ConfirmRisky) is a usable risk vocabulary even if we keep the actual gate outside the agent process.

### Optio (3)
- scripts/optio-git-credential: a git credential-helper that fetches a GitHub token on demand via an HMAC-signed internal API call, so no static PAT ever sits in the pod's environment.
- AgentAdapter interface (validateSecrets, buildContainerConfig, parseResult) hosts 7 CLI harnesses behind one seam. Confirms a thin Harness interface scales without a heavier plugin system.
- A fake ContainerRuntime implementation runs the full reconcile-to-merge pipeline in CI with no live Kubernetes cluster, per .github/workflows/ci.yml's e2e job.
- Polymorphic workflow_triggers table keyed by (target_type, target_id) with one fireTrigger dispatcher, instead of one trigger table per source.
- Native iOS and Android apps for watching and approving tasks, and a Slack bot trigger, next to the web UI.
- Ticket-provider adapters for GitHub, GitLab, Linear, Jira and Notion behind one ticket model, relevant to our own 'one forge only' gap.

### Paperclip (3)
- {'The IssueExecutionPolicy / IssueExecutionStage / IssueExecutionStageParticipant shape and its issue_execution_decisions audit table (docs/guides/execution-policy.md)': 'a generalised multi-stage review object instead of one fixed approval flag.'}
- {'The Task Watchdog pattern (doc/TASK-WATCHDOG.md)': 'an opt-in agent per issue subtree that wakes only once every leaf has stopped, and answers "was this stop legitimate", separate from liveness or stall detection.'}
- {'The MCP Access Governance model (doc/MCP-ACCESS-GOVERNANCE.md)': 'Application -> Connection -> Catalog -> Profile -> Policy -> Gateway -> Action Request, deny-by-default, with a call-event audit log per tool invocation.'}
- {'The budget_policies schema (packages/db/src/schema/budget_policies.ts)': 'one scope+window+metric table instead of separate per-company and per-agent budget columns.'}
- {'npx paperclipai test-drive': 'one command for a fully disposable, zero-commitment instance, good for a try-before-you-install console mode.'}

### Sortie (3)
- the cumulative per-issue token budget (agent.max_tokens, cost_budget tool) with matching advisory and enforcing surfaces reading the same number
- a one-shot credential verification pre-flight before a session claims a task, shared across every adapter kind
- the "three writes only" tracker boundary, the orchestrator writes observed events, never a judgement call, back to the tracker
- a kind-based adapter registry with blank-import self-registration and a hard no-cross-adapter-imports rule
- an AGENTS.md-style Always/Ask-first/Never contract for AI contributors, enforced by lint and generic-naming tests
- an 11-row disposition table that normalises every harness's heterogeneous exit signal into one of four outcomes

### SWE-AF and CodeAF (AgentField) (3)
- The deterministic git fast-path, mechanical worktree setup, conflict-free merge and cleanup done by plain git calls instead of an LLM agent, with the LLM called in only for real conflicts. SWE-AF's own docstring says this replaced 23 of 88 agent calls and about 20 minutes of a 103-minute build ('swe_af/execution/git_fast_path.py'). A ready template for cutting our own agent-call count on mechanical git steps.
- The CI-gate anti-cheat rule. The bounded, two-cycle LLM fix loop that runs after a failing PR check is explicitly forbidden from 'pytest.skip', 'xfail', commenting tests out or loosening assertions. Worth lifting near verbatim into our own reproduction-gate fix prompts.
- Typed, severity-rated technical debt objects instead of silent scope-narrowing. When an issue cannot fully meet its acceptance criteria, the result carries a structured item with a severity and a reason that survives into the PR body, rather than the agent quietly declaring success.
- Risk-proportional review depth. A middle-loop Issue Advisor can route a flagged issue into a four-call path with QA, reviewer and a synthesiser running in parallel, while ordinary issues take a cheap path with up to five inner coder-reviewer retries. A concrete version of 'scrutiny scales with risk', an idea our own supervisor design only sketches.
- CodeAF's spend-rail catalogue ('docs/LIMITS.md'). Fourteen named dollar and time limits, each documented with what it blocks, what zero means for that specific rail, and a visible receipt string shown to the user. A ready checklist for our own budget defaults and console copy.
- The sub-harness issue-level entry point ('implement_issue'). Lets a caller that has already planned hand off one scoped issue for four to eight LLM calls, instead of paying for a full multi-hour build. A useful shape for letting our supervisor delegate small, pre-scoped work cheaply.

### Tembo (3)
- Live per-session diff and event stream as a first-class API object, worth copying as a structured per-task feed instead of only kanban-card state
- Sandbox snapshotting and pre-warmed projects to cut cold-start time, the same problem our gVisor and build-VM path already has
- Automatic session recovery when its sandbox restarts, a pattern for resuming a Temporal task after its worker pod is evicted
- Port-tunnel previews of the running app for reviewers, a cheaper trust signal than a diff alone
- A published stop-and-comment guardrail for alert-driven tasks, one rule we can add to our own alert-to-issue path
- Multi-surface task creation (Slack, Linear, forge mention, a Raycast extension) in addition to a web console
- A customer-facing per-second and per-token cost table, not only an operator-facing cost view

### GitHub Copilot cloud agent and Agent HQ (3)
- Org/repo two-tier firewall override (Enabled / Disabled / Let repositories decide), configurable independently per agent surface
- copilot-setup-steps.yml: one named job, a narrow honoured key set, hard 59-minute cap, as a deterministic worker bootstrap file
- Hooks taxonomy (sessionStart, preToolUse allow/deny, postToolUse, agentStop, errorOccurred) as minimal, scriptable lifecycle instrumentation
- Custom Agents precedence (repo overrides org overrides enterprise), resolved by simple file lookup, for shared but overridable agent profiles
- Agents panel pattern: one running-session view, natural-language session search, live steering, stop, archive, share link
- Automation rationale/confidence autonomy dial paired with an explicit not-a-security-boundary warning in the UI
- Multi-channel intake (issue assignment, PR @mention, scheduled automation) beyond issue-or-telemetry-only

### Centaur (3)
- The Kubernetes Agent Sandbox CRD (agents.x-k8s.io/v1alpha1) with pause (spec.replicas=0) and resume (spec.replicas=1) against a retained state PVC, from services/api-rs/rfcs/0001-sandbox-abstraction.md, a cheaper lifecycle than destroy-and-recreate for a task that idles and resumes.
- The per-turn requester-credential union in services/api-rs/rfcs/0005-per-turn-requester-credentials.md: bind the human who actually triggered a turn, verified by signature, as an extra credential principal for that turn only, so a shared bot in a channel still opens a PR as the requesting person.
- Typed secret declarations colocated in each tool's own pyproject.toml (http, oauth_token, gcp_auth, pg_dsn types), keeping the capability a tool needs next to the tool's own code rather than only centralised in policy.
- Workflow queue isolation classes (agent, webhook, etl, maintenance, from services/api-rs/rfcs/0003-python-workflow-host.md) so a long ETL-style workflow cannot starve a chat-facing turn sharing the same worker pool.
- A warm pool of pre-spawned sandbox containers to remove cold-start latency, referenced in the launch post and as WARM_POOL_ENABLED in docs/pages/reference/configuration.mdx.
- Streaming an agent's turn into the same chat thread a human is already watching, with a reply as the intervention, instead of a task board a human has to remember to check.

### Coder Tasks / Coder Agents (with Boundary and AgentAPI) (3)
- Agent loop moved out of the disposable sandbox and into the durable control plane, so the workspace never needs outbound access to a model provider and a crashed or killed sandbox does not lose conversation state
- Real-time chat UI with a live git diff sidebar and a plan-mode step, both rendered from the same event stream the API exposes, not a screen-scraped terminal
- Landlock-based egress enforcement (landjail) as a capability-free alternative to iptables/CAP_NET_ADMIN for the common case, falling back to network-namespace interception (nsjail) only where the LSM is unavailable
- Default-deny allowlist rule engine with three matchable keys (method, domain, path) that OR together, small enough to read as a single Go file (rulesengine/engine.go)
- Post-response cost booking per call plus proactive steering (warn the agent as it nears its cap) as a cheap addition on top of a hard ceiling, not a replacement for one

### Kelos (3)
- The GitHub App credential path: appID, installationID and private key are exchanged for a short-lived installation token that a controller writes to a kubelet-synced Secret-backed file, so the token refreshes in place without a pod restart, worth copying wherever we deliver a rotating token into a running sandbox
- TaskSpawner's declarative event-to-task mapping: a JSONPath fieldMapping plus filters/excludeFilters plus a Go text/template promptTemplate turns any webhook payload into a Task, a compact reference design if we ever add a second, non-Forgejo intake source
- The Console's per-Session Changes tab (a live diff view) and lane-style Sessions list (grouped by review state, each carrying a PR and checks badge) is close to the real-time, diff-first watch surface our own console admittedly lacks
- A first-party agent skill (npx skills add kelos-dev/kelos) that teaches a user's own coding agent to drive the tool conversationally, replacing hand-written YAML and CLI flags as the onboarding path
- A CronJob-based OAuth refresher pattern (one CronJob per labelled credentials Secret, scoped to that Secret only) for keeping provider OAuth bundles fresh independently of any running agent

### OpenSandbox (3)
- Server-wide [secure_runtime] config (type/docker_runtime/k8s_runtime_class) validated at startup, refusing to boot on a mismatch: a clean pattern for offering runc/gVisor/Kata-QEMU/Kata-Firecracker as one operator choice instead of a per-task decision nobody audits.
- Kata-Firecracker as a documented, ~125ms-overhead microVM tier between gVisor and full Kata-QEMU: directly answers our own noted gap that gVisor on systrap is slow for builds and microVMs need bare metal.
- Egress sidecar deny.always/allow.always overlay (components/egress/pkg/policy/always_rules.go, MergeAlwaysOverlay): platform rules that unconditionally outrank any tenant-supplied network policy, hot-reloaded every 60s with no restart.
- Credential Vault: the sandboxed process only ever sees a fake credential; the sidecar injects the real bearer/apiKey/basic header into matching outbound requests by host, method and path. Worth weighing against our own design, where the worker process holds two real per-session Forgejo tokens directly.
- execd's PTY-over-WebSocket holder/viewer sharing model plus SSE command streaming: a concrete mechanism for the real-time 'watch the agent work' console view we do not have.
- Pool CRD's two-level buffer/total warm-capacity model with Delete/Restart/Noop recycle strategies, and a 'fresh identity per allocation' guard (503 on all business endpoints until a recycled pod is atomically rebound) that closes the obvious warm-pool tenant-bleed risk.

### Temps (3)
- {"The non-root, capability-free Node.js egress-proxy sidecar embedded as a single Rust string constant (SANDBOX_EGRESS_PROXY_SCRIPT in crates/temps-agents/src/sandbox/docker.rs) that is a sandboxed agent's only route out": 'blocks private/link-local/cloud-metadata IPs including 169.254.0.0/16, resolves DNS once and connects to the resolved IP to defeat rebinding, and allowlists ports 80/443 only.'}
- {'Phase-gating enforced in the service layer, not the UI': 'run_fix_inner checks run.phase == "analyzed" and create_pr checks run.phase == "fix_ready", each returning AgentError::Validation otherwise, so an approval cannot be skipped by calling the API out of order.'}
- For non-continuable CLI providers (Codex, OpenCode), the prior analysis is re-injected into the fix prompt explicitly marked as untrusted input, a deliberate prompt-injection defence at a phase boundary that Claude's own --continue session carries implicitly.
- refuse_granted_project_push, a defense-in-depth guard re-checked at every git-push call site even when an earlier check already ran, justified in a code comment as "the failure mode this guard family has hit four times already" -- an incident-driven pattern worth copying literally.
- Shipping the product's own control surface as 440+ CLI operations mirrored into Claude Code/Codex/OpenCode-compatible "skills" plus an MCP server, so an agent drives the platform through the same interface a human's CLI would, instead of a bespoke tool schema bolted on afterwards.

### Google Agent Development Kit (2)
- {'BasePlugin (src/google/adk/plugins/base_plugin.py)': 'before/after callbacks on user message, agent, model and tool calls, executed in registration order with short-circuit on a returned value. A clean interception pattern for hooks even though our own enforcement has to live outside the agent process.'}
- {'ToolConfirmation (src/google/adk/tools/tool_confirmation.py) and the request_confirmation flow': 'a single tool call can pause mid-turn for a human decision with a hint and a JSON payload, then resume. Worth stealing as a UX shape for approving one action, distinct from approving a whole task.'}
- One BaseSessionService interface with five interchangeable backends (in-memory, SQLite, SQLAlchemy database, Vertex AI managed, Firestore/Redis via integrations). Good reference for keeping our own session/finding store swappable.
- {'LLMRegistry (src/google/adk/models/registry.py)': 'native classes for Gemini, Anthropic, Gemma and Apigee, and one LiteLlm wrapper that defers to litellm.provider_list for everything else instead of hand-listing providers. Cheap to copy for our own model-tier ladder.'}
- Pub/Sub and Eventarc trigger endpoints (src/google/adk/cli/trigger_routes.py) bake a concurrency semaphore and exponential backoff on 429/RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED into the intake path itself, so a burst of events cannot blow the model quota. A concrete pattern for our own signal intake.
- container_code_executor.py runs a supervisor as a separate forked process to enforce the timeout, so sandboxed code cannot disarm its own deadline and SIGKILL reaches the whole process group. Good detail for our own gVisor time bounds.

### GitHub Agentic Workflows (gh-aw) (2)
- Typed side-effect requests ("safe outputs") with a per-type max, validated and applied by a separate privileged step after the agent is gone, for every write that is not the PR itself
- Default protected-files list (package manifests, lockfiles, agent instruction files, CODEOWNERS, .github/, any top-level dot directory) with fallback-to-issue or request-review policies
- Content sanitiser applied to both agent input and agent output - @mention and closing-keyword neutralisation, URL redaction to the allowed domain list, tag defanging, size caps
- Integrity filtering in the tool gateway - drop tool results authored by accounts below a trust level (merged, approved, unapproved) instead of only marking them untrusted
- Budget steering - the model proxy injects warnings as the run nears its credit cap, plus cheap extra guards (max tool denials, max cache misses, model allowlist per alias)
- Exact-value secret redaction over every artifact and log before upload, run unconditionally
- Staged mode and replay - produce the full side-effect plan without applying it, then replay the recorded plan later
- Run-scoped steering issue whose comments from write-access users the agent reads mid-run

### CodeRabbit (2)
- Pre-merge checks live in .coderabbit.yaml with three modes (off, warning, error) tied to a request-changes workflow that blocks merge through the forge's own required-review rule, with a logged single-PR override rather than a silent bypass
- @coderabbitai evaluate custom pre-merge check --name ... --instructions ... dry-runs a natural-language policy rule against the live PR before it goes into config, a cheap way to test a gate prompt before it goes live
- Built-in deterministic checks (docstring coverage threshold, PR title and description shape, linked-issue and scope-creep checks) run before the expensive full LLM review, matching our own cheap-and-deterministic-first ordering
- A per-plan reviews-per-hour rate limit plus a 0.25 USD per file overage charge with a user-set spending cap, a simple ceiling worth mirroring for any pay-per-artifact step in our own pipeline
- IDE and CLI review clients pair directly with the coding agent CLI that wrote the change (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor CLI) so a check runs before a PR even opens, worth an equivalent pre-push hook in our own harness loop
- Per-repo learnings persist reviewer feedback and get replayed into later reviews, a small step toward the project memory we admit we do not have

### claude-mem and Letta Code (agent memory for coding agents) (2)
- Git-backed MemFS as the persistent memory store. A real git repo under $MEMORY_DIR, a required MEMORY.md index, size and depth limits from .memfs.config.json (Letta Code). Project memory as a git repo we clone and reseed like any other.
- The paired pre-commit and post-commit git hooks that validate memory markdown frontmatter and protect a read_only field from agent edits (src/agent/memory-git-hooks.ts, Letta Code). The right place to attach a stronger trust rule of our own.
- Kernel-level sandboxing for cross-agent memory writes using bwrap on Linux and seatbelt on macOS (src/sandbox/bwrap.ts, Letta Code). A known primitive worth a look for our own worker sandbox alongside gVisor.
- The three-layer MCP retrieval pattern, search then timeline then get_observations (claude-mem). Cheap coarse search first, full detail only on demand, for any memory or codebase-index tool we build.
- The never store secrets, reference dollar SECRET_NAME rule written straight into the agent's own system prompt (src/agent/prompts/letta_local_memfs.md, Letta Code). Cheap to copy verbatim into any memory-writing prompt of ours.
- Content-hash deduplication of new memory entries, a SHA256 of session, title and narrative inside a 30 second window (claude-mem). A one-line guard against duplicate or noisy memory growth.

### Cline (2)
- {'Hub-spoke session architecture': 'a singleton local daemon (the hub) owns session state and event routing, spoke worker processes run the agent loop, and any number of clients (CLI, IDE, Telegram) attach over WebSocket and can disconnect and reattach mid-task without killing the run.'}
- Kanban's ephemeral git worktree per task, with gitignored directories like node_modules symlinked in from the main checkout, so parallel tasks never fight over install time or files, only a real container boundary is missing.
- {'Checkpoint-scoped diffs': 'the review view can show the diff for one message range instead of only the full cumulative diff, and a click on a diff line sends an inline comment back to the agent as steering feedback.'}
- Subagents are a hard-permissioned read-only role (read_file, list_files, search_files, list_code_definition_names, read-only execute_command, use_skill only, no writes, no browser, no MCP, no nested subagents) with cost tracked per subagent and rolled into the parent task total.
- A resume ID survives after a task card is trashed, so a finished or abandoned session can be picked up again later.
- The OpenTelemetry event vocabulary (agent_created, tool_usage, model_api_call, session_ended) is a reasonable starting taxonomy for our own SigNoz task events.

### Gemini CLI (2)
- TOML policy schema (toolName, commandPrefix, commandRegex, argsPattern, decision, priority, modes) is a compact readable shape for tool-level overrides, and its "deny hides the tool from the model entirely" behaviour saves context and stops probing, worth copying over our current allow/refuse-only gateway behaviour.
- Shadow-git checkpoint before every write tool call, restored with a restore command, is a cheap last-good-state safety net independent of the agent's own branch, worth exposing as a per-task quick-revert in the console.
- The headless JSONL event schema (init, message, tool_use, tool_result, error, result) with a small closed set of exit codes, including one for "turn limit exceeded", is a good reference shape for our Harness Event stream and Result type.
- Sandbox expansion (a scoped, one-time permission grant on denial, remembered per approval-mode hierarchy plan < default < autoEdit < yolo) is a better mediated-consent pattern than an on/off sandbox switch, and maps onto an approval-card style exception request.
- MCP tool rules addressed by server name rather than fragile fully-qualified-name wildcards is a naming lesson for our own gateway's MCP proxy rules.
- The official GitHub Action wrapper (dispatch, issue triage, pull request review workflows) shows how far a bare harness plus a thin CI-triggered wrapper covers the signals and verification planes without a dedicated control plane, useful to revisit for a future read-only analyzer capability class.

### Goose (Block) (2)
- GooseMode: a four-value autonomy dial (auto, approve, smart_approve, chat) switchable mid-session with /mode, cheaper than a kanban state for how much rope a task gets.
- goose run --output-format stream-json plus an Agent Client Protocol server (goose acp on stdio, goose serve over HTTP/WebSocket) as a structured, non-text-scraped interface a harness wrapper can consume.
- Recipes are size-capped and format-validated before they are allowed onto the cron scheduler (MAX_SCHEDULE_RECIPE_BYTES, validate_recipe_for_scheduling), a cheap guard against a hostile TaskBrief-equivalent.
- The local permission store hashes tool name plus argument context, supports an optional expiry per remembered decision, and writes atomically (temp file plus rename), a small durable pattern worth copying wherever we cache a human approval.
- security/egress_inspector.rs regex-parses shell commands for outbound destinations as an advisory check layered on top of, not instead of, real network policy.

### OpenAI Codex (2)
- execpolicy: a small Starlark-like DSL (codex-rs/execpolicy) with prefix_rule(pattern, decision, match, not_match) that classifies a shell command as allow, forbidden or prompt purely from argv, outside the model. Worth copying the shape for our own command-level policy layered on top of network policy.
- AGENTS.md resolution algorithm: walk from repo root down to cwd, prefer an AGENTS.override.md over AGENTS.md per directory, concatenate root-to-leaf so nearer files win by position, cap the total at project_doc_max_bytes (32 KiB default). A clean, small precedence rule we can lift as-is.
- The codex exec --json event schema (thread.started, turn.started, turn.completed with token usage, item.started/updated/completed, error) is a compact, well-typed shape for a harness event stream, close to what our normalised event stream needs from any Harness implementation.
- features.network_proxy: allowlist-first egress with deny-beats-allow, local/private destinations blocked unless explicit, and DNS-rebinding checks on top of the allowlist. Directly reusable as a checklist for our own default-deny egress gateway.
- The GitHub Action's two-job pattern: the job that runs codex exec never gets pull-requests: write and the job that posts the comment never sees the API key. That is our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle already applied to a CI trigger, worth copying verbatim for our own Forgejo Actions gates that call out to an agent.
- codex review's convention of a Code Review Rules section placed in the AGENTS.md nearest the changed code, with GitHub-side output capped to P0/P1 only. A cheap way to keep an automated review's noise bounded that we could apply to our own stronger-model review verdict.
- LiteLLM (our model gateway) already exposes a native /v1/responses endpoint, the only wire format Codex CLI still speaks. That removes the main technical risk of adopting Codex as our named successor harness: no bespoke translation shim needed between factory-gateway/LiteLLM and Codex's model_providers config.

### OpenCode (2)
- opencode run --format json plus --attach <url> against a warm opencode serve instance: a documented JSON event stream over a long-lived process, instead of parsing interactive TUI output or paying a cold-start cost per task
- The plugin hook surface (tool.execute.before/after, shell.env for env injection, experimental.session.compacting to replace the compaction prompt) as a place to bolt our own audit or double-check logic onto the harness without forking it
- The policies-vs-permissions split: a separate experimental.policies layer (glob-matched, last-rule-wins, resource-scoped, currently gating provider.use) sitting next to the per-tool permission engine, a clean way to keep 'what can this task use' apart from 'what can this task do'
- {env:VAR} and {file:path} substitution in config files, a one-line way to keep secrets out of committed config without building a secrets manager integration
- opencode stats: a built-in CLI command that breaks down token usage and cost by session, tool, model and project, worth copying as a debugging and cost-visibility surface even though it enforces nothing
- The Go proxy's published compatibility table of third-party clients that do or do not send a stable routing header, an honest way to document cross-harness interoperability that we could copy for our own model-tier documentation

### SWE-agent and mini-swe-agent (2)
- litellm is already the model layer (sweagent/agent/models.py imports litellm directly), so pointing SWE-agent or mini-swe-agent at factory-gateway is a base_url and virtual-key change, not an integration project. Confirms LiteLLM as the common substrate across this whole market, not just our choice.
- The reviewer/best-of-n retry loop (sweagent/agent/reviewer.py, sweagent/agent/action_sampler.py) runs several attempts and can score them with a separate, optionally stronger, reviewer model. A smaller, concrete version of our supervisor "retry on a stronger model" idea, worth reading before building ours.
- mini-swe-agent's execution model, every action is an independent subprocess.run call instead of a persistent shell session, makes the sandbox trivially swappable (docker exec, singularity, bubblewrap, local) per its README. That is a cleaner shape for a Harness implementation than a long-lived shell, and closer to how a gVisor pod exec would want to be driven.
- The open_pr hook (sweagent/run/hooks/open_pr.py) has the agent create its own branch, commit and PR against the source issue with commands run inside the sandbox. A small, self-contained example of "artifacts not chat" done entirely with plain version-control primitives.
- Per-instance and total cost limits (agent.model.per_instance_cost_limit, total_cost_limit in sweagent/agent/models.py) are computed from litellm's own per-call cost accounting, giving a ready-made table of per-model prices to crib from instead of maintaining our own.

### Amazon Bedrock AgentCore (2)
- {'Temporal (session-aware) Cedar/Dogwood policy conditions': 'rules that read the history of a session, not just the current call, such as "require an approval before a transfer" or "cap a running total". Our capability policy is stateless per call, this is a real gap.'}
- Natural-language policy authoring checked by automated reasoning before it takes effect, catching rules that are always-true, always-false or over-broad. A cheap lint pass we could add to our own YAML capability policy in Git.
- The explicit warning that a policy gateway only protects you if the protected resource refuses direct calls. Worth an audit of whether every path to our worker sandboxes and tool targets actually forces traffic through factory-gateway, with nothing reachable around it.
- The inbound auth split between IAM/SigV4 (service-to-service) and JWT bearer (end user through an IdP), with an explicit warning to deny the "trust an opaque user id header" path in production. A clean model for any endpoint we expose to more than one kind of caller.
- Concrete input-validation guidance against structuring an attacker's text as a toolUse content block so it dispatches a tool without model reasoning. Directly applicable to how we pass untrusted issue and PR-comment text into the harness.
- Persistent session filesystem across stop and resume with no external storage step. Relevant if we ever want cheaper long-running or paused agent sessions than a full re-clone per resume.
- Semantic search over a large tool catalogue at the gateway, so an agent's prompt lists a handful of relevant tools instead of every registered one. Worth revisiting once our MCP tool surface grows past a handful of targets.

### Vertex AI Agent Engine (2)
- Sessions (short-term conversation state) split from Memory Bank (long-term memories generated from events by a model). Our design admits "no project memory, no codebase index" as a known gap; a memory bank fed by task findings across sessions is a concrete shape to copy.
- Idle time is not billed, the compute clock stops between agent turns and usage rounds to the second. A cleaner metering unit than wall-clock time for our own gateway holds, which should track active sandbox work, not pod lifetime.
- Sandbox templates, snapshots and bring-your-own-container images for warm reuse of a prepared execution environment. Relevant to our own pain point, gVisor cold start and the 38-second throwaway build VM path.
- Evaluation Service plus Example Store as a standing offline-eval, simulation and online-monitor loop, feeding a "Feedback service" alongside telemetry. We admit no benchmark of agent success rate per model tier exists yet; this is a concrete shape for one.
- Agent Gateway as a named, separate hop that routes all agent egress to MCP servers and VPC networks under one governed point. Confirms the factory-gateway pattern is converging industry practice, not a one-off choice.

### Microsoft Foundry Agent Service, Agent Framework, Agent 365 (2)
- FIDES (docs/decisions/0024-prompt-injection-defense.md, python/packages/core/agent_framework/security.py): deterministic label-based IFC middleware, IntegrityLabel TRUSTED/UNTRUSTED and ConfidentialityLabel PUBLIC/PRIVATE/USER_IDENTITY, most-restrictive-wins composition, quarantined_llm for isolated processing of untrusted tool output. Directly applicable to how we mark issue and PR comment text as untrusted.
- Toolbox pattern (Foundry docs, concepts/toolbox-overview): curate MCP tools once, expose behind one managed, versioned, centrally authenticated endpoint consumed by every agent. Close to what factory-gateway should be for MCP tool calls instead of per-task ad hoc wiring.
- On-Behalf-Of token passthrough from a per-agent Entra identity to remote MCP servers, so a tool call carries the calling user's own scoped grant rather than a shared service credential. Worth comparing against our per-task ServiceAccount-to-gateway mapping.
- Built-in agent versioning with automatic snapshotting and one-click rollback for prompt agent definitions. A cheap default our console lacks for TaskBrief or policy edits.
- Lightweight ADR log (docs/decisions, adr-short-template.md) for every non-trivial design change, including ones that get superseded a month later. Cheap discipline, easy to copy for our own ledger.
- MCP ToolAnnotations hint mapping (readOnlyHint, openWorldHint) to auto-label tool trust and a server _meta.ifc metadata convention for per-result security labels, an emerging convention worth watching if we grow our own MCP surface.

### Ona (2)
- Veto: a kernel LSM that identifies executables by content hash, not path, and checks plus blocks within the same syscall so there is no time-of-check-to-time-of-use gap. Worth an equivalent exec-gate inside our gVisor sandbox as a second layer under network policy, and worth testing our own sandbox against the exact bypass Ona disclosed against itself (invoking the ELF dynamic linker directly so the load goes through mmap, not execve)
- OIDC to cloud-credential federation: the environment mints a JWT, AWS STS exchanges it via AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity for a short-lived role session, and trust policies match a colon-delimited sub claim (organization_id:...:project_id:...) with StringLike. The sub-claim scoping pattern is a concrete, reusable idea for scoping our own gateway-issued or MCP tokens to a specific project or resource path
- A command and executable deny list as a second, independent policy layer next to network policy, enforced immediately for new actions and logged to audit, cheap to add and catches a class of accidents (agent runs a disallowed binary) that network policy alone misses
- Bootstrapping a new repository by asking the agent itself to generate the sandbox config: Ona's quickstart has the agent produce a devcontainer.json and .ona/config.yaml tailored to the repo. Applicable as a first-run self-describe task for repositories our worker has not seen before
- Warm pools as a first-class resource in the Ona Terraform provider, pre-provisioned runner capacity to cut environment start latency. Worth a small pre-warmed pod pool per project for latency-sensitive tasks instead of always cold-starting a namespace and pod
- AGENTS.md plus a checked-in skills directory as portable, git-committed, per-repository agent tuning, explicitly kept compatible with the same file from Claude Code and Cursor. A near-free start on our own project-memory gap
- One-keystroke parallel launch (Cmd+Enter) from a single list view, each task its own environment. A cheap affordance to add to our kanban board

### OpenAI Frontier (2)
- The layered pitch of business context, agent execution, evaluation, and governance as four named, separate concerns is a clean way to explain a factory's shape to a non-technical stakeholder. Worth reusing the four-word framing in our own portfolio narrative even though our actual mechanisms differ.
- Frontier explicitly governs agents from other model vendors (Google, Microsoft, Anthropic) alongside its own, positioned as a control-plane feature rather than a concession. It is a reminder to keep pushing multi-vendor routing through LiteLLM as a selling point, not just a cost hedge.
- OpenAI's Responses API added async tool calling and mid-turn steering over WebSockets on 2026-09-03 (developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/steering): a client can inject a response.steer event referencing the in-progress previous_response_id without losing prior work. If our harness interface ever needs a live-inject path for a supervisor's 'redirect' verdict instead of killing and restarting the session, this is a concrete mechanism to study, though it is a model-API feature, not something we saw Frontier itself expose.
- The same Responses API update lets a caller raise or lower reasoning effort mid-conversation with a configuration_update item, instead of restarting on a different model. Our own retry-on-a-stronger-model ladder could take a cheaper first step: bump effort on the same model before promoting to coding-frontier.
- Frontier's agent identities live inside the customer's existing enterprise IAM rather than a separate agent-only system. Confirms a default worth keeping: task identity should extend an org's existing identity plane (for us, the cluster OIDC issuer and Forgejo accounts), not invent a parallel one.

### Atlassian Rovo Dev (2)
- {'Split-plane resilience': 'the user-facing conversation lives in a always-up control plane, the compute sandbox is disposable. A sandbox crash gets a fresh container and a retry with no visible break, instead of killing the session.'}
- {'A callback bridge so the sandbox never gets direct external network access at all. Every tool call, including calls to remote MCP servers, is proxied back through the control plane, which does the permission and auth check before any external API is touched. Stronger than an egress allowlist': 'there is no egress path from inside the sandbox.'}
- Three-tier tool approval (once, this session, always) with compound shell commands split and checked subcommand by subcommand, saved to a config file. A concrete default for how our console should ask a human to approve a tool call.
- Milestone checkpointing so a failure late in a run resumes without repeating side effects such as duplicate artifacts, instead of restarting the whole task.
- {'A named self-evolution loop': 'failed or inefficient runs are flagged from telemetry, turned into eval cases, and only promoted to the harness after a human engineer reviews and approves the change. Directly answers our own no-benchmark-of-agent-success-rate gap.'}
- Runtime, per-user tool manifests (which of thousands of API actions are enabled) generated at session start rather than baked into a static policy file, worth comparing against our YAML-in-Git capability policy for cases where policy depends on live user state.

### Augment Code Intent (2)
- Three checkpoints in one linear flow, approve the spec, approve the task breakdown, review the diffs, gate every stage of a run behind a single human click. Our console's human lane could adopt the same shape for the approval states we already model as workflow state.
- The living spec is a shared artifact that every agent in a Space reads and writes, so a mid-run edit by the human propagates to all running implementors at once. Worth copying as a pattern for TaskBrief revisions mid-session, which our design does not currently support.
- A dedicated Verifier agent re-checks implementor output against the spec before the human sees a diff, on top of background agents that run tests, lint and type checks during the run itself. A cheap first-pass gate worth adding ahead of our own CI stage, not instead of it.
- Auto-generated PR descriptions that keep updating themselves as later commits land in the same Space. A small, concrete default for our bot-authored PRs.
- Per-tool-call credit accounting shown back to the user, task size in credits, model choice in credits, is a transparency pattern worth copying for our own cost view, independent of whether we keep their pooled-allowance billing model.
- Bring-your-own-agent support for three external harnesses, Claude Code, Codex and OpenCode, through a Context Engine MCP server rather than a bespoke integration per harness, is a clean way to keep a product's own agent optional.

### Blitzy (2)
- A persistent, always-current knowledge graph of the whole codebase, rebuilt on every merge, given to every agent as shared context. Directly answers our own listed weakness of having no project memory or codebase index.
- PR Review as a rationale-plus-evidence summary (what changed, why, what validated it) instead of a raw diff, aimed at PRs too large to review line by line. A concrete pattern for our console's missing diff view.
- A .blitzyignore file, gitignore syntax, to scope what an agent run may touch. Trivial to add to our worker plane as a repo-level exclusion list on top of policy.
- {'A hard two-phase approval gate': 'approve a generated plan of action (tech spec) before any code is written, then approve the PR after. Matches our "approval as workflow state" principle better than most competitors we reviewed.'}
- Secrets for build and runtime are entered through an encrypted UI and are "never exposed to AI agents" (per their FAQ, not verified in code). The intent, injecting credentials at the build step rather than into agent context, matches our identity-per-task principle even though we cannot check the implementation.
- A non-cancellable job that consumes a fixed, pre-agreed quota of lines. A blunt but simple hard-cap pattern for a single expensive multi-day run, worth considering alongside our budget holds.

### Charlie Labs (2)
- Daemon spec: a role definition as a committed Markdown file (YAML frontmatter for name, watch triggers, routines, deny rules, cron schedule; prose body for policy and escalation), reviewed and versioned like code
- "Roles, not tasks" framing for standing maintenance work, distinct from one-shot coding tasks, as a model for a future capability class beyond our single coder class
- Durable turn-based kernel primitives named explicitly: single-winner task claiming, a mailbox for mid-run follow-ups, and cancellation checked every turn
- Two-identity split on the forge: a human-facing bot account (CharlieHelps, MFA'd, for mentions and reviews) kept separate from the machine-facing GitHub App (CharlieCreates, repo-scoped token) that actually holds access
- Onboarding default: the GitHub App is installed per repository with explicit "start narrow and add more repositories later" guidance, not org-wide by default
- Pooled, team-based credit billing so agent maintenance work does not eat an individual's personal budget

### Continue (2)
- Three-tier tool permission model (allow, ask, exclude) with glob argument matching per call, e.g. Write(**/*.ts), stored in ~/.continue/permissions.yaml and overridable by CLI flags or a mode (extensions/cli/src/permissions, ToolPermissionService.ts) - more granular than a single capability class
- Headless-mode default: any tool at ask is silently excluded rather than blocking, so an unattended run degrades safely with no human to approve (docs/cli/headless-mode.mdx) - worth checking our worker does the same when a step needs approval it cannot get
- Org secrets are referenced as a mustache-style secrets.NAME token and resolved server-side; model calls are proxied through api.continue.dev so the raw key never reaches the IDE or CLI process - same shape as our gateway-holds-the-key principle, a useful external validation of the pattern
- Per-run Session record (summary, diff, logs) plus a personal Inbox of PRs awaiting review is a concrete, buildable UX target for our console's missing diff view
- Intervention Rate, the percentage of agent-generated output that needed a human edit before merge, is a simple outcome metric we do not track yet and could add cheaply to our audit trail
- Trigger breadth in one abstraction: cron, webhook, GitHub issue, Sentry alert and Slack message all start the same kind of agent run, a wider signals surface than our Forgejo-plus-SigNoz-only intake
- PR review runs the agent inside a forked process against a git worktree copy of the repo (extensions/cli/src/commands/review/reviewWorker.ts) - a cheap, dependency-free way to isolate a review pass without a full sandbox, useful for low-stakes internal tooling

### Cyrus (2)
- Typed, incremental activity stream posted into the issue during the run (thought/action/response/error), not just a final comment. Even without Linear's native agent-session API, we can emulate the pattern against a Forgejo issue with edited/appended comments keyed by session id.
- {"Label-driven tool and prompt presets (Cyrus's labelPrompts)": 'a label on the issue selects a system prompt and an allowed-tool list (readOnly/safe/all/custom). Cheap, config-only policy knob worth copying for Forgejo labels.'}
- {'Routing priority chain for multi-repo setups (teamKeys > projectKeys > routingLabels, most specific wins)': 'a clean, declarative pattern for mapping an incoming issue to a workflow or model tier.'}
- One-question-at-a-time inline approval ("select signal") surfaced directly in the issue thread as a dropdown, instead of a separate approvals UI. Worth trying for our console's human lane.
- userAccessControl allow/blocklist of which requesters may delegate to the agent, with a configurable silent-vs-comment block. A cheap trust-boundary knob at the comment layer, complementary to our "only write-access steers a task" rule.
- Per-PR FIFO event queue that serialises same-PR comment webhooks instead of letting concurrent sessions race on one PR. Simple, worth having wherever we allow follow-up comments on a task already in flight.

### Gas Town and Beads (2)
- Optimistic-concurrency claim column (row_lock, surfaced as Issue.RowVersion) with a single conditional UPDATE for atomic, idempotent work claiming, a clean pattern for any queue with competing workers
- Schema-version guard that refuses to open a database migrated by a newer binary, prints the exact rebuild or install command in the error, and offers a named, deliberate escape hatch (BD_IGNORE_SCHEMA_SKEW=1)
- Deterministic content-hash IDs with progressive length and a nonce (6 to 8 base36 characters) so independent agents or branches mint non-colliding issue IDs without a central counter or coordination before merge
- The "murder spree lesson" supervision split, cheap mechanical detectors (the daemon, 3-minute heartbeat) are only allowed to notify, never to kill, restart or nuke, that judgment is reserved for an AI supervisor (the witness). This corroborates a cheap-detector-then-model-judgment design rather than adding a new idea
- A merge-slot bead as a single-row exclusive lock for serialising pushes to one branch, cheap to build, easy to reason about
- LLM-based compaction of old closed issues (Claude Haiku, tier 1 only, 30 and 90 day thresholds) that shrinks stored text while keeping a snapshot for restore, a direct answer to bounded long-horizon memory
- A lightweight cost ledger fed by an agent-CLI stop hook (gt costs record) rolling into a daily digest bead, worth copying as a human-readable spend summary even where hard caps already exist upstream

### Jules (2)
- Environment snapshot: 'Run and Snapshot' caches a validated per-repo setup (deps installed, tests green) and reuses it for every later task on that repo, cutting cold start time
- Planning Critic (jules.google/docs/changelog/2026-01-26-1): a second cheap model reviews the plan itself before execution, targeted at the auto-approved path, reported as a 9.5 percent cut in task failure
- Semaphore-backed concurrency limiter with exponential backoff on 429/500/502/503/504 in jules-sdk/packages/core/src/api.ts, a one-file pattern for any client fanning out to a rate-limited API
- SessionSnapshot.toJSON({include, exclude}) field masking (jules-sdk/docs/snapshot.md) for trimming what a session dump exposes to a client or log
- TUI dashboard (running 'jules' with no arguments) gives a side-by-side diff viewer and session list from the terminal, matching the web app
- Suggested Tasks: proactive TODO scanning that becomes a one-click-start task card, worth copying for turning static analysis findings into pre-drafted, human-approved cards
- jules-merge's scan, detect overlap, stage, push workflow for reconciling several parallel agents' diffs on the same repository
- MCP server (jules-sdk/packages/mcp) exposes session lifecycle (create_session, send_reply, get_code_review_context) as tools, a template for exposing our own task lifecycle to any MCP client

### Kilo Code and Kilo Cloud (2)
- The GitHub Action mode (packages/opencode/src/cli/cmd/github.handler.ts) fetches a workflow OIDC token (core.getIDToken) and exchanges it server-side for a short-lived, repo-scoped GitHub App token, instead of storing a PAT in secrets. It also checks the commenting actor's repo permission (must be admin or write) before doing anything.
