Both match our own short-lived-credential and write-access-only trust rules, and are worth citing as independent validation of the pattern
- Generic webhook and cron triggers (app.kilo.ai/cloud/triggers) turn any HTTP event into a Cloud Agent session against a repo, with a prompt template that interpolates {{body}}, {{bodyJson}}, {{headers}}, {{query}}, plus hard caps (256 KB payload, 20 in-flight requests, no binary content). A source-agnostic trigger with a templated prompt and small hard caps is a cheaper way to add a new signal source than a bespoke connector per system
- Forcing 'question' and 'suggest' tool permissions to deny for unattended runs (session.create permission list in github.handler.ts) stops the agent from stalling on a human who is not there. Any of our capability classes that can run with no interactive channel should disable in-band 'ask' tools the same way, rather than let a supervisor call cover for a missing UI
- kilo-console embeds a real terminal (ghostty-web) for watching a Cloud Agent or remote CLI session live in the browser, and the CLI has a --stream JSONL mode for the same session. This is a direct, low-effort answer to a weakness we already listed for ourselves (no real-time view of an agent's work): a terminal-replay panel is smaller to build than a full trace or diff UI
- kilo-sandbox's config shape (sandbox.enabled, network: allow|deny, allowed_hosts as exact host:port, writable_paths) is a compact schema for default-deny-with-allowlist network policy at process level, built on bubblewrap (Linux) and seatbelt (macOS) plus the seccomp profile from @anthropic-ai/sandbox-runtime. Useful as a reference if we ever need a lightweight non-Kubernetes sandbox mode, e.g. for local development of the harness itself
- Kilo Gateway's pricing is 'exact provider rate, no markup' plus a flat 5 percent fee on credit top-ups, rather than a per-call markup. A single, auditable fee point is simpler to reason about than a tiered markup table

### Attractor (StrongDM Software Factory) (2)
- DOT/Graphviz as the pipeline definition format, version controllable, diffable in a pull request, and free SVG visualisation with standard tooling. Our Temporal workflow has no equivalent diagram.
- The per-stage status.json/prompt.md/response.md contract (Section 4.5, Appendix C) is a dead simple audit trail any tool can read or write. Worth mirroring in the worker's log layout for cheap forensics without touching SigNoz.
- The five-step deterministic edge selection algorithm (condition match, preferred label, suggested next ids, weight, lexical tie break) is a clean, inspectable routing order we could reuse for the supervisor's continue/redirect/retry/escalate decision.
- Context fidelity modes (full, truncate, compact, summary:low/medium/high) with explicit precedence (edge over node over graph over default) name a lever for context window cost we currently handle ad hoc.
- The pluggable Interviewer pattern (AutoApprove, Console, Callback, Queue, Recording) lets a human gate be swapped for a deterministic stub in tests. Useful for testing our own approval workflow without a human in CI.
- {'The scenario-as-holdout principle': "keep the exact input a step will be graded against out of that step's own read access. Worth auditing what our reproduction gate and model review can currently see versus what they are scored on."}

### Sweep (2)
- {'The issue-native status view': 'on_ticket.py edits a single comment on the triggering issue into a live checklist with a progress link, instead of a separate dashboard. Worth copying for small tasks where opening the console is overhead.'}
- sweep.yaml per-repo policy file (blocked_dirs, rules, gha_enabled, draft) that repo owners commit themselves. A minimal, self-service version of "policy as a file in Git" scoped per project rather than factory-wide.
- on_failing_github_actions.py re-triggers a fix from a failing CI run's own logs, closing the loop from verification failure back to a new attempt without a human filing a new issue. Matches our "reproduction gate" idea but done as a webhook, not a workflow step.
- GitHub comment buttons ("Restart Sweep", "Rollback Files For Sweep") give one-click abort/undo entirely inside the PR thread, no custom UI needed for those two actions.
- DOCKERFILE_CONFIG_LOCATION optionally replays a repo's own CI Dockerfile in a throwaway container to reproduce a failure before proposing a fix, a cheap way to get a real repro signal without owning the CI system.

### Terragon (2)
- packages/sandbox/src/provider.ts switches between four sandbox backends (E2B, Daytona, Docker for dev, Mock for tests) behind one ISandboxProvider interface, a clean working precedent for a swappable sandbox backend if gVisor's build slowness ever forces a second provider.
- packages/daemon/src/{claude,codex,gemini,amp,opencode}.ts are five real per-agent adapters behind one IDaemonRuntime, a working multi-harness abstraction at a larger scale than our single opencode implementation, worth reading before building the Codex successor path.
- A per-sandbox DAEMON_TOKEN bearer plus server-side proxy routes (apps/www/src/app/api/proxy/{anthropic,openai,google,openrouter}) inject the real model API key only on the control-plane side, so the sandbox never holds a usable provider credential, the same shape as our gateway's virtual key and independent validation of the pattern.
- BYO subscription (packages/shared/src/db/schema.ts's agentProviderCredentials table, encrypted OAuth token or API key per user) lets an operator's own Claude Max or ChatGPT Plus quota power a session instead of metered API calls, worth offering alongside our LiteLLM ladder for cost-conscious solo use.
- A pre-flight balance check (balanceCents <= 0 blocks the call, apps/www/src/app/api/proxy/anthropic route.ts) plus auto-reload on low balance is a minimal working shape for a hard spend floor, simpler than our priced hold and worth a look as a cheap fast path.
- Live progress over a small dedicated WebSocket service (apps/broadcast, built on PartyKit) plus streamed git checkpoints is a proven, low-effort architecture for exactly the live-stream and diff-view gap our own console admits to.
- terry, the CLI, both hands a running task to a local terminal and runs an MCP server so a task can be created or steered by natural language from inside Cursor or Claude Code, a light pattern for driving factoryd intake from inside a coding session rather than only from Forgejo.

### Warp Oz (2)
- Parent and child runs communicate over a durable, server-backed message bus with a per-agent inbox and a global sequence number, so a child's result is guaranteed to arrive before its SUCCEEDED state is observed. A concrete pattern for ordering our own supervisor and worker events.
- Orchestration is offered as named, documented patterns (supervisor/worker, fan-out/fan-in, critic/verifier, review-swarm, DAG) behind two API modes, an orchestrate mode and a plan mode, rather than left to prompt engineering. Worth copying as vocabulary and an API shape once we add non-coder capability classes.
- A required approval step before any child agent launches, where the parent proposes child count, prompts, environments and parallelism, and a human approves or edits the plan first. A lightweight version of approval-as-workflow-state for multi-agent fan-out.
- Environments as the reusable unit of task setup (a Docker image, one or more git repos, a startup command), created in under five minutes and shared across a team. A good default shape for cutting our own per-task cold start.
- Session Sharing links as the audit primitive. Every run gets one link that shows a live, steerable transcript and can be listed by who started it. Directly addresses our stated gap of no real-time stream and no diff view.
- Kubernetes self-hosting keeps the orchestration daemon's ServiceAccount separate from the per-task Job's serviceAccountName, so the worker and the task it runs carry different identities. Worth checking our own RBAC against this split.
- Cost is shown as one spend unit ("credits") across both AI and compute usage, with a team-wide spend cap and per-seat pooling, which is a more legible framing than raw dollars-per-token for a cost view.

### Linear for Agents (2)
- Six-state AgentSession lifecycle (pending, active, error, awaitingInput, complete, stale) derived automatically from the latest emitted activity, no separate state bookkeeping
- Typed AgentActivity vocabulary (thought, action, elicitation, response, error) with an ephemeral flag on thought and action so noisy steps collapse and get replaced by the next entry
- A delegate relation kept distinct from assignee, so agent acts on behalf of and human still owns this are two separate, visible facts
- Fixed unresponsiveness clock (ack within 10s, stale after 30 minutes of silence, recoverable by one more activity) as a concrete number to steal for our own supervisor stall detector
- externalUrls array on the session for publishing the eventual pull request link, separate from the comment thread

### Agyn (2)
- Egress Gateway rule shape (domain or private-resource-id, path glob, method, port, deny beats allow) with header injection resolved from a secret store at request time is a clean model for our own egress policy documents
- Default-to-public-trust TLS verification when a rule sets no upstream_tls (system roots against the dialed hostname) fails loud instead of silently trusting an internal certificate, worth copying verbatim
- Run Timeline's three event types (LLM call with full context, tool execution with streamed stdout and stderr, summarization event) plus a Terminate button is a concrete, minimal spec for the trace UI we admit we lack
- Kata plus Firecracker as an opt-in RuntimeClassName that only schedules onto prepared nodes is a safe partial-rollout pattern for mixing isolation tiers in one fleet
- Agent definitions, secrets, MCP servers and volumes exposed as Terraform resources (agyn_agent, agyn_environment, agyn_llm_model) is a real agents-as-code surface worth matching for the parts of our policy admins hand-edit today
- Each MCP tool as its own sidecar container, not just a gateway policy check, gives a hard process and network boundary per tool worth considering for our own MCP calls

### kagent (2)
- Harness as a Kubernetes CRD (Agent, Harness, ModelConfig, SandboxTemplate) lets an operator swap the coding/ops agent runtime with a YAML edit, no controller rebuild, unlike our compiled Go Harness interface.
- Substrate's golden-snapshot suspend/resume per Actor (gVisor or microVM/Cloud Hypervisor+Kata) reuses a warm sandbox instead of a cold pod per task, worth stealing if our worker cold-start cost ever matters.
- Egress mTLS-interception gateway swaps an inert placeholder header for the real provider API key at the network edge, so no SDK or process ever holds the real key, a cleaner variant of "no raw credentials in the sandbox" for non-Git secrets.
- A2A human-in-the-loop extension, a versioned, framework-neutral tool_approval_request/response and ask_user protocol that pauses a task at an exact boundary and resumes the same task, a cleaner wire shape than a bespoke approval state.
- EP-1270 scoped authorization, where collection filtering happens before pagination/totals, decisions come from stored or validated resource attributes (never the caller's request), and failure is closed by default.
- Idle-session expiration via a leader-only sweep under a Postgres row lock, once a minute, with a durable per-row execution claim that expires if the worker crashes, a concrete pattern for our own task garbage collection.

### agentgateway (2)
- one CEL expression language reused everywhere: HTTP/MCP authorization rules, rate-limit keys, virtual-model routing (crates/agentgateway/src/cel, http/authorization.rs, mcp/rbac.rs)
- budget counters held in memory (dashmap) and flushed to Postgres/SQLite every 5s with an atomic upsert and restart reconciliation (http/budget/database.rs) - a working reference for our hold-then-ledger design
- token-based local rate limiting settled after the real response is known, keyed by an arbitrary CEL expression such as jwt.sub or jwt.sub + model (examples/llm-keyed-rate-limit)
- SPIFFE workload identity (control/spiffe.rs): an X509Source rotates the gateway's own mTLS certificate from a local Workload API for inbound and outbound TLS
- a built-in LLM/MCP playground and spend dashboard shipped in the UI (ui/src/pages/Playground.tsx, Costs.tsx), not curl as the only way to see a result
- conditional virtual-model routing by CEL on parsed request fields (cost_tier metadata, max_tokens) as a declarative cheap-to-frontier fallback ladder, the same shape as our model tiers
- MCP guardrail phases (mcp/guardrails: client.rs, methods.rs, phase.rs) as a pluggable pre/post filter pipeline around tool calls, worth copying once agents call MCP tools through our gateway

### Bifrost (Maxim) (2)
- Hierarchical budget and rate-limit checking (customer > team > virtual key > provider config), each level independently checked and independently deducted on every request, see docs/features/governance/budget-and-limits.mdx
- Calendar-aligned budget resets (1d/1w/1M/1Q/1Y snap to UTC boundaries, sub-day windows stay rolling) as an opt-in flag on any budget owner, a small config idea worth copying into our cost ledger
- Deny-by-default model access since v1.5.0: an empty models array on a key denies every model, callers must opt in with an explicit list or a wildcard, a safer default than an implicit allow-all
- RE2 regex allow/deny patterns for model names on a key (regex:^gpt-4.*), cheaper than enumerating every model id per tier rule
- Gitleaks-backed secrets-detection guardrail that scans prompts and completions before they leave the gateway, a concrete pattern for stopping credential leakage through model output
- MCP tool calls are suggestions only by default, a second explicit API call is required to execute one, autoexecution is opt-in per tool via tools_to_auto_execute, matching our default-deny instinct for anything an agent can do

### Envoy AI Gateway (now Agent Router) (2)
- CEL-based per-request cost expressions (internal/llmcostcel) that turn token counts into a numeric cost consumed by Envoy's global rate-limit filter. A cleaner way to express a pricing rule than hardcoding it in Go; worth it for factory-gateway's worst-case-price clamp.
- BackendSecurityPolicy's OIDC token-exchange option for AWS/GCP/Azure backends (api/v1alpha1/backendsecurity_policy.go), an alternative to a static secretRef that avoids a long-lived provider key sitting in a Secret. Worth mirroring for factory-gateway's own gateway-to-provider leg.
- MCP tool authorization scoped per identity via CEL over JWT claims and MCP context (docs/proposals/006-mcp-gateway, 010-mcp-token-exchange, 011-mcp-backend-crd): a caller only discovers and can invoke tools it is permitted to use. More granular than our single coder capability class.
- internal/backendauth is one small handler per provider auth scheme (x-api-key for Anthropic, SigV4 for AWS, Azure AD token, GCP), each under 40 lines. A reasonable shape if factory-gateway ever needs to front more providers directly instead of through LiteLLM.
- aigw run starts a full local OpenAI-compatible router with one env var and one command, no Kubernetes. Worth stealing as an onboarding idea even though our own target is Kubernetes-only.

### TensorZero (2)
- Cost rate limits estimate cost upfront with a configurable default_cost, take the hit, then reconcile against the provider's real reported usage after the call (crates/tensorzero-core/src/rate_limiting) -- the same estimate-then-reconcile shape as our gateway's hold-then-book, worth checking our clamp logic against.
- Rate limit rules scope by tag (tensorzero::each per value, tensorzero::total summed) or by API-key public ID, matched in priority order until the first hit -- a compact declarative shape for per-project or per-user quotas.
- {'API keys are sk-t0-PUBLICID-SECRET, twelve characters public then secret': 'the public id is embedded in the key itself, so scoping, revocation and audit logs never need a lookup table.'}
- Per-provider cost config is a list of JSON-pointer extraction rules (pointer, cost_per_million, required), including negative entries to net out prompt-cache discounts -- a clean, provider-agnostic way to keep a cost ledger honest without hardcoding per-vendor response shapes.
- {'Dynamic per-request credential override (a dynamic:': 'prefix on api_key_location) sits next to static env-var credentials as a one-line escape hatch, worth having in factory-gateway for one-off overrides without a redeploy.'}

### Weave Router (2)
- Hold-then-settle PrepaidAuthorization state machine (Reserved to Settled or Released, UpperBoundUsdMicros as a worst-case reserve) as a concrete, production-tested pattern for our own per-task budget holds.
- Explicit fail-closed rule for model selection: a scorer failure returns 503 with no silent fallback model, written down after a past silent heuristic fallback masked a real regression.
- Generated, hash-stamped policy documentation (docs/POLICY_INFERENCE.md) produced from the same source that enforces it, so the policy table read by humans can never drift from the code.
- Per-key model and provider exclusion list as a primitive for scoping a shared router to an approved model set (how we would keep it to our own ladder's models only).
- GET /v1/sessions/:id/cost and a POST /v1/route dry-run endpoint as cheap, user-facing cost and routing transparency primitives worth mirroring in our console.
- One-command per-harness installer (npx @weave-os/router --claude / --codex / --opencode) that patches the target tool's config and ships a clean uninstall path.

### Emdash (2)
- Provider plugin registry (packages/plugins/src/agents/registry.ts, impl/) declares PATH binary names, install and self-update argv, ACP capability and hook installation per agent CLI in one file. A clean template for widening our Go Harness interface past opencode.
- Agent Client Protocol (@agentclientprotocol/sdk) drives structured session and turn events for 23 of 37 providers, replacing stdout scraping for status. Worth adopting once opencode or Codex expose it, instead of the log-pattern detectors our supervisor relies on.
- Diff-and-checks pane (source-control/browser/diff-view, checks-list.tsx, CheckRunBucket) puts the PR diff, review comments and per-check pass/fail/pending/duration in one view next to the terminal. Directly answers our console's missing diff view.
- Cron-triggered automations (builtin-catalog.ts) pair a cron expression and timezone with a canned prompt, for example a Monday 10:00 "find critical bugs" sweep. A cheap model for a scheduled, prompt-driven TaskBrief source alongside our issue-driven one.
- Secret<string> wrapper that only exposes plaintext at true boundaries (safeStorage/keychain writes, ssh2 connect, wire edges), enforced by convention across the codebase. A useful discipline pattern even though our agents should never hold key material at all.
- {'Host-authoritative reconciliation (ADR 0001)': 'treat the filesystem as ground truth and the local database as a mirror plus annotations, rather than Kubernetes-style spec/status convergence, because the tool is a guest on a shared host. Relevant if we ever let a human touch a worktree outside factoryd.'}
- {'Session resume': 'closing and reopening the app reattaches the running terminal and agent state without losing context. A concrete bar for a live task detail page in our console, which today has no real-time view of a running session.'}

### HumanLayer and CodeLayer (2)
- {'The request_approval MCP tool pattern': "wire Claude Code's own permission_prompt_tool hook straight to an external daemon endpoint, so escalation happens at the exact point a tool would otherwise run."}
- {'The RPI slash-command files (research_codebase.md, create_plan.md, implement_plan.md) as a ready template for a plan-review gate': 'versioned prompts that force research and plan into written artifacts before any code.'}
- The hierarchical hotkey-scope stack (HotkeyScopeBoundary, scopeManager) for a keyboard-first console, so approval views and session views never fight over the same keys.
- Surfacing cost_usd and total_tokens directly in per-task state from day one, taken straight from the harness's own self-reported totals, instead of bolting cost visibility on later.
- Git worktrees as the concrete unit of parallel agent workspace on one host (hack/create_worktree.sh, launch -w DIRECTORY), proven workable even if unpolished.

### Superset (2)
- The relay JWT and gate ticket split: one set of host-service procedure names, callable identically from CLI, MCP, mobile or web, addressed differently only by where the work physically runs. A template for giving factoryd one API that future surfaces can share.
- Audience-scoped signed tokens where the executor holds only the verification public key and can never mint its own. Worth checking our own gateway-verified tokens give the same guarantee.
- Stripping all identity material, workspace id, git token, agent credentials, before a sandbox is promoted to a shared golden fork base. Apply the same rule to any template or base image we let agents fork.
- Per-terminal, short-lived, host-issued attribution tokens kept separate from the main host auth token. Finer grained than one identity per task.
- An explicit, commented tool allowlist for a chat-triggered agent. Worth copying if we ever add chat intake.
- A reaction emoji as an in-progress indicator, plus an explicit thread quiet, stop and hand-back state machine for async chat sessions. Cheap and effective.

### Agent Orchestrator (AO) (2)
- SCM Observer pattern (backend/internal/observe/scm): a provider-neutral poller (30s tick, ETag guards, semantic diffing) that writes durable PR, CI and review facts, decoupled from the lifecycle logic that reacts to them. Good template for adding GitHub or GitLab intake without duplicating our reaction code, and for a lighter nudge-the-existing-worker path for cheap retries.
- Status derived at read time, never stored: AO's OBSERVE to UPDATE to DERIVE split keeps a durable fact log and computes kanban state fresh on every read. Removes a whole bug class where a stored status drifts from reality. Worth adopting for our own console.
- ACP (Agent Client Protocol, github.com/coder/acp-go-sdk) as the wire format for structured, non-terminal driving of a coding agent. An emerging shared standard with several agent vendors already implementing it, worth evaluating against our bespoke Go Harness interface.
- The reviewer capability gateway pattern (ADR 0002): a structured, no-shell surface for specific git reads, bound to an immutable task manifest, shipped honestly labelled as not a sandbox. Useful middle step wherever we want an agent to read repo state without a full sandboxed pod.
- The PostHog cost-control runbook (docs/posthog-cost-controls.md): ingestion rate limits, per-install caps, a kill-switch env var, billing hard limits. A reusable checklist for our own SigNoz telemetry cost discipline, unrelated to agent spend but a real ops pattern worth lifting.
- Mobile approval companion over an opt-in LAN listener or Cloudflare tunnel/Tailscale, password-protected and off by default. Our console has no mobile view at all. A tunnel-based approval-only view would be a small, high-value addition.

### Vibe Kanban (2)
- {'The NormalizedEntry and NormalizedConversation event schema (crates/executors/src/logs/mod.rs)': "one shape (UserMessage, ToolUse, ErrorMessage, Thinking, TokenUsageInfo, ActionType, ToolStatus including PendingApproval, Denied, TimedOut) that every agent CLI's raw stdout is parsed into before the UI ever sees it. A direct template for what our own Harness event stream should normalise to."}
- WebSocket push for both the diff and the process log (stream_workspace_diff_ws, stream_workspaces_ws) instead of polling, the concrete mechanism to fix our console's own admitted "no live streams, no diff view" gap. htmx's SSE or WS extension could carry the same normalised event JSON with little client code.
- {'The StandardCodingAgentExecutor trait shape (spawn, spawn_follow_up, spawn_review, normalize_logs, discover_options, get_preset_options, check_availability)': 'one trait, N adapters, each adapter owning only "build this CLI\'s command" and "parse this CLI\'s stdout." Everything else (worktree, diff, streaming, database) stays shared code.'}
- {'Batched inline review comments': 'a human adds a comment to any line in the diff view, and every comment across every file is collected and sent as one combined follow-up message rather than one round trip per comment.'}
- The git-worktree-as-per-task-filesystem-layout convention is worth copying on its own, independent of their lack of process isolation. It can sit inside our own gVisor pod as the working-directory layout, giving branch separation without weakening our sandbox boundary.
- A per-tool-call approve or deny dialog (ExecutorApprovalBridge) as a fast, in-session path for low-risk actions, kept separate from and smaller-grained than our workflow-state approval, which stays the path for anything that needs an audit trail.

### LangWatch (2)
- Assemble a replay transcript by ordering every event in a task's own stream by timestamp, not by re-parsing the latest agent call, so a session that ends on a single tool-only turn still renders in full.
- Join a session to its pull request over two channels, the working directory a session reports at each turn plus an explicit declare-your-context command for anything done outside it, matched against the PR's head branch.
- Keep an agent's self-reported cost beside the cost computed from its own token counts, using the computed figure as the total and treating a mismatch between the two as a drift signal, not noise.
- Mint a git credential per turn rather than per task, scoped to the two permissions actually needed, cached only briefly, handed to the worker as an environment variable and never written to disk.
- Redact secrets in three layers, exact vendor key prefixes, a shape and entropy heuristic, and contextual naming such as "the password is", replacing a hit with a flat marker rather than a blank, and treat over-redaction as a bug as serious as a leak.
- Write a sandbox's actual limits into its own docs in plain language, naming which boundary is enforced in the kernel and which is cooperative today, instead of only describing the parts that work.
- Number design decisions as individual dated files under one directory rather than folding them into a single running log.
- Split a spend cap into named scopes, organisation, team, project, key, person, group and end user, so one key can protect a noisy caller without capping everyone behind it.

### Promptfoo (2)
- The promptfoo-action GitHub Action pattern: on a PR touching prompt files, run eval on base and head, post one PR comment with a pass-rate and score diff table plus a link to the full interactive result. This is the literal shape of the before-and-after gate we do not have for the harness, supervisor or review prompt.
- The assertion type vocabulary as a ready-made schema for a prompt regression file: equals, contains, regex, is-json, is-valid-function-call, tool-call-f1 and the trajectory:tool-used, trajectory:tool-sequence, trajectory:step-count family for grading tool-calling agents. Cheaper than inventing our own expectation DSL.
- The indirectInjectionVar mechanism from the indirect-prompt-injection plugin: one config field names the templated variable holding untrusted text, and adversarial payloads are generated against it. Copyable shape for our own trust-boundary tests: name the issue-body or PR-comment variable in the harness, supervisor and review prompt templates and fail CI on instruction-following.
- The coding-agent plugin catalogue (repo-prompt-injection, terminal-output-injection, secret-env-read, sandbox-read-escape, sandbox-write-escape, verifier-sabotage, network-egress-bypass, procfs-credential-read, delayed-ci-exfil, generated-vulnerability, automation-poisoning, steganographic-exfil) as a threat checklist. Usable as a list of fixture attacks even without running promptfoo's engine.
- The adaptive rate-limit scheduler design (src/scheduler: AIMD concurrency, provider header parsing for OpenAI and Anthropic, exponential backoff, 256 tests) as a reference if LiteLLM's own throttling is ever too coarse for one provider.

### Arcade (2)
- Decorator-declared tool auth: requires_auth=GitHub(scopes=[repo]) on a Python function, the runtime injects a live token into context, the model and MCP client never see it
- 23 built-in OAuth provider classes (GitHub, Google, Slack, Notion, Linear, and more) each a thin subclass carrying a provider_id, a reusable shape for a small credential-broker catalogue
- MCP Resource Server pattern: every HTTP request is a Bearer token checked against JWKS from the org's own IdP (RFC 9728 discovery), separate from the tool-level OAuth layer
- arcade evals scores whether a model picked the right tool and the right arguments, a cheap regression check before a new tool goes live
- arcade new scaffolds pyproject.toml, .env.example and a working server.py in one command, first tool call in under five minutes

### Deno Claw Patrol (2)
- Placeholder credentials: agents get shape-valid fake tokens (ghp_clawpatrol_placeholder_do_not_use) via env pushdown, the gateway swaps in the real secret only at MITM time, so a compromised task process never holds a usable key.
- CEL as the rule-condition language (google/cel-go) with per-protocol typed facets (http.*, sql.*, k8s.*, ssh.*), and the explicit design choice that unevaluable conditions fail closed instead of silently skipping the rule.
- Trust-on-first-use plugin sandboxing: default-deny OS sandbox (Linux namespaces plus Landlock, macOS seatbelt) with network grants recorded in a committed clawpatrol.lock.hcl, plus Sigstore build-provenance attestation for plugins pulled from GitHub.
- Protocol-aware endpoint plugins that decode Postgres, ClickHouse and Kubernetes API traffic well enough to expose typed facets (sql.verb, k8s.resource) to the rule engine, not just host and path.

### Infisical Agent Vault (2)
- {'The proposal flow': 'an agent that hits a 403 gets a proposal_hint naming the denied host, files a structured request (services and credential slots, one human-readable message), gets an approval_url to hand the human in chat, then polls. A self-service capability-request pattern our human-approval flow lacks.'}
- Netguard: cloud metadata IPs blocked unconditionally, RFC-1918 and other private ranges blocked by default, and a resolve-then-validate-then-dial dialer that re-checks the IP right before connecting, closing the DNS-rebinding TOCTOU gap. Directly portable to any Go egress proxy.
- Container isolation mode's default-deny egress, iptables OUTPUT DROP with a single allowed destination, host.docker.internal pinned via /etc/hosts instead of DNS so there is no DNS-exfiltration channel, capability-dropped non-root user. A concrete worked example to check our own gVisor and Cilium policy against.
- Deterministic host and path matching, exact host beats wildcard, port-specific beats any-port, longest literal path prefix wins, ties break on declaration order, with a documented worked example. Good reference if factory-gateway ever needs finer HTTP-path-level capability matching than whole-tool grants.
- A fire-and-forget request log, method, host, matched service, status, latency, auth scheme and which credential keys were used, but never header values, bodies or query strings, with a pluggable sink interface and age-or-row-count retention trimming. A cheap, safe-by-construction shape for our own audit trail.
- A 34-entry built-in catalog of common APIs, host, auth type, suggested credential key, so a new service rule starts from a template instead of a blank form.

### Keycard (2)
- {'The credential-provider abstraction in the Python SDK (packages/oauth/src/keycardai/oauth/server/credentials.py)': "ClientSecret, WebIdentity and WorkloadIdentity as three interchangeable ways to prove an application's identity to the token endpoint, with WorkloadIdentity taking a pluggable IdentityTokenSource (FileTokenSource for EKS, AKS and any Kubernetes projected ServiceAccount token, GCPMetadataTokenSource, FlyTokenSource). A clean shape to copy if our own identity proxy ever needs to support more than one cluster or cloud."}
- {'The Temporal integration (packages/temporal)': 'a `@grant(resource)` decorator plus a `KeycardInterceptor` that mints a fresh token per activity execution and writes nothing to workflow history. We also run one Temporal workflow per task; the discipline of scoping a credential to the activity rather than the workflow, and keeping it out of durable history, is worth checking against our own gate and session activities.'}
- Cedar as the policy language for per-tool rules, with `forbid` always overriding `permit`, an `@itl("prompt")` annotation that turns a single rule into a human-approval gate, and a console-level observe-only mode that replays a new policy version against real recent traffic before it goes live, with one-click rollback. Richer authoring and test workflow than our flat YAML file.
- {'The Claude Code hook wiring itself (plugins/keycard-cli/.claude-plugin/plugin.json)': 'a SessionStart hook that warns when the session was not launched under policy control, and PreToolUse plus UserPromptSubmit hooks that shell out to a policy check before every tool call and every prompt. A working, minimal reference for hooking Claude Code if we ever add local guardrails for engineers running agents outside the factory.'}
- {'Policy evaluated at issuance, not at use': 'their STS either mints a scoped credential or mints nothing, so a denied request has no credential to leak, intercept or revoke later. Worth an explicit check that our gateway never holds a virtual key before the capability, budget and approval checks all pass.'}
- {'Delegation chaining': "each hop in a multi-agent call gets its own narrower token, and revoking the originating session invalidates every token in the chain at once. We mint one task-scoped token per pod and have no equivalent for a task that spawns sub-agents or calls another agent's tools; worth a look once that shape appears in our design."}

### OneCLI (2)
- Fail-closed body-condition matching: a truncated request body resolves by rule polarity instead of silently passing or silently blocking (apps/gateway/crates/policy/src/condition_match.rs)
- Declared harness capabilities plus a conformance test suite every adapter must pass, including a fake adapter (packages/agent-protocol/src/harness.ts)
- A steer() verb that injects a message into a running agent turn, with a joined or missed acknowledgement event
- An automated open-core boundary check that counts references from the OSS composition root into a gated crate (packages/api/src/licensing/ee-boundary.ts)
- One generic MITM broker with declarative per-service interception rules, instead of a bespoke MCP server per integration
- A single chat surface for starting, watching and approving a task, closing part of the gap our console has with no real-time event stream

### Stacklok ToolHive (2)
- Cedar (github.com/cedar-policy/cedar-go) as a real declarative ABAC language for tool authorisation, checked through one interface (pkg/vmcp/core/admission.go's core.Admission) shared by list-filtering and call-denial, closing the class of bug where a tool is listed but its call is refused
- The two-boundary auth split: incoming caller auth (OIDC/anonymous) is a fully separate concern from outgoing auth to each backend (token exchange RFC-8693, header injection, AWS STS), configured per backend in MCPExternalAuthConfig
- Rate limiting as a decorator around the CallTool seam only (pkg/vmcp/ratelimit/decorator.go), explicitly not on the pre-flight CheckToolCall path, so a check never consumes a token, and fail-open on limiter infrastructure errors so a broken limiter cannot become a denial-of-service on the whole gateway
- CRD/operator lifecycle split into two Helm charts (operator-crds, operator), so CRD upgrades and controller upgrades roll independently, with Helm snapshot tests to catch accidental template drift
- The open-core stub pattern (oboStrategyStub returning obo.ErrEnterpriseRequired, replaced only if an out-of-tree binary calls RegisterOBOStrategy from its own init()): the gated feature fails loud and typed at the call site, not silently, and the OSS binary never needs to know the enterprise code exists

### CubeSandbox (2)
- PVM: install a replacement host kernel (Tencent's OpenCloudOS-Kernel fork) on an ordinary cloud VM to get a real /dev/kvm without the cloud provider exposing VT-x/AMD-V, directly unblocking microVMs needing bare metal
- CubeEgress design: TPROXY interception, per-SNI MITM with a proxy-held root CA, a small Match/Action/Inject policy language, and transparent credential injection so the sandbox never holds the real third-party API key
- cubecow: XFS reflink (FICLONE) copy-on-write engine for instant, storage-cheap per-sandbox rootfs forks instead of copying a whole template image
- CONTRIBUTING.md's AI-Generated Code Policy (AI agents must not add their own Signed-off-by, a human reviews and certifies DCO) is worth copying verbatim
- Dashboard hardening pattern: public JWT API plus an admin API bound to localhost/Docker-bridge only, 15-minute access tokens and 7-day refresh tokens with separate typ/aud claims
- The two harness-integration examples (Claude Code PreToolUse hook, OpenCode tool.execute.before hook) show a lighter sandboxing granularity: redirect only the Bash tool call into a microVM, leave Read/Write/Edit on the host

### Docker Sandboxes, MCP Gateway, cagent (2)
- Sandbox Kits: a YAML spec declaring tools, env vars, injected credentials, allowed network domains, files to drop in and startup commands for a sandbox. Docker has committed to submitting the spec to the CNCF, which would make it a portable declarative sandbox-launch format worth adopting or mapping our own TaskBrief-plus-policy shape onto.
- Host-side forward proxy credential injection: the agent inside the microVM only ever sees a placeholder value in its outbound request. The proxy on the host swaps in the real API key or token after the request leaves the VM, so the raw credential never enters the sandbox. Same shape as our own 'sandbox holds no key material' rule, worth comparing against our gateway-verifies-JWT approach for any secret that is not a Kubernetes-native identity.
- The harness concept (docs/features/harnesses): an agent config can declare harness type claude-code, codex, opencode or pi and delegate the whole coding loop to an external CLI, while Docker Agent supplies orchestration, hooks, permission checks and OCI distribution. This is an independent, shipped implementation of exactly our own pluggable Harness interface, one generation ahead in breadth (four external CLIs, not one).
- Board (docker agent board): a Kanban TUI where each card runs an agent in a tmux session on its own git worktree, with a customisable column pipeline (Dev -> Review -> Push -> Done) whose forward move sends the destination column's prompt to the agent, live status per card (starting/loading/attaching/running/crashed) and a bound diff view. Our own vision doc lists 'no diff view' and 'no real-time stream' as known console gaps. This is a working answer to both, in a TUI rather than a web console.
- Deny-by-default host proxy for all outbound sandbox traffic, managed by a sbx policy CLI independent of the agent process and the model, with sbx policy ls and sbx policy log to inspect rules and traffic. Confirms our own default-deny network policy is the right default, but also a cautionary tale: Docker ships a permissive default allowlist with broad wildcards (*.googleapis.com) that the docs explicitly warn to prune, a trap worth checking we do not repeat in our own gateway defaults.
- pkg/permissions: an Allow/Ask/Deny Checker over glob tool-name and argument patterns (e.g. shell:cmd=rm*), evaluated deny-then-allow-then-ask entirely in Go, with a Merge to combine several rule sets. A small, reusable shape for any place we want per-tool-call policy finer than network egress without touching a prompt.

### Kubernetes SIG agent-sandbox (2)
- The SandboxTemplate/SandboxWarmPool/SandboxClaim split: a template is the security-reviewed blueprint, a warm pool pre-provisions N idle pods against it, and a claim atomically checks one out (or cold-starts if the pool is empty). This replaces our per-task hand-built pod spec with a three-object chain a platform team can review once and reuse; the claim is the only object a task-spawning caller touches.
- Managed NetworkPolicy at the template level, not per pod: one shared NetworkPolicy object covers every sandbox from a template, with a secure default (ingress from the router only, egress to the public internet minus RFC1918 and the cloud metadata address) that a template author can override once. Cheaper to audit than a policy generated per task.
- The ValidatingAdmissionPolicy in examples/policy/vap/secure-sandbox-policy.yaml: seven CEL expressions rejecting any Sandbox that lacks runtimeClassName: gvisor, sets hostNetwork, mounts a service account token, runs privileged, keeps a capability, skips resource limits, or runs as root. This is policy enforced by the API server itself, before the controller ever sees the object; worth lifting directly as a template for our own admission gate on task pod specs.
- System-reserved label/annotation protection (agents.x-k8s.io/* is stripped from user-supplied pod template metadata on both create and adopt): closes a concrete cross-tenant traffic-hijack bug class (spoofing the Service-selector label to steal another sandbox's traffic) that a naive metadata-passthrough implementation would have. Worth checking our own namespace/pod metadata propagation code for the same hole.
- Ed25519 scoped bearer tokens (sandbox-router/authz/scopedtoken_v2.go): a token signed over namespace, sandbox name, sandbox UID, port, method, path and expiry, unforgeable without the private key, and useless for anything but the one call it names. A cleaner shape than a broad per-task bearer token for any narrow, short-lived, single-purpose credential we hand to a task (e.g. a one-shot callback token to the gateway).
- Suspend/resume via spec.operatingMode with PVC-backed state (pod deleted, PVC retained, identical pod recreated on resume): a generic, cluster-agnostic hibernate primitive that does not need GKE-specific memory snapshotting to be useful for cost control on idle long-running sandboxes.

### microsandbox (2)
- Secret placeholder substitution at egress: the guest env only ever holds `$MSB_<VAR>`, and the host-side TLS-intercepting proxy swaps in the real credential only when the DNS-pinned destination IP, SNI and HTTP authority all match the secret's allowed host. A real value never enters the VM. Directly applicable anywhere a worker must call a third-party API without routing through factory-gateway.
- DNS-to-IP pin set: a domain rule only matches a connection whose destination IP was actually returned by a DNS query for that domain from that sandbox, closing the TOCTOU gap a name-only allowlist leaves open. Worth checking against our own gateway/git/module-proxy egress rules.
- `msb doctor` and `msb context`: one command reports virtualization support and fixes, another reports the fully resolved backend and config without ever printing a secret. Good shape for a factoryd debug verb on a task's resolved policy.
- Config precedence with a hard-wired top layer: built-in defaults, global config, per-workload YAML, CLI/SDK input, then an administrator `managed.json` that always wins and cannot be weakened by any layer below it. Clean model for keeping our Git-sourced policy always outranking a per-task override.
- Restricted in-guest security profile (no_new_privs, drop mount-admin capability, nosuid/nodev on user mounts) as an opt-in knob independent of the network policy. A reusable idea for a pod security tier per capability class once we have more than `coder`.

### NVIDIA OpenShell (2)
- Trust-on-first-use SHA256 binary pinning for process identity (docs/security/best-practices.mdx), a cheap defence against a swapped executable inheriting a capability grant.
- An SMT-solver boundary proof before a policy change ships (openshell-prover, docs/how-it-works/policies/prover.mdx), a machine-checked "stays within X" proof rather than a diff review, for auto-approved policy widenings.
- Non-overridable SSRF hard blocks (loopback, link-local, 0.0.0.0, 169.254.169.254) that no policy YAML can lift, per docs/security/best-practices.mdx.
- Two independent authorisation checks before a credential is released (network policy must allow the call, and the credential binding must match host/port/path), narrower blast radius than one combined check.
- OCSF-native JSONL audit export (docs/observability/ocsf-json-export.mdx) for SIEM ingestion, something our SigNoz traces do not give us today.
- {'An agent-facing policy API (policy.local': 'GET /v1/denials, POST /v1/proposals) so an agent reads its own denials and proposes a fix, instead of a human hand-editing YAML first.'}

### Kiro (2)
- EARS grammar for requirements.md: numbered 'Requirement N' blocks with a User Story and 'WHEN trigger, THE Component SHALL behaviour' criteria, plus an upfront component glossary.
- tasks.md convention: checkbox tasks, an optional-task marker for tasks skippable in an MVP, a trailing '_Requirements: X.Y_' tag on every leaf task, and explicit numbered Checkpoint tasks whose content is 'ensure tests pass, ask the user if questions arise.'
- Analyze Requirements: a dedicated pass that reasons over the whole requirement set for contradictions, ambiguity and gaps, and turns findings into chat questions with selectable fixes rather than guessing.
- Correctness properties: the design phase extracts quantified invariants from EARS requirements and generates property-based tests with shrinking, each property tagged back to the requirement and task it validates.
- Permission engine shape: deny beats ask beats allow with the most restrictive rule winning regardless of scope, hardcoded invariants the agent cannot edit even under full autonomy, and headless mode turning every ask into a deny.
- Named network-egress tiers per sandbox (git-proxy only, common package registries, open internet, plus a custom allowlist) offered as a simple per-task choice instead of one fixed allow-list.
- Issue-label intake: a plain 'kiro' label or an '/kiro' mention starts a task, and each triggering user gets a separate sandbox, so simultaneous claims never collide.

### Superpowers (2)
- SessionStart hook injects a short 'check for a relevant skill before any action, including clarifying questions' directive at session start and after compaction: the model for a preamble our own worker prompt or TaskBrief could inject before every session
- A per-plan ledger file (progress.md) recording 'Task N: complete (commits a..b)' and 'Ruling: <decision> - <why> - <cost if wrong>' as the recovery map after context loss, read before trusting the agent's own memory: directly answers our own gap that session records exist but nothing resumes cleanly from them
- Model-tier selection by task complexity (mechanical edit = cheap model, integration/judgment = standard, architecture and final review = most capable, +1 tier on a stuck fix loop) is the same ladder shape as our coding-cheap/coding-frontier/review-frontier/supervisor, but tied to a legible rubric (file count, whether the plan already gives the exact code) we could fold into factoryd's escalation policy
- 'Rulings, not stalls': decide on ambiguity and keep going, log 'Ruling: <what> - <why> - <cost if wrong>', and stop only for four named cases (irreversible op, security-sensitive action, external side effect, plan broken beyond repair), a tighter, more general version of our supervisor's continue/redirect/retry/escalate that we could apply to the agent itself, not just the supervisor
- Fix-loop cap of 5 rounds: rounds 1-3 resume the same implementer with the findings verbatim, rounds 4-5 hand off to a fresh implementer on a stronger model, then a human-visible adjudication step if still unresolved: a concrete, boundable algorithm to drop into our review-gate retry policy instead of an open-ended retry count
- diagnosing-superpowers' evidence discipline (every finding cites path:line, parallel per-dimension analyst subagents read the same transcript, explicit approval gate before any bundle is built or shared) is close to a ready design for the trace/replay UI we admit we lack, and a pattern for staffing our own 'every alert gets a verdict' loop with cheap parallel analysts
- The eval harness named in AGENTS.md (superpowers-evals/Quorum drives real CLIs through a QA agent graded against scenario acceptance criteria plus deterministic post-checks, before/after numbers required in every skill-changing PR) is exactly the regression suite we flagged as missing for our own harness prompt
- brainstorming's three-tier approval gate (spike/bounded/architectural, each needing an explicit human sign-off before the next artifact exists) is a usable shape for the spec-driven intake we do not have yet: an issue could carry a state machine of 'design proposed -> design approved -> plan approved -> executing' instead of jumping straight from issue to code

### Cleric (2)
- A post-deploy verification window keyed to the merged PR's own stated intent, watching p95 latency, error rate and DB connections for up to 14 days, not our current 15-minute staging soak, catches regressions that only show up days later.
- Confidence-scored findings that stay silent below a threshold and only post above it (async by default, faster interactive mode on request), a cheap pattern for our own alert-verdict and supervisor escalation path.
- Every line of a fix PR links to the production data behind it, a concrete discipline for our own model-review output and fix PRs so a human approver checks evidence, not just a verdict.
- A layered knowledge graph: a deterministic layer built by walking infrastructure with the same CLIs an engineer uses, plus a fuzzy inferred layer of undocumented relationships, refreshed by background jobs and by findings written back from live investigations. A concrete shape for our stated gap of no project memory and no codebase index.
- Read-only-by-default integrations with an hourly health check and a visible Connected/Error badge per integration, a simple pattern our console could copy today for surfacing gateway and credential health.
- Cost expressed in human-legible units of work (10 credits per investigation, 1 credit per minute of chat) rather than raw token spend, worth carrying into our own cost view.

### Datadog Bits AI (Dev Agent and SRE) (2)
- 'Fix with Bits' as a one-click affordance directly on an Error Tracking issue, a flaky test, or a Cloud Security finding, starting a scoped session with the finding as context already loaded. A concrete shortcut for turning a SigNoz alert into a bounded task without a human writing a TaskBrief by hand.
- Per-repo internet-access tiers (No Internet Access, Default Allowlist, Custom + Default, Custom Allowlist), set in product settings rather than by redeploying a network policy. Worth stealing as a per-project knob on top of our Cilium default-deny, so a project can widen its own egress list without a cluster-level change.
- Repo-native instruction files read at session start (AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, .cursorrules, .claude/skills/, .codex/skills/, .gemini/skills/). A lightweight, harness-agnostic convention for per-project context that costs nothing beyond a docs convention, worth adopting for TaskBrief augmentation.
- The CI feedback loop: the agent watches CI logs and PR comments after pushing and keeps revising until checks pass or a human blocks it, driven by an @-mention rather than a new task. Close to what our worker should do on a reproduction-gate failure instead of failing the whole session.
- Hard product rule: Bits Code never auto-merges, regardless of confidence. A useful sanity check against our own auto-merge rule for low-risk, reproduced changes; worth re-reading their reasoning even though we deliberately go further.
- The hypothesis-tree investigation shape described in Datadog's engineering blog (targeted, hypothesis-scoped queries instead of dumping all telemetry into one summarisation pass, which the post says degrades accuracy as tokens scale linearly) is directly relevant if we ever build the planned analyzer capability class on SigNoz data.

### Latitude (2)
- Volume-scaled promotion gate for discovered clusters (PROMOTION_MIN_SESSIONS=2, PROMOTION_MAX_SESSIONS=15, PROMOTION_WINDOW_DAYS=30, CANDIDATE_EXPIRY_IDLE_DAYS=45 in packages/domain/signals/src/constants.ts) so a one-off failure never becomes a task, and near-duplicate candidates merge their evidence before anything is created.
- pgvector hybrid search (lexical tsvector plus cosine) with a cheap reranker as a second pass and an explicit fallback to embedding-order when the reranker is unreachable (assign-or-create-signal-from-score.ts), rather than one all-or-nothing similarity call.
- {'Regression-test recipe': 'capture the traces behind a fixed signal as a dataset, replay them against the new build, and score the replay with the exact evaluation that first caught the failure, so the acceptance bar is identical to the production detection bar.'}
- Webhook dispatch contract (X-Latitude-Signature HMAC-SHA256, X-Latitude-Delivery idempotency key, retries only on 429/5xx, a documented optional JSON reply carrying externalRunId and deepLinkUrl to link back an external run) is a clean template for a signed, idempotent, loosely-coupled hand-off to any external actor, not just Forgejo.
- {'Config resolution ladder per AI feature': 'LAT_AI_<FEATURE>_<SETTING> then LAT_AI_GENERATION_<SETTING> then a built-in default, the same shape as a model-tier fallback, worth copying for per-capability overrides in the gateway.'}
- {'One-shot, concurrency-safe feedback latch': 'a single conditional UPDATE ... WHERE feedback IS NULL claims the "was this alert real" verdict before any side effect runs, so two racing callers cannot both win and the verdict cannot be edited after the fact.'}

### Sentry Seer (2)
- A numeric fixability and actionability score, 0 to 1, with named thresholds super low, low, medium, high, super high, used twice, once to decide whether to act at all and again to decide how far to go, instead of a single automation on or off switch.
- The stopping point ladder, root cause, then solution, then code changes, then open PR, where the run always stops at whichever is more conservative of the score suggested point and the human configured ceiling, a one line minimum over an ordered enum.
- Delegating only the final code writing step to an already authenticated external coding agent, Claude Code, Cursor Background Agents, or GitHub Copilot, while keeping retrieval and root cause reasoning in house, so the hardest part, sandboxed execution, stays with a specialist.
- A scheduled overnight job that re reads the previous night's agent runs with a stronger model and files tickets for the broken ones, a cheap automatic verdict pass over agent output quality itself.
- Reusing the same telemetry plus code context store to review PRs a human wrote, not only PRs the agent wrote, so the same investment pays twice.
- GitHub permission tiering that names exactly which feature breaks when a specific OAuth scope is missing, instead of a single all or nothing integration broken flag.
- A tool catalog indirection, search tools and execute tool, that keeps the directly registered MCP tool count near 20 while still exposing dozens of operations, to stay under other agents' tool count ceilings.

### ChatDev (DevAll) (1)
- Visual DAG canvas backed by editable YAML (entity/graph_config.py, frontend/) as a reference for a diff-and-flow view our console lacks.
- Per-session WebSocket log and artifact streaming (server/routes/websocket.py) closes our "no real-time stream of the agent's work" gap cheaply.
- Provider-per-node abstraction (runtime/node/agent/) swaps model and base URL per node from one registry, the same shape as our tiered LiteLLM routing, confirming the pattern rather than adding a new one.
- HumanNodeExecutor's request/response pause (runtime/node/executor/human_executor.py) is a minimal template for turning "waiting on a human" into a typed graph node instead of bespoke workflow code.
- Tarjan-cycle-detection scheduler (workflow/executor, docs/user_guide/en/execution_logic.md) for bounded agent retry loops expressed declaratively rather than in Go control flow.

### CrewAI (1)
- Fingerprint dual-ID (crewai/security/fingerprint.py): every agent gets an auto human-readable id plus a UUID, creation timestamp and free-form metadata for tracing and audit. Cheap idiom worth copying for tagging every log line and event with a stable per-agent identity, independent of our task ID.
- HumanInputProvider protocol (crewai/core/providers/human_input.py): human-in-the-loop is one Protocol with sync and async variants, so the default blocking CLI input() can be swapped for a different channel without touching agent logic. Same seam we would want if the console ever needs to inject approval into a running agent loop rather than only gate a workflow step.
- LLMGuardrail and HallucinationGuardrail (crewai/tasks/llm_guardrail.py, hallucination_guardrail.py): a second, cheap LLM call checks a task's output against a rule or the source context before it is accepted, with automatic retry on failure. A smaller-scale version of our own 'cheap detectors before a stronger model' philosophy, worth a look for a fast pre-check ahead of our review-frontier gate.
- DeployValidator (crewai_cli/deploy/validate.py): `crewai deploy` runs local, deterministic checks (lockfile present, project shape valid) and blocks the network step on failure. Confirms the value of ordering gates cheap-and-local first, which is already our own CI policy.
- First-party MCP client and A2A wrapper live inside the framework core (crewai/mcp/, crewai/a2a/), not as a bolted-on plugin. crewai/mcp/tool_resolver.py, which maps MCP tool schemas onto CrewAI's own Tool type, is worth a skim if we ever proxy more MCP tools through factory-gateway.
- agents/*.jsonc plus crew.jsonc (the new default scaffold from `crewai create crew`): agent role, goal, backstory and tool list live in a git-diffable JSON file, not Python, with {placeholder} templating filled at run time. A cleaner model than a Python class for any of our own agent configuration that should be editable without a code change.

### LangGraph and LangGraph Platform (1)
- interrupt()/Command(resume=...): a typed, resumable pause exception raised inside a node, matched to a caller-supplied resume value by call order. A clean vocabulary for expressing a human-wait state, worth reusing as naming, not code, on top of Temporal signals.
- BaseCheckpointSaver: one small interface (put/get_tuple/list) with three swappable backends (memory, SQLite, Postgres) in libs/checkpoint, libs/checkpoint-sqlite, libs/checkpoint-postgres. A compact reference shape for a per-step snapshot store if we ever need one outside Temporal history.
- EncryptedSerializer (libs/checkpoint/langgraph/checkpoint/serde/encrypted.py): opt-in at-rest encryption for whatever gets persisted as step state. Cheap to imitate for any findings or state blob we write to Postgres.
- BaseStore (libs/checkpoint/langgraph/store/base): a namespaced put/get/search interface for memory that outlives one run, kept deliberately separate from per-run checkpoint state. Matches a gap we already flagged: no project memory across tasks.
- langgraph.json: a single declarative manifest (graphs, deps, env) that the CLI turns into a reproducible Docker build. A boring, worth-copying convention for describe-once, build-once.

### MetaGPT (1)
- ActionNode (metagpt/actions/action_node.py) forces an LLM turn to emit a typed, schema-checked document rather than free text, one concrete way to make a harness turn produce a structured TaskBrief-shaped artifact.
- {'Roles hand off through ProjectRepo/FileRepository, not chat': 'WritePRD writes docs/prd/*.json, WriteDesign writes docs/system_design/*.json, the next role reads the file. Validates our own "artifacts not chat" principle from the other direction.'}
- BM25ToolRecommender (metagpt/tools/tool_recommend.py) narrows a large registered tool set to the k most relevant per turn, worth copying if our worker's tool list grows past what fits comfortably in a system prompt.
- The experience pool (metagpt/exp_pool) caches (context, action, outcome) so a repeated task shape can skip a fresh model call, a cheap idea for cutting spend on repeat-shaped tasks.
- HumanProvider (metagpt/provider/human_provider.py) substitutes a human typing in the terminal for the LLM backend of any single role, a minimal way to get a human-in-the-loop checkpoint without a separate approval workflow.

### OpenAI Agents SDK (1)
- Guardrail design in docs/guardrails.md: separate input, output and tool guardrails, an explicit run_in_parallel=False/True choice between latency and guaranteed-no-side-effects, and a fail-closed rule that malformed or non-object tool-call arguments always force manual approval instead of invoking the approval callable. Worth copying verbatim into factory-gateway's own approval-callback path.
- RunState pause/resume in docs/human_in_the_loop.md: result.to_state() serializes a paused run, state.approve()/reject() resume it, and always_approve/always_reject decisions are sticky per tool identity and survive state.to_string()/from_string() round trips. A clean serialization boundary for 'task paused on a human' that our Temporal workflow could adopt directly instead of the ad hoc shape we have.
- The tracing span taxonomy in docs/tracing.md and src/agents/tracing (agent_span, generation_span, function_span, guardrail_span, handoff_span, transcription_span, speech_span, all children of one trace per Runner.run) is a ready event vocabulary for our own normalised event stream instead of inventing span names from scratch.
- Manifest-declared sandbox workspaces (docs/sandbox/guide.md: Manifest(entries={'repo': GitRepo(...)})) separate the fresh-session contract from live sandbox state, with named pluggable backends (Docker, E2B, Daytona, local Unix) behind one SandboxRunConfig. A clean shape to compare our TaskBrief-plus-git-branch construction against.
- Redaction-on-rejection in docs/guardrails.md: when an output guardrail rejects a terminal tool result, the SDK keeps replay-valid function-call metadata but replaces only the payload with a fixed placeholder ('Output withheld by an output guardrail.'). A concrete pattern for redacting a rejected agent output from our own event stream and console without breaking replay.

### Lovable (1)
- Preview toolbar: click (or Cmd/Ctrl-click for multi-select) any rendered element in the live app, then describe the change in plain language, no separate editor panel to open first
- Stable per-element IDs injected by a custom Vite plugin that survive AI edits, so a click in the rendered DOM maps back to the exact JSX node both ways, the mechanism that makes click-to-edit reliable instead of fragile
- Three-mode split (Chat to talk without touching code, Plan to produce a reviewable .lovable/plan.md before any edit, Build to execute) gives a cheap, visible checkpoint before an agent commits to changes
- Per-message credit cost shown inline in the chat history, and viewable while a message is still running, granular and legible cost attribution a user sees live rather than after the fact
- Prompt queuing: a user can stack follow-up instructions while an earlier build is still running, instead of blocking on one turn at a time
- Subagents (May 2026) show up as separate threads in an activity log, a concrete answer to 'what is the agent doing right now' for a multi-step task

### Replit Agent (1)
- Checkpoints fire at semantic milestones (feature finished, tests pass, before a risky fix), not on a timer, and bundle files, dependencies, config, database schema and data, and the full agent conversation into one restorable unit. Our session records have no replay or trace UI yet; this is the shape to copy for that gap.
- Database checkpoints are Neon Postgres branches (copy-on-write), so a rollback restores code and data together instead of leaving them out of sync. CloudNativePG has its own branching and point-in-time recovery; giving a task's Postgres schema the same per-checkpoint branch as its Git commit would give us a real undo for stateful integration tests, not just a code diff.
- History is bidirectional: roll back, then roll forward again, and a rollback followed by new work forks an alternate branch of checkpoints rather than destroying the abandoned future. Worth keeping in mind if our own session records ever grow a replay UI, so a supervisor retry does not silently discard the path not taken.
- Agent 4 splits a request into sub-tasks, each executed in an isolated copy of the project, shown as cards on a Drafts, Active, Ready, Done board, and nothing merges into the main project until a human explicitly approves that card. That is our own approval-is-a-workflow-state principle, already built as a UI pattern, one card per sub-agent.
- Intelligent Model Routing picks a model per sub-task from measured performance on real tasks rather than a fixed ladder, and Replit claims 65 percent lower average cost than a fixed frontier model at similar quality. Our own model choice is a fixed ladder by design; this is the concrete shape an outcome-learned router would take if we ever build one.

### Dagger and container-use (1)
- Secret redaction pattern: secrets are configured as references (op://, env://, vault://, file://), resolved to real values only inside the container via Dagger's own dagger.Secret type, and stripped from command output and logs. The agent's model never sees a raw credential even though the code it runs can use one. Worth copying into how factory-gateway hands MCP tool output back to the model, wherever a tool result could echo a secret.
- Non-interactive review before checkout: container-use list/log/diff let a human see what an agent did (files, exit codes, stdout/stderr per command) without checking out the branch or opening a shell. This is exactly the diff view and real-time work stream our own vision document names as missing from the console; the CLI shape (list of environments, per-environment structured log, diff) is a concrete, small target to copy.
- One environment equals one git branch plus one container, with the environment's JSON state and command history stored as git notes on a container-use/<id> ref (repository/repository.go, environment/state.go, environment/note.go). No separate database row is needed to track a worker's identity or history; git already carries it. This matches our own 'artifacts not chat' principle almost exactly and shows it is implementable with plain git plumbing (worktrees, notes, refs) rather than custom infrastructure.
- container-use terminal drops a human directly into the live container an agent is working in, for the case log and diff are not enough. A cheap, literal answer to 'how does a user intervene' that our console does not yet offer at all.
- Config layering: a committed .container-use/environment.json holds the project's default base image, setup and install commands, env vars and secrets; agents can adapt their own environment's config during a session, and a human explicitly promotes useful changes with config import. A clean default-versus-session-override split we could reuse for per-project worker image customisation.
- It ships as a plain stdio MCP server (mcpserver/tools.go, using github.com/mark3labs/mcp-go), so it attaches to any MCP-compatible harness including opencode, our own harness today, with one config line and zero code on the harness side. Confirms that betting on a harness-agnostic interface, as our Harness abstraction does, is the right layer to standardise on.

### Depot (1)
- Ephemeral builder, persistent cache split: the builder VM is thrown away after every build, but the project's layer cache lives on its own volume (formerly EBS, then Ceph, now NVMe-oF from dedicated storage hosts on Depot Metal) and is reattached to whichever fresh builder picks up the next job. Our build VM currently starts cold every time; the pattern of a stateless throwaway compute layer over a stateful cache layer is directly reusable even though our trust model forces the cache to stay read-only for agent branches (see differs).
- Per-build mTLS: the CLI gets a certificate scoped to one build, issued when a builder machine is assigned, expired when the build ends, and the control plane never hands out a longer-lived credential for build traffic. A clean small pattern for factoryd's build-VM channel, which already does no better than this.
- OIDC token exchange for CI identity: GitHub Actions, CircleCI and Buildkite jobs exchange their platform's own OIDC token for a short-lived, project-scoped Depot token instead of holding a standing API key. Worth comparing against how factory-ci's Forgejo account token is scoped, since Depot's version needs no stored secret in the CI system at all.
- Session resume, fork and share for agent sandboxes: exiting a Claude Code session persists the filesystem and the full conversation; --resume continues it, --resume --fork-session branches a new session off an old one, and the dashboard lets a teammate open and resume someone else's session. Our own vision document already names session replay and a trace UI as unfinished; this is a concrete shape for that feature, not just an idea.
- Depot Metal's stated reason for going to bare metal: running the hypervisor directly on the host lets Depot move its own monitoring and orchestration agents outside the job's microVM, shrinking what runs inside the isolation boundary and improving security incidentally. If we ever justify bare-metal nodes for microVMs (our own doc already names Kata or Firecracker as the road once build volume justifies it), this is the argument to make for going straight to a thin hypervisor rather than nesting under a cloud provider's own one.

### Namespace (1)
- The one-line runs-on swap for adopting a different runner backend without touching the rest of the workflow file: a model for letting a project pick a faster worker pool without a pipeline rewrite.
- Cache Volumes replace actions/cache with a locally-mounted cache instead of a remote upload/download, removing a full round trip on every job; worth comparing against our own gVisor-runner cache path if CI minutes are going to cache transfer.
- The Remote Builder pattern: Docker builds run on a machine sized and provisioned independently from (and larger than) the runner itself. This is the same shape as our own throwaway Hetzner build VM, used because no rootless BuildKit runs under gVisor; their public numbers (about 0.05 USD per build) are a useful sizing reference.
- A fresh Devbox per agent session that is torn down the moment the worker process exits (their Cognition/Devin integration, blog post 'Giving Devin a fast computer'). It is external, paid-customer validation of the exact per-task disposable sandbox lifecycle we already run, not a new idea, but confirmation the pattern holds at their scale.
- A per-compute-instance workload token that every API call and immutable audit-log entry traces back to. Cheap to add to our own sandbox pods for finer audit attribution, though it authenticates the machine, not a task's capability set, so it is a smaller idea than our ServiceAccount token (see differs).
- Idle auto-shutdown (Devboxes stop after 15 minutes idle, adjustable) as a single, simple, automatic cost backstop. We already exceed it with hard caps and holds, but it is a cheap belt-and-suspenders addition for any long-lived interactive sandbox we might add later.

### Nx Cloud Self-Healing CI (1)
- The always-run repair step: `nx fix-ci` is added once with `if: always()` (or `after_script` on GitLab/Bitbucket) and becomes a no-op automatically when the pipeline is green. Cheap way to bolt a repair phase onto an existing Forgejo Actions pipeline without restructuring it.
- Auto-apply is gated by three ANDed conditions: the failing task matches a configured glob, the model states high confidence, and an independent re-run explicitly verifies the fix against the failing task. That triple gate (declared scope, confidence, reproduction) is close to our own reproduction-gate idea and is concrete enough to copy for any future auto-merge path.
- PR-native approval: the fix lands as a PR comment with a reasoning summary, a diff and Apply/Reject buttons, so a human approves without leaving the code review tool. Worth doing literally for our console: post the same summary to the Forgejo PR, not only to the kanban board.
- A deterministic preset short-circuits known-mechanical failures (`nx format:check`, `sync:check`, `conformance:check`) by running the writable counterpart directly, skipping the model entirely. Same 'cheap and deterministic first' ordering we already use for verification; worth applying inside supervision too.
- Artifacts are tied to the identity of the token that produced them, and revoking that token invalidates every artifact it produced. A cheap idea for shrinking the blast radius after a leaked credential in our own gateway or cache path.

### Greptile (1)
- Build a dependency graph (files, functions, classes, call edges) before review starts, so the reviewer sees callers outside the diff, not just the patch. Our review-frontier step could take the same graph as extra context.
- Feed a model the team's own past PR review comments as few-shot context so it learns house conventions instead of restating generic advice on every PR.
- Runtime verification as a distinct pass after static review, spin up dev servers, mocks and browser agents against the PR branch, attach logs, screenshots and traces as evidence on the PR. Close to our reproduction gate, and shows what the evidence artifacts could look like in the console.
- A single merge-safety confidence score, 0 to 5, per PR plus a user-set minimum-severity floor, instead of a binary pass or fail verdict. Worth trying on our review-frontier step to cut noise without hiding real findings.
- Plain-English, file-pattern-scoped rules as a lightweight per-repo review policy layer, separate from our capability YAML, for style and convention concerns that do not need code-level enforcement.
- One-click handoff of unresolved review comments to a coding agent (Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, Devin), and a mode that keeps an agent iterating against the reviewer until clean. Our worker could consume unresolved review findings as a follow-up TaskBrief without a human round-trip.

### Plannotator (1)
- The PermissionRequest-hook pattern for Claude Code: intercept ExitPlanMode, hold the tool call open (timeout 345600s), let a human annotate in a browser, then answer allow/deny with a message field the agent reads as its next instruction. Directly portable to any harness we adopt that exposes a permission hook.
- The plain-markdown feedback convention (packages/ui/utils/parser.ts exportAnnotations): each annotation becomes a quoted excerpt, a location line, and the comment body, concatenated under one '# Plan Feedback' heading and wrapped in a directive template ('YOUR PLAN WAS NOT APPROVED... You MUST revise the plan to address ALL of the feedback below'). Worth copying verbatim for our own waiting-human reply payload.
- anchorText/anchorContext on CodeAnnotation (packages/ui/types.ts): the exact lines a comment was anchored to, plus two lines of surrounding context and the hunk header, captured at annotation time so a later, changed diff can mark the comment 'outdated' instead of silently misplacing it. Directly useful for a console diff view that survives amend/rebase.
- safeInline() in packages/ui/utils/parser.ts: collapse whitespace and defuse backtick runs before any page- or tool-supplied string is spliced into agent-facing markdown, closing the one injection path a naive exporter would leave open. Worth mirroring anywhere we forward untrusted text into a prompt.
- Conventional-Comments style severity/decorations on CodeAnnotation (important/nit/pre_existing, blocking/non-blocking/if-minor) as a cheap triage vocabulary for a diff-review card, cheaper than inventing our own taxonomy.
- PrivateBin-style sharing for the async link-share path: AES-256-GCM in the browser, only ciphertext uploaded, the key lives in the URL fragment and never reaches the server, 7-day expiry, the relay itself is a small self-hostable service. A clean pattern if we ever need to hand an artifact outside Forgejo without trusting the relay.
- Release supply-chain discipline for a single small binary: SHA256 sidecar, SLSA provenance from v0.17.2, a CycloneDX SBOM evaluated against a fresh Grype database before anything is attested, with a single gh attestation verify call as the one dependency for install-time verification. A reasonable bar for our own agent-worker image and factoryd/factory-gateway binaries.

### PR-Agent (1)
- The host-only versus repo-overridable config split in pr_agent/config_security.py (REPO_OVERRIDABLE_KEYS_BY_HOST_SECTION, REPO_HOST_ONLY_KEYS_BY_SECTION), a concrete, code-enforced list of which settings keys a repository's own config file may touch and which stay host-controlled, closing named threats such as a repo pointing the skills loader at ~/.ssh or a repo widening its own sibling-repo read allowlist.
- The compression strategy for large diffs in pr_agent/algo/pr_processing.py, prioritising languages and additions over deletions and packing hunks by token budget until a hard stop, a reusable pattern for our own coder harness prompt construction when a diff will not fit.
- The self-reflection pass, a cheap second model call that scores and re-ranks the first pass's own suggestions and drops the ones scoring zero, a small two-call pattern worth trying inside our reviewer or supervisor step to cut noise before a human sees it.
- Agent Skills (pr_agent/algo/skills_loader.py), an admin-curated SKILL.md library injected into prompts, with paths host-only and enable and max_skills_tokens the only keys a repo may set, a workable shape for distributing review standards across every project in a factory without checking them into each repository.
- Persistent inline comment fingerprinting (comment_identity.py, persistent_inline_comments) to avoid reposting the same suggestion across repeated runs on one PR, a small de-duplication mechanism worth copying for our own reviewer output.

### Aider (1)
- {'The repo map': 'tree-sitter tag extraction (grep_ast, aider/repomap.py) ranked with networkx PageRank and cached with diskcache, a dependency-light way to give an agent codebase context without embeddings. Directly answers the "no codebase index" gap in our own vision.'}
