- litellm's model_cost metadata table, already used in aider/models.py for per-call and per-session cost display, is a ready-made $/token and context-window source factory-gateway could read from instead of hand-maintaining one.
- {'The ConfirmGroup pattern in aider/coders/base_coder.py': 'ask once per class of repeated risky action ("run shell commands? (yes/no/always/never this session)") rather than once per action, a cheap approval-batching idea for the console.'}
- An exact per-turn cost line printed after every model reply, not an estimate, worth matching in granularity in our own cost view.

### Plandex (1)
- Autonomy-level matrix (none/basic/plus/semi/full, in docs/docs/core-concepts/autonomy.md) bundles related booleans (auto-load-context, auto-apply, auto-exec, auto-debug, auto-commit) into named presets instead of one flag. Our supervisor concept could borrow the naming, not the mechanism.
- Auto-debug loop: a failed command re-enters the plan as a prompt (getOnApplyExecFail in app/cli/plan_exec/apply_exec.go) with the exit status and output, bounded by an attempt counter (AutoDebug tries). Same idea as our supervisor retries, worth comparing bound choices.
- Cumulative diff sandbox: changes are held as a separate pending layer and only written to the working tree on explicit or auto apply, with an atomic rollback path (lib.Rollback). Cheaper than a full sandbox for local single-user use, not a substitute for ours.
- Server ships its own embedded LiteLLM proxy (app/server/model/litellm.go, EnsureLiteLLM) as the single point of model routing. Independent confirmation that LiteLLM is the default choice for this job.
- Plan-level version control distinct from git (docs/docs/core-concepts/branches.md, version-control.md): every plan update is a revertible step, and branches let you compare two model runs on the same task. Not needed since our unit of work is already a Git branch and PR, but the compare-two-attempts-side-by-side UX is worth a look for the console.

### DBOS (1)
- SetWorkflowID as an idempotency key: a webhook handler starts a workflow exactly-once keyed on the event id, then acknowledges immediately. Directly reusable for factoryd's Forgejo webhook intake to make retried deliveries safe.
- DBOSClient.list_workflows plus fork_workflow(id, step): query failed runs by time range and status, then restart a batch from a named step instead of the top. We flagged 'no replay' as a weak spot; this is the shape of the fix.
- Queue rows carry concurrency limit, rate limit, dedup key and priority as plain columns on workflow_status, so flow control is a WHERE clause, not a second service. Worth checking before adding more machinery to our Temporal queue.
- Workflow state as a handful of Postgres tables (workflow_status, workflow_input, workflow_output, operation_outputs) rather than an external orchestrator confirms the value of keeping durable state inside CloudNativePG.
- Conductor instances are stateless because everything lives in Postgres, so any instance can serve any request and several run for availability with no leader election. A pattern for any control-plane service we add.

### Hatchet (1)
- hatchet-dev/pgoutbox (used in pkg/scheduling/v1/pool.go): a transactional outbox reading Postgres logical replication to publish committed state changes to NATS/RabbitMQ without the dual-write race a polling outbox table has. Directly reusable for getting factoryd's Postgres task state onto our own event stream reliably.
- internal/cel wraps Google's cel-go and is used to match durable event waits (frontend/docs/content/docs/v1/durable-event-waits.mdx) against a payload, e.g. this approval event is for task X. A ready vocabulary for our own gate conditions instead of hand-rolled YAML predicates.
- The DurableContext event-wait pattern: a task blocks on an event key plus a CEL match, and on worker crash it replays from the durable event log instead of re-running the steps (including any human approval) that already completed. Close to the approval-is-a-workflow-state primitive we want inside our own Temporal workflow.
- Payload-visibility is a single per-member or per-user-group boolean (docs/v1/user-roles.mdx): VIEWER/MEMBER roles can be shown run status without task input/output payloads, while OWNER/ADMIN and API tokens always see them. Cheap pattern for our console's own can-this-viewer-see-raw-agent-output question.
- Hatchet Lite (build/package/lite.dockerfile) bundles engine, API and migrations in one image for small deployments. A concrete reference shape if we ever want a single-binary story for a from-scratch factoryd re-seed, beyond what we build today.

### Inngest (1)
- Declarative per-function flow control (concurrency key + limit, throttle, debounce, batch, rate limit) sits on the function definition, not hand-rolled in workflow code. pkg/execution/queue implements this as a Redis-backed, multi-tenant fair queue with constraint-aware leasing (constraints.go, backlog.go). Worth stealing the declarative shape for any per-task or per-project concurrency limit we express in policy YAML, even staying on Temporal.
- cancelOn and waitForEvent take a CEL-like match expression against the live event stream, so a running function is cancelled or resumed the moment a matching event arrives, no polling. Directly applicable to auto-cancelling a task workflow when the originating issue closes or a newer commit supersedes it.
- The Connect protocol (pkg/connect, docs/plans/003-connect-gateway-lifecycle-controller.org) lets a worker open a long-lived outbound connection to the server instead of the server making inbound calls to the worker. Same shape as our default-deny-inbound, worker-initiates posture; worth comparing against how factory-gateway holds worker connections.
- End-to-end payload encryption middleware (event.data.encrypted, encrypted step/function output) means the platform itself never sees plaintext task payloads. A pattern to consider for any findings or TaskBrief content that transits a shared control plane we do not fully trust.
- The dev server (inngest-cli dev) bundles every service in one process with in-memory Redis and SQLite for a true zero-dependency local loop, npx inngest-cli@latest dev to a working dashboard in one command. A good bar to hold our own local-dev story to.

### Restate (1)
- durable promises and awakeables as the shape for a human-approval gate: an external actor resolves a specific promise ID with one HTTP call, no polling needed on either side
- an embedded, per-invocation journal inspector plus automatic OpenTelemetry tracing, shipped as part of the server itself, is exactly the 'no replay or trace UI' gap our own weak-points list names
- the documented guarantee that an x.y to x.(y+1) upgrade needs no manual data migration is a concrete bar for how we should describe our own Temporal and Postgres upgrade path
- keyed single-writer state ('virtual objects') as a clean primitive name if we ever need per-entity serialised state outside the per-task workflow, instead of inventing our own locking convention

### Temporal (1)
- Worker Versioning (GA at Replay 2026) replaces our hand-rolled workflow.GetVersion markers. Pinned workflows freeze to the Worker Deployment Version that started them; Auto-Upgrade workflows move to the Current or Ramping Version automatically. Temporal's own Kubernetes/GitHub Actions posts describe a Worker Controller that derives Build IDs from image digests, ramps traffic, and treats a return to a known-good version as an instant rollback.
- Workflow Streams (Public Preview), durable streaming built on the existing Signal and Update primitives, is a first-party fix for the console gap we already flag ("no real-time streams of the agent's work"). It could carry session progress to the console instead of a bespoke side channel.
- Standalone Activities (Public Preview for Go, Python, .NET) run outside any workflow and later slot into one without a rewrite. A fit for one-off infra actions (build VM commissioning, runner pod spin-up) we currently hand-roll retry logic for.
- External Payload Storage (Public Preview, Python/Go, S3 driver out of the box) is Temporal's own claim-check implementation. It validates the split we already do by hand (full session history to object storage, compact restart state to Postgres) and could replace the hand-rolled half of it.
- {'Nexus (GA for the Python SDK, Public Preview for TypeScript/.NET) is the pattern to reach for if factoryd ever splits into more than one Temporal namespace, for example one per project': 'a typed, cancellable, durable async RPC across namespace boundaries instead of ad hoc cross-namespace activity code.'}
- The AI cookbook's guardrail recipe (deterministic hard rules layered over an LLM verdict, with an auditable override) is close in shape to our own policy-gate-then-model-review pairing and is worth reading as a second implementation to check ours against.
- activity_as_tool's rule of thumb, "anything that does I/O is an Activity, everything else runs in-process," is a clean litmus test for our own harness and gateway-tool code even without touching the SDK it comes from.

### codebase-memory-mcp & Sourcebot (1)
- Zero-network single binary: bake codebase-memory-mcp into the agent worker image at build time so the sandboxed pod never needs an egress rule for it, matches our default-deny network policy for free
- CBM's opt-in path fence and size caps (CBM_ALLOWED_ROOT, index_max_files, index_max_source_mb in docs/INDEX_RESOURCE_LIMITS.md) are a cheap fail-closed pattern worth copying and turning on by default for any local agent tool
- Sourcebot's plain public REST API (/api/search, /api/repos, /api/tree, /api/source, /api/commits, /api/diff in docs/api-reference/sourcebot-public.openapi.json) carries no licence gate. A thin MCP shim in factory-gateway could call it directly and skip Sourcebot's own paywalled MCP transport and its per-seat Pro requirement
- CBM's detect_changes tool (git diff to affected symbols plus a blast radius with a risk class) is close in spirit to the path risk classes our verification plane already computes by hand, worth reading as a candidate signal
- Sourcebot's sizing guide (docs/docs/deployment/sizing-guide.mdx) gives a ready repo-count to CPU, RAM and disk table plus named concurrency settings (maxRepoIndexingJobConcurrency, maxConnectionSyncJobConcurrency), reusable if we ever run a shared index

### Ralph (the Ralph Wiggum technique) (1)
- The machine-checkable stop sentinel: the agent prints the literal string <promise>COMPLETE</promise>, ralph.sh greps stdout for it to end the loop. A one-line, robust completion signal worth reusing anywhere a supervisor needs a deterministic 'this session is truly done' check.
- The prd skill's right-sized-story rule, written as a 2-3 sentence test with worked right-sized versus too-big examples (add a column and migration versus refactor the API). Better-phrased task-scoping guidance than most TaskBrief authoring advice, worth stealing the wording.
- AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md as a living, agent-maintained lessons file read at the start of every session and updated at the end of each one. Crude (one file, no dedup, no expiry) but a real, working answer to the 'no project memory' gap in our own weak-spot list.
- Auto-archiving the previous run's state (prd.json, progress.txt) into an archive folder keyed by date and feature name whenever the tracked branch name changes, so a new feature never silently overwrites the last one's history.
- Explicit 'backpressure' framing: treat type checks, tests, static analysis and security scanners as the thing that makes fast generation safe, and size the checks to the language (fast for dynamic languages, accept slow correctness for compiled ones). A useful vocabulary for our own verification-plane tradeoffs, even though Ralph's checks are self-run and self-graded.

### Dapr Agents (1)
- The Hooks vocabulary (Deny, RequireApproval, Mutate, Skip, Proceed) in dapr_agents/hooks.py is a small, closed decision set for gating a tool or LLM call; worth copying the shape for our own before/after Harness hooks.
- AgentApprovalConfig.default_timeout_seconds auto-denies a stuck human approval instead of hanging forever, a good default for our own approval-as-workflow-state wait.
- MCPServer auto-discovery (agent queries the Dapr sidecar's metadata API for loaded MCPServer resources and lists tools with zero tools= wiring) removes per-agent boilerplate we still hand-write.
- The per-agent GET/POST /hitl/approvals REST pair (dapr_agents/workflow/runners/agent.py, _mount_hitl_routes) is a minimal fallback approval surface worth having even where a console exists, for scripts and CI.
- dapr_agents/observability/wrappers/* wraps every workflow, activity, tool and LLM call in an OTel span; a ready checklist of what to instrument in our own harness.

### LiteLLM (1)
- litellm/integrations/prometheus.py and opentelemetry.py are free OSS callbacks. They could push per-call LLM traces straight into SigNoz instead of us only parsing x-litellm-response-cost and x-litellm-model-name off response headers.
- The custom_pricing config lets an operator declare a price for a model LiteLLM cannot price itself. Our PriceBook.Price currently just refuses (LiteLLM has no model) for anything unpriced. A custom entry would turn that refusal into a usable price the day we add a self-hosted or uncommon model.
- The built-in /health endpoint pings every configured deployment credential. Cheap to wire into our own alerting to catch a rotated or expired provider key before a task fails mid-session.
- security.md three-tier bug bounty triage (P0 supply chain, P1 unauthenticated access, P2 authenticated privilege escalation) plus a mandatory exploit video is a lightweight template for our own disclosure process once outside reports are worth inviting.
- Cosign-signed images verified against a pinned commit cosign.pub is a supply-chain check we do not do for our own factoryd, factory-gateway and agent-worker images yet.

### Portkey AI Gateway (1)
- Stateless proxy on Hono: the whole gateway is one Hono app (src/index.ts) that runs unmodified on Node, Docker, or Cloudflare Workers, with no database in the open-source core. Worth copying for factory-gateway if we ever want an edge-deployable variant.
- Config-driven strategy tree resolved per request: tryTargetsRecursively (src/handlers/handlerUtils.ts) walks a JSON target graph with modes single/loadbalance/fallback/conditional (schema in src/middlewares/requestValidator/schema/config.ts), merging retry, cache and guardrail settings down the tree. Cleaner than hardcoding our coding-cheap to coding-frontier ladder in Go if we ever want operators to edit the ladder without a redeploy.
- Guardrail hooks as small addressable plugins: HooksManager and HookSpan (src/middlewares/hooks/index.ts) run named checks before and after the provider call; each guardrail vendor is a plugin folder with its own manifest.json under plugins/ (22 of them: aporia, pangea, patronus, promptfoo, walledai and others). A reasonable shape for wiring extra automated checks into LiteLLM without a bespoke integration each time.
- conf.json's admin_token plus a 12-hour session cookie (src/middlewares/adminAuth/index.ts) is a minimal, dependency-free way to gate a local debug console. Smaller than anything we would build for a throwaway dev view.
- npx @portkey-ai/gateway to a running proxy with a local log console in under two minutes is a genuinely fast zero-to-first-result path. Our own onboarding gap (noted as a weak spot in 00-our-vision.md) is exactly this kind of local, no-cloud-dependency quickstart.

### Claude Squad (1)
- Pause/Resume: dropping the git worktree but keeping the branch to park an idle task cheaply, then rebuilding the worktree from the branch on Resume. A candidate for how our worker plane parks a task that is waiting on a human, instead of tearing the whole pod down and starting cold.
- Graceful handling of a dead session: session/instance.go Start() treats a missing tmux session as pause this one instance, not a fatal error, so one crashed session never blanks the whole instance list on load. Same shape as a task-list reconciler that should skip, not abort, on one bad row.
- Orphaned-worktree recovery: Pause() checks IsValidWorktree() first and takes a separate cleanup path (skip dirty check, force-remove directory, prune metadata) when the worktree's directory or .git is already gone, rather than erroring out.
- Profiles: a short list of name/program pairs in config.json to switch which CLI (claude, codex, aider, gemini) a new session launches, with a picker in the creation overlay when more than one is defined.
- Program-specific trust-prompt auto-dismiss for claude, aider and gemini, so a new session can start unattended without reaching for a blanket skip-all-permissions flag.

### Conductor (1)
- {'Multi-model mode': 'fire the same prompt at Claude Code and Codex in parallel tabs on the same workspace, then pick a branch. A ready-made pattern to A/B our model-tier ladder or a harness swap, not just trust the fixed order.'}
- Checkpoints, an automatic snapshot per agent turn with one-click rollback, layered on top of git history. Cheaper than a diff view to build, and it plugs the "no diff view" gap our own vision page lists as unfinished.
- {'The Checks tab': 'one pane per workspace merging git status, PR state, CI and status checks, review comments and open todos. A concrete shape for a task detail page richer than a kanban card.'}
- {'Routines': "a saved prompt tied to a webhook URL, with GitHub trigger filters by label, author and base branch, and cron presets (hourly, daily, weekly, weekdays). Worth stealing as UX vocabulary for factoryd's own trigger definitions, even though the auth model (bearer token embedded in the URL, no scoping) is one we would not copy."}
- Spotlight testing, syncing a worktree's changes back into the main checkout to run against untracked files like .env or node_modules. A direct answer to the bootstrap problem every worktree-per-task design hits.

### Crystal (1)
- Session list with tabs per session for diff, run output and terminal, backed by an execution_diffs table so diffs are stored, not recomputed. Fixes exactly the gap our own vision names, no diff view in the console.
- Per-tool-call permission bridge (mcpPermissionServer.ts, permissionManager.ts), every tool call the CLI agent wants to make is intercepted, shown to the human with the raw input, and can be approved, denied, or approved with an edited input, before it runs.
- cliToolRegistry.ts, one interface (capabilities, config schema, panel types, availability check) behind which Claude Code, Codex and other CLI agents are plugged in. Same shape as our Harness interface, worth a field by field diff.
- worktreeNameGenerator.ts, auto-names each session's branch and worktree so a user never has to invent one, small but removes friction at the one step users do most often.
- Per-project run script (runCommandManager.ts, scriptExecutionTracker.ts) a user fires manually against any worktree, a cheap local smoke test before anything reaches real CI.

### Google Antigravity (1)
- The Agent Manager: a fleet-level Inbox separate from the editor window, agents grouped into Projects, scheduled recurring messages for automation. A concrete shape for the cross-task, real-time view our console admits it lacks.
- Artifacts as the review surface: every task produces a Task List, Implementation Plan, Code Diff and Walkthrough that a human comments on Google-Docs style, and the agent resumes from the comment without a full stop. Directly answers our console's missing diff view.
- Browser-driven empirical verification: the agent starts the app from a terminal, drives a real Chrome instance (navigate, click, screenshot, record video), and bundles the recording as a Media Artifact plus a Walkthrough Artifact describing the sequence. We have no equivalent: our verification is CI plus model review, never 'did the page actually render'.
- Per-project scoped security settings: a trusted project can run a looser permission preset, an untrusted one a stricter preset, set independently per Project rather than once globally. Worth comparing against our single flat policy YAML.
- The Terminal Sandbox's dynamic allowlist: paths granted under read_file/write_file populate a live filesystem allowlist, domains granted under read_url populate the network policy. Same shape as our capability-to-allowlist mapping, worth checking our gateway's egress generation against.
- A simple three-position review-cadence knob at project setup (Agent-driven, Review-driven, Agent-assisted) for how often the agent stops to ask. Cheap UX primitive our console's per-project settings could copy.

### Happy (1)
- the hasActiveUiClient presence check, which suppresses a push when a desktop or web client already shows the same session, plus an explicit sent/partial/suppressed/no_tokens/failed outcome on every push so a silent push outage cannot hide for months again
- the QR-pairing key exchange, an ephemeral NaCl box keypair drawn on the terminal as a QR code, phone encrypts its reply to that public key, so the relay carrying the handshake never sees the resulting shared secret
- ship a single-binary, embedded-database self-host path ("happy server" over PGlite) as a first-class alternative to the hosted service, with an explicit "fail closed, no silent fallback to the hosted relay" rule if self-host is misconfigured
- wrap the agent process with Anthropic's own "@anthropic-ai/sandbox-runtime" package for OS-level read/write path and network-domain restriction, instead of writing a bespoke sandboxer
- a named decision enum on every approval (approved, approved_for_session, denied, abort) instead of a bare yes/no, so "allow this tool for the rest of the session" is a first-class state, not a client-side hack
- one shared, versioned wire-protocol schema package (Zod) imported by the CLI, the server and the app, so a client and a harness cannot silently drift out of sync

### Orca (1)
- The plain-text diff-comment wire format (formatDiffComment/formatDiffComments in src/shared/diff-comments-format.ts): file path, line number and quoted comment body batched into one prompt block. Worth copying the format even though we would deliver it differently.
- Guarded prompt delivery with a pre- and post-send readiness check (sendPromptWithGuardedPasteAndEnter in src/renderer/src/lib/active-agent-note-send-delivery.ts): paste, wait, re-check the target is still ready, then send Enter as a separate step. A useful pattern anywhere we inject text into a process we do not fully control.
- --precheck on scheduled automations (docs/site/content/docs/cli/automations.mdx): skip a scheduled run when a cheap shell probe exits non-zero, and record it as a skipped run rather than a failure. Cheap to add to our own scheduled jobs.
- The mandatory PR-template field in .github/CONTRIBUTING.md forcing every PR (including AI-authored ones) to carry a short security and cross-platform review summary. Low-cost nudge worth requiring on our own bot-authored PRs.
- Anonymous, opt-out product telemetry that explicitly excludes file, prompt and terminal content (docs/site/content/docs/telemetry.mdx). A reasonable default if we ever add usage analytics to the console.
- The documented 'race N agents' recipe (docs/site/content/docs/recipes/parallel-agents.mdx) as a piece of user-facing documentation, not code: worth writing our own short doc on running the same TaskBrief twice when a task is ambiguous, even without new engineering.

### Arize Phoenix (1)
- A maintained per-model dollar-per-token pricing table as a plain JSON manifest (src/phoenix/server/cost_tracking/model_cost_manifest.json) matched by regex specificity (regex_specificity.py) instead of hand-coded price constants; factory-gateway's cost model could use the same shape.
- A pluggable sandbox backend interface (src/phoenix/server/sandbox/types.py, SandboxBackend ABC) with one session manager handling idle TTL eviction, refcounts and per-config session fingerprinting (session_manager.py) across seven providers (Daytona, E2B, Modal, Deno, Vercel, WASM, an in-process 'Monty' runtime). Same shape as our Harness interface, applied to sandboxes instead of harnesses.
- Multi-variant release images per version: root, nonroot and debug Docker tags built from one Dockerfile. Worth copying for the agent worker image: a debug variant with a shell for a human investigating a stuck sandbox, without weakening the hardened default.
- A single boolean flip from single-user to multi-user: PHOENIX_ENABLE_AUTH plus PHOENIX_SECRET turns on OAuth2, LDAP or local accounts and role-based permissions; off by default for a local laptop run. Clean default-closed-when-it-matters pattern.
- A built-in OTLP receiver inside the same process that serves the UI (pyproject.toml lists opentelemetry-exporter-otlp and grpcio) so sending traces needs no separate collector. Worth weighing against a second SigNoz-adjacent path for agent-session-scoped, developer-facing trace debugging.
- A Playground that replays a single traced model call with an edited prompt, model or parameters, and an experiment comparison table that diffs runs side by side. Direct answer to our own listed gap: no per-task trace or diff view in the console.

### Braintrust (1)
- eval-action (GitHub Action): runs evals on push/PR and posts a scored PR comment, with terminate_on_failure to block merge. Same shape as our reproduction gate, but for LLM-output quality drift instead of test pass/fail.
- autoevals (MIT, 1041 stars, Python and JS): a ready library of scorers (Levenshtein, factuality, JSON diff, moderation, closed-QA). Use it directly in our stronger-model review gate instead of hand-rolling scorers.
- Trace to dataset in one click turns a production trace into a versioned regression case. Directly answers our own stated gap of no regression suite for the harness prompt.
- The Temporal tracing plugin attaches spans to workflow and activity boundaries. Since factoryd runs one Temporal workflow per task, this is a concrete pattern for what to instrument in our own SigNoz traces: workflow start, each activity, each retry.
- MCP server exposing logs, evals and prompts to an IDE agent. Confirms that giving a coding agent read access to its own prior run history via a capability-scoped tool call is worth adding once we have project memory.

### Future AGI (1)
- Virtual API keys as a first-class object (agentcc-gateway/internal/auth/keystore.go): each key carries its own model/provider/IP/tool allow-lists, RPM/TPM limits and a microdollar credit balance, so a caller gets a scoped, revocable identity instead of a raw provider key. This is close to what identity-per-task should look like at the gateway edge.
- CONTRIBUTION_POLICY.md forces an explicit AI-disclosure line on every PR ('AI use: none / assisted / generated') and states unattended-agent-authored PRs are closed on sight unless disclosed and reviewed by a human. A cheap, enforceable norm for a project that itself uses agents to build itself.
- Committed .claude/skills/ and .agents/skills/ directories (writing-e2e-flows, reviewing-prs) ship the project's own working conventions as files agents read, not tribal knowledge. Same idea we use, worth comparing our skill set against theirs for gaps.
- nsjail-based short-lived code sandbox (futureagi/code-executor/server.py) is a much lighter mechanism than a full per-task pod for narrow, bounded jobs (one Python/JS function, one evaluation): rlimit_as, rlimit_cpu, rlimit_fsize, time_limit, read-only root bind, tmpfs /tmp. Worth having as a cheap option for short evaluator-style tasks that do not need a whole gVisor pod.
- A public roadmap plus GitHub Discussions roadmap category gives outside users a visible backlog and a low-cost way to signal demand, which doubles as portfolio evidence of an active project.
- SECURITY.md pins a HackerOne bounty, a PGP key and a response-time table by severity, and explicitly lists agentcc-gateway/ as in scope. A short, concrete template for our own security policy page.

### Harbor (1)
- The per-phase typed NetworkPolicy model (no-network, public, allowlist, with typed hostname, wildcard-hostname, IPv4/IPv6 address and CIDR entries) applied separately to environment setup, agent run and verifier phases, with fail-closed validation that rejects a trial at submission time if the chosen sandbox provider cannot support the requested policy.
- The capabilities object pattern. BaseAgent and BaseEnvironment each declare a typed capabilities class (AgentCapabilities, EnvironmentCapabilities) the harness checks before scheduling, replacing brittle boolean SUPPORTS_* flags.
- The dataset-registry-as-pinned-git-commit-manifest pattern (registry.json, 80 datasets, each a task list pointing at a pinned commit in an external repo) to keep a task corpus out of the harness repo and every run reproducible.
- The viewer's compare waffle grid (task by agent/model, cell colour is pass or fail) as a working template for the per-model-tier pass-rate dashboard our own design currently lacks.
- The parity CI pattern, a workflow triggered on adapter config changes that reruns a task set and auto-commits a pass-rate-vs-baseline CSV, as the shape for a harness-prompt regression suite.
- The LLM-judged task-quality rubric (behavior_in_task_description, anti_cheating_measures, hardcoded_solution, tests_or_solution_in_image, and more) run once against a task definition to catch trivial or leaky regression tasks before they are trusted.

### Helicone (1)
- Helicone-RateLimit-Policy header: quota;w=window;u=request|cents;s=user|property, an IETF-shaped, provider-agnostic way to express per-user or per-cost ceilings at the gateway edge.
- packages/cost is an open, file-based registry (providers/mappings.ts, models/calculate-cost.ts) covering 300+ models and providers. Worth vendoring or diffing against instead of hand-rolling our own price table for the cost ledger.
- Sessions: three headers (Helicone-Session-Id/-Path/-Name) build a parent/child call tree across LLM, tool and vector-db calls with no tracing SDK. Cheap way to give the console a per-task call tree before a real OTel trace view exists.
- Provider routing string syntax (model/provider, comma fallback chains, ! to exclude a provider) is a compact way to express a routing policy inline, without a config file.
- Two-tier prompt-injection screening: a small model (Prompt Guard, 86M) gates every call, a bigger model (Llama Guard) runs only on suspicion. Same cheap-detector-then-strong-model shape as our supervisor ladder, one more data point that the pattern generalises.

### Laminar (1)
- Compress a trace to roughly 10% of its tokens before any expensive step (dedupe repeated system prompts, move the user request to the front, keep LLM turns and tool calls, drop plumbing spans unless they error) — cuts the cost of any stronger-model read of a session, directly applicable to our supervisor calls.
- Signals — a user writes a plain-English rule plus a JSON output schema, a trigger point and trace-level filters; an agent with tools reads the compressed trace, can pull any span's full uncompressed content by id, and emits typed findings with a severity that route to Slack or email. Close to a shipped version of our own supervisor plus "alerts with verdicts" idea; read the mechanism before building ours.
- Per-span dollar cost rendered inline next to each LLM call in the trace view, not just an aggregate — a cheap, high-value console default for showing an agent's live spend per step.
- app-server/src/features/mod.rs — optional integrations (RabbitMQ, a ClickHouse read replica, Sentry) are enabled purely by whether their env vars or credentials are present, never by a separate on/off flag that can drift from what is actually deployed.
- One SQL layer ("query_engine") fronts every stored entity, traces, spans, signal events, evaluations, and is exposed identically through the UI SQL editor, the CLI, the MCP server and the HTTP API, so a coding agent and a human use the same read path.
- Agent debugger — set a checkpoint on any span, then rerun the trace from that point reusing cached upstream outputs, so a config or prompt tweak can be tested without repeating a whole multi-step run.

### Langfuse (1)
- The in-app agent's 'one log, three derivations' rule (web/src/features/in-app-agent/ARCHITECTURE.md): one durable event table (in_app_agent_events, ordered by sequence_number) is the only source of truth; canonical, display and replay views are all folded from it at read time, never stored separately. Directly applicable to our own task event stream and the diff/replay view our console lacks.
- SSE tail-watching over a persisted cursor (app/api/in-app-agent/watch): the browser hydrates one snapshot, then tails new events above its cursor; closing the drawer detaches without cancelling the run, reopening re-hydrates and resumes the tail. This is exactly the 'real-time stream of the agent's work' our own weak-spots list says the console is missing.
- Durable approval interrupts as first-class events in the same log (packages/shared/src/in-app-agent/interrupts.ts) rather than a side table for human-in-the-loop state. A clean shape for our own approval workflow state next to a task's event stream.
- LLM-as-judge and code-evaluator pipelines with datasets and dataset-run-items (web/src/features/evals, features/datasets) are a ready-made pattern for scoring model output against criteria at scale. Reusable almost as-is for the harness-prompt regression suite and per-model-tier success benchmark we say we do not have.
- The Rust ai-gateway's pass-through design (ai-gateway/README.md): native /openai/v1 and /anthropic/v1 namespaces relay provider bytes unmodified while still capturing full generation traces via OTLP. A lighter alternative to LiteLLM's normalise-everything approach worth weighing whenever exact provider wire semantics matter more than a unified schema.
- Automations turn a metric or score threshold into a Slack message, generic webhook or GitHub repository_dispatch call out of the box (web/src/features/automations/githubDispatchUrl.ts). A small, concrete pattern for wiring a telemetry alert to an external action without hand-rolling the dispatch code.

### LangSmith (1)
- Trajectory vs. Details tabs on one trace: Trajectory shows the conversation exactly as sent to the model, Details shows the full nested run tree (outer span, tool span, LLM span with exact prompt and response). A direct template for our own listed gap, no per-task trace or replay view in the console.
- process_inputs / process_outputs hooks on the traceable decorator (python/langsmith/run_helpers.py) let a caller scrub a payload client-side before it ever leaves the process, before the PII/secrets redaction the LLM Gateway does later at the wire. Worth two layers, not one, if we ever pipe agent transcripts anywhere outside the cluster.
- LLM Gateway spend caps at four levels (org, workspace, user, API key) return a plain 402 with a clear error when a cap is hit, plus real-time cost rollups per level. A concrete convention to check factory-gateway's own budget responses against.
- Insights: automatic clustering of traces into recurring usage and failure patterns, plus generated error-analysis summaries. A mechanism worth adding on top of our own SigNoz traces for the console's alerts and task views, instead of a human reading raw traces one at a time.
- Prompt Hub versioning: every push gets a content-addressed commit hash tying an exact prompt, its variables and model config to every trace produced with it. Directly answers our own listed weakness of no regression suite for the harness prompt: pin a hash, diff outcomes across hashes.
- SmithDB's stated shape (three stateless components over object storage and Postgres, no local disks, no sharding) is a clean pattern for a trace store if SigNoz/ClickHouse ever becomes the bottleneck; not something to copy today since SigNoz already covers this for us.

### Opik (1)
- The `uvx opik mcp configure` MCP server lets Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot, Codex or opencode read traces, score outputs and run evaluations from chat. We have no way for a supervisor or reviewer to query past session traces at all; an MCP tool like this over our own SigNoz traces would close that gap cheaply.
- The `@track` decorator (Python and TypeScript SDKs) turns any function into a full nested trace tree with one line, no manual span plumbing. Our "no replay or trace UI" weakness could shrink a lot just by wrapping the harness call sites the same way, even before building a console view.
- The PyTest integration runs LLM-as-judge metrics as ordinary test assertions, so an eval regression fails CI like any other test. That is a ready pattern for a fourth, cheap verification signal ahead of stronger-model review in our own gate.
- Online Evaluation Rules score a sample of production traffic continuously with LLM-as-judge metrics and surface drift on a dashboard. It is the same shape as our "alerts with verdicts" mechanism, aimed at model quality instead of infra, and could feed the same alert stream.
- IsolatedSubprocessExecutor (apps/opik-python-backend/src/opik_backend/executor_isolated.py) gives each custom scoring script a fresh subprocess with scoped env vars, a 20MB stack cap and a timeout, cheap protection for the many places we run small model-authored snippets outside the main sandbox path (report generation, config validation) where a full gVisor pod is overkill.

### Cedar (1)
- The permit and forbid, deny by default, forbid wins evaluation model as a clean way to document what our YAML plus Go check already tries to do.
- cedar-go, a pure Go authorizer, as the concrete shape a "beyond plain Go code" upgrade could take, cheaper than a Rust FFI or a sidecar process.
- {'Schema first validation ("cedar validate") as a pattern': 'a typed check on the policy document at commit time, not only at gateway enforcement time.'}
- The action naming convention Bedrock AgentCore Policy uses for MCP tool calls, a Gateway target and tool name pair as the action, the Gateway itself as the resource.
- cedar-policy-symcc's automated reasoning, equivalence and counterexample search over a policy set, as a worked example of what "prove the security model holds" costs and buys.

### Composio (1)
- The meta-tool / tool-router pattern (python/composio/core/models/tool_router_session.py): an agent gets a small fixed set of meta tools (search, authorize, execute) instead of hundreds of tool schemas up front, and the router resolves the real tool at call time. Worth checking against our own factory-gateway if its tool surface ever grows past a handful of first-party tools.
- Reusable, persistent per-user connected accounts (ConnectedAccounts.ts, connected_accounts.py): a user authorises once and every later session reuses the stored connection. A pattern to weigh only for a future long-lived per-project integration, never for our per-task credentials, which are deliberately not reused.
- Treating an untrusted API response field as attacker-controlled before using it to build a network destination: triggers.py validates the Pusher `cluster` value against `_PUSHER_CLUSTER_PATTERN` (`^[a-z0-9-]+$`, max 64 chars) before it is formatted into a websocket host, with a comment noting the field arrives from an untrusted response. A concrete habit worth grepping our own gateway code for missing equivalents.
- A hard, legible free-tier cap with no silent overage (Hobby plan stops at 100K tool calls a month until upgrade) is a simple model worth comparing to our own hold-then-settle ledger for the tone of our own budget messaging.
- "Self-managed credentials", where a caller passes its own token at execution time and Composio never stores it, is offered as an explicit lower-trust mode on their own platform. It is a tacit admission from a company whose whole product is storing other people's OAuth tokens that not storing them is the safer default, the default our design already takes.

### Cycles (1)
- reserve.lua checks every affected scope in the tenant hierarchy and mutates all of them in one atomic Redis script, so two concurrent reservations can never jointly overdraw shared headroom without any explicit lock code
- three named overage policies (REJECT, ALLOW_IF_AVAILABLE, ALLOW_WITH_OVERDRAFT) set per reservation or per tenant default, with an explicit debt ledger and an is_over_limit circuit breaker, instead of one hardwired "book it afterwards"
- mandatory idempotency_key on every mutating call, with a documented per-operation TTL table and a distinct 409 IDEMPOTENCY_MISMATCH vs transparent replay of the original response
- {'fail-closed on ledger-store outage': 'reserve fails rather than silently allowing when Redis is unreachable'}
- dry_run flag on the reserve/decide path for shadow-testing a new budget policy against live traffic before it can deny anything
- CyclesEvidence, an Ed25519-signed, content-addressed audit envelope per decision, fetchable from a stable public capability URL and verifiable offline via a JWKS endpoint, independent of live ledger access
- calendar-bucketed rolling windows (per_minute_tumbling, per_hour_tumbling, per_day_tumbling) for action-count quotas, implemented as plain Redis keys with natural TTL expiry, no external cron needed

### Invariant (Guardrails, Explorer, Gateway) (1)
- The flow-matching DSL operators -> (eventually) and ~> (immediately next) over a trace of Message/ToolCall/ToolOutput events, for rules like "no send_email after get_inbox" (invariant/analyzer/language).
- {'The ready-made detector catalogue as a checklist for our own untrusted-input handling': 'PII via Presidio, secrets, a prompt-injection classifier, a semgrep-based code check, moderation (invariant/analyzer/stdlib/invariant/detectors/).'}
- {"Monitor's de-duplicating incremental check": "an error fires once per trace location even if the condition persists (invariant/analyzer/monitor.py), a pattern worth copying into our supervisor's cheap detectors."}
- Enforcing behavioural policy at the LLM/MCP proxy choke point, blocking before a call reaches the provider or before a tool result reaches the agent, as a hook alongside factory-gateway's existing pricing and authorization checks.
- Proof that a minimal, Python-like external policy DSL is enough; it does not need to be a general-purpose language, just pattern matching over a typed event trace.

### iron-proxy (1)
- dnsguard's dial-time IP-CIDR recheck (net.Dialer.Control after DNS resolution) closes the SSRF and DNS-rebinding gap on any allowlisted hostname, defaults deny cloud metadata IPs and loopback
- resolver TTL plus a separate failure_ttl for cached secrets, so a transient secret-backend outage falls back fast instead of being pinned behind a long positive cache
- the judge transform's shape for ever letting a model touch a capability decision: deny-only, fail-closed on error or timeout, circuit breaker on consecutive failures, semaphore-capped concurrency, one audited verdict per call
- MCP tool policy at argument level, not just tool level: dotted-path equals/in/matches conditions on tools/call arguments, plus tools/list and per-SSE-event filtering so a denied tool never even appears
- a checked-in egress-rules.yaml enforced against their own CI runner by a GitHub Action, with a build-time summary of blocked and allowed calls, applied to the tool's own pipeline, not just downstream users

### Agent Governance Toolkit (1)
- Action-bound approval protocol (ADR-0030): an action_digest is a SHA-256 hash of the RFC 8785 JCS canonical serialisation of the exact tool call, and it is re-verified immediately before execution, so an approval silently stops covering a call whose parameters changed after the human clicked approve. Worth copying into our own approval-gate schema.
- Reserved reason-code namespace: policy authors cannot emit a reason string starting with runtime_error:, so a genuine enforcement failure can never be spoofed as a policy verdict. A one-line convention worth adding to our gateway's decision schema.
- Named, numbered intervention points (agent_startup, input, pre_model_call, post_model_call, pre_tool_call, post_tool_call, output, agent_shutdown) give a clean shared vocabulary for documenting where a decision is made, even though we enforce at the network edge, not in-process.
- OpenTelemetry metric and event naming (acs_intervention_{allow,deny,warn,escalate,transform}_total counters, acs_intervention_duration_ms histogram, a fixed decision/annotator_dispatch/policy_evaluation/policy_failed event set with a documented never carries raw tool arguments contract) is a ready-made naming scheme for our gateway's own per-decision telemetry.
- docs/LIMITATIONS.md is a dated, versioned, first-class file listing each known gap with a plain-language example, today's mitigation and the roadmap fix, and it names external critiques by source. Our own Where we know we are weak section in 00-our-vision.md would read sharper in this format.
- The paired policy/lib/egress.rego and policy/cedar-lib/egress.cedar for the same rule, with the Cedar file's comments spelling out exactly what Cedar cannot express (no URL parser, no glob, no substring match) and how the host must pre-compute those into annotations, is a good reference for how to degrade gracefully if we ever add a second policy backend.

### Anthropic Sandbox Runtime (1)
- {'Credential sentinel substitution': 'mask a secret file or env var from the sandboxed process with a fake value, then have the egress proxy swap the real bytes back into outbound headers when it sees the sentinel, so a tool that reads the credential path still works without the agent ever holding the real secret.'}
- {'Resolved-address check': "after a hostname passes the domain allowlist, resolve it once and refuse to dial if the address is loopback, link-local, cloud metadata, or one of the host's own interfaces, closing the DNS-rebinding hole a name-only allowlist leaves open."}
- {'Mandatory deny paths and pinned directories': 'shell rc files, .git/hooks, .gitconfig and IDE config dirs are always write-blocked regardless of the allow list, and every ancestor of a protected path is bind-mounted so it cannot be renamed or deleted out from under the deny.'}
- control-fd: push new network allow/deny lists into a running sandbox over a file descriptor the caller already holds, taking effect immediately on the live proxy while filesystem rules stay fixed at wrap time.
- Two-stage seccomp with a nested PID namespace as PID 1, so the syscall-filtered command cannot reach an unfiltered helper process (bwrap's own init, the socat bridge) via ptrace or /proc, keeping the seccomp boundary intact regardless of ptrace_scope.

### Daytona (1)
- The Toolbox daemon (apps/daemon/pkg/toolbox) bundles files, git, process exec, PTY, LSP, computer-use and session recording behind one small in-sandbox Go binary. Close to the scope our own sandbox-side adapter would need if it ever grows past model/tool proxying.
- Three mutually exclusive, live-editable network modes per sandbox: networkAllowList (CIDRs), domainAllowList (hostnames, wildcards allowed), networkBlockAll. A cleaner shape than one static NetworkPolicy if we ever expose per-project egress control to project owners.
- Hostname-based preview routing, {port}-{sandboxId}.{proxy-domain}, is a one-line pattern for handing a human a live URL into a running task's sandbox. Worth remembering when the console grows real-time views.
- Per-second metered pricing split into vCPU, memory, storage and GPU line items is a reasonable reference shape for costing our own commissioned-server tool prices.
- Sub-90ms claimed cold start on plain containers is a latency bar worth keeping in mind for our own gVisor/microVM roadmap, even though it trades away kernel-level isolation to get there.

### E2B (1)
- userfaultfd lazy memory restore + copy-on-write NBD rootfs for pause/resume/fork (e2b-dev/runtime packages/orchestrator/pkg/sandbox/{uffd,rootfs,nbd,block}): the concrete recipe for our own aspirational microVM tier
- secret-marker pattern: store a reference in the control plane, resolve the real value only at point of use, never let it cross an API, a log or a span
- best-of-K node placement with full-jitter backoff on a busy signal (internal/orchestrator/placement/), a small reusable scheduling heuristic
- per-sandbox nftables egress firewall with SNI/Host-inspecting domain allow and deny lists, finer-grained than a static Cilium allowlist
- Embed's preflight discipline: two pinned files, docker compose up, and a failing check prints an actionable FIX: line instead of a stack trace

### Edera (1)
- PV-mode paravirtualisation as working prior art that a per-pod kernel does not require /dev/kvm; worth reading krata's xenclient/xenplatform crates even if we never adopt Edera itself
- am-i-isolated (ghcr.io/edera-dev/am-i-isolated, Rust, 326 stars): a free isolation-posture scanner we can run against our own gVisor sandbox today for an objective score instead of an assumption
- hyperbench: an open benchmark harness comparing hypervisor, interposer and namespace runtimes on raw compute and syscall overhead; run it ourselves against systrap gVisor instead of citing a 2019 USENIX number
- Their agent-sandbox guide's hard lesson: an unset memory limit makes the sandbox default to 1024M and OOM-kill the agent; always set resources.limits.memory on our gVisor pod specs, never rely on an implicit default
- edera-check's preinstall/postinstall/collect CLI pattern: a small readiness-report tool run before and after a node joins the fleet, worth copying as a factoryd node-readiness check
- A single host-level Falco instance covering all zones' eBPF events, instead of one instrumented agent per sandbox; a lighter observability shape than gVisor allows today
- SPIFFE/SPIRE-based per-zone workload identity (their spire-controller-manager fork) is a second working reference point for short-lived, workload-scoped credentials, to compare against our projected ServiceAccount token design

### Fly.io Sprites (1)
- Checkpoint/restore of the sandbox filesystem as an agent-callable tool, distinct from git, with the last few checkpoints mounted read-only for inspection.
- DNS REFUSED fast-fail on a denied egress domain instead of a hanging connect, a small legibility win over a silent network-policy drop.
- Connectors' "test this connection before an agent run" step in the control plane, worth copying for our own gateway-held git and cloud tokens.
- A pre-installed agent skill file that teaches the harness the sandbox's gateway conventions at boot, instead of relying on prompt text in the TaskBrief.
- The storage split (fast local cache in front of durable object storage, metadata kept small and shipped via Litestream) as a reference for a future microVM-tier design.

### Modal Sandboxes (1)
- The three-tier outbound egress model: block_network for a full cutoff, outbound_cidr_allowlist for IP/CIDR ranges, and outbound_domain_allowlist (beta) that matches on TLS SNI without decrypting traffic, the two allowlists additive. Our Cilium egress policy could adopt the same SNI-domain-match shape instead of hand-maintained CIDR lists for third-party API domains.
- Runtime policy hot-swap (alpha): a running sandbox's outbound allowlist can be replaced in place, with now-disallowed connections torn down immediately, no pod restart. A direct fit for our supervisor's redirect action: tighten a task's egress the moment a stall or anomaly detector fires, instead of killing and rescheduling the pod.
- Connect Tokens for tunnel access: an authenticated path to a sandboxed HTTP server that injects an unspoofable X-Verified-User-Data header, cheaper than standing up mTLS or full OIDC for a narrow, short-lived callback from inside a sandbox to a caller.
- Filesystem snapshot to persist and fan out sandbox state past the normal lifetime cap, the same idea as Kubernetes SIG agent-sandbox's golden warm pool but shipped as a documented product primitive. Relevant to our supervisor's retry-on-stronger-model path: resume from exact filesystem state rather than replaying the task from the branch tip.
- Sidecar egress relay (proxy_traffic_via_sidecar) that centralises TLS-port-443 policy and logging for one process without terminating and re-encrypting traffic, an option if we ever want one egress audit point without breaking end-to-end TLS.

### BMad Method (1)
- Step-file architecture. A workflow loads one step file at a time, reads it fully, executes it, then loads the next by absolute path. Keeps the acting model's context to one step instead of the whole workflow, a pattern worth for our long worker prompts.
- Review triage taxonomy in skills/bmad-build/step-04-review.md. Every finding gets exactly one verdict (high, medium, low, false, maybe-false), then routes to intent_gap, bad_plan, patch or defer. Patch findings are auto-fixed in the same session. Defer findings are appended to a standing deferred-work.md instead of being dropped. Close to what our stronger-model review gate should do with its findings.
- Rendered, hashed skill snapshots. The skills/bmad/scripts/render_skill.py script compiles Jinja2 markdown plus a three-layer TOML customization (base, team and user layers, scalars override, tables deep-merge, arrays-of-tables key by id) into an immutable, hashed snapshot under _bmad/. Gives a reproducible, diffable prompt build, a pattern for versioning our own harness prompts and policy overlays.
- Ticket state kept out of the ticket body. A leaf's status, assignee and block reason live in a sidecar <slug>-plan.md joined by a ticket field, so the human-authored ticket text and the agent-authored build state never collide in the same diff. Useful separation for our TaskBrief versus findings.
- Deprecated skill names resolve as forwarders (bmad-create-prd to bmad-prd, etc.) so renames do not break existing callers, a cheap backward-compatibility habit worth copying for our own tool and skill names.
- bmad-retrospective re-runs the review lenses over a whole completed epic's diff as a closing gate, separate from the per-change gate in bmad-code-review. We only verify per task. A closing look at the full arc of a multi-task epic is a gap we do not cover.

### OpenSpec (1)
- The delta-spec format (ADDED Requirements / MODIFIED / REMOVED, each with Given/When/Then scenarios) is a ready template for the "specification with acceptance criteria" intake we do not have yet.
- One canonical artifact schema (schemas/spec-driven/schema.yaml and its templates) rendered into many tool-specific command files is the same trick our Harness interface needs when swapping opencode for Codex CLI.
- openspec validate runs a cheap, deterministic schema check on a spec before any model touches it, a pattern that fits directly ahead of our reproduction gate.
- SECURITY.md's in-scope/out-of-scope threat-model table is a compact, reusable shape for documenting factoryd's own trust boundary.
- The forced pause after /opsx:propose (plan written, human reads it, only then /opsx:apply runs) is a cheap textual approval gate worth adding before an agent starts on a weak issue.
- Stores (planning kept in its own repo, referenced read-only from several code repos) is the shape to copy if factory planning ever needs to span more than one registered project.

### GitHub Spec Kit (1)
- the converge command diffs the codebase against spec, plan and tasks and appends only the delta as new tasks, instead of re-planning from zero
- a three-way bug verdict (verified, partial, failed) recorded as an artifact, separate from the fix step itself
- a constitution.md every planning and implementation prompt is required to re-read before acting
- the download hardening in _download_security.py (byte caps, zip entry caps, path traversal and internal-IP checks) is a template for any artifact fetch path
- 30-plus coding-agent integrations behind one small adapter interface shows a thin per-harness adapter beats baking one harness's syntax into shared templates

### Tessl (1)
- The .spec.md format: YAML frontmatter (name, description, targets: file globs) plus inline [@test] links next to each requirement. A greppable, git-native way to trace a requirement to its implementation and its test, worth copying for the spec-driven intake our own vision names as missing.
- rules/spec-before-code.md spells out what counts as approval (an explicit yes, or a correction plus confirmation) and what does not (silence, "just do it", the agent's own judgement). That exact distinction is reusable almost verbatim for our own approval-as-workflow-state gate, to stop an agent from treating a non-answer as a yes.
- The eval shape: evals/<scenario>/task.md plus criteria.json as a weighted checklist, run locally with tessl eval run . and in CI on every PR (lint, skill review, version-bump check, publish-on-merge). A cheap template for the harness-prompt regression suite our own vision lists as absent.
- work-review's step 4 (capture ALL discovered requirements, not just new features but behavioural differences from the spec) is a good discipline for closing the loop between an implementation and its spec, applicable to our own end-of-session findings record.
- Distribution as a one-line install (tessl install tessl-labs/spec-driven-development) that layers a methodology onto whatever agent is already running (Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot) rather than requiring a new agent or runtime. Worth remembering as the friction floor for any optional methodology add-on to opencode.

## Differs items, by project

### ABCA (Autonomous Background Coding Agents on AWS)
- The whole design is AWS-native on purpose (AgentCore Runtime, Bedrock, Lambda Durable Functions, Cognito, DynamoDB, WAF). "Self-hosted" means your own AWS account, not commodity hardware. Copying this wholesale breaks our no-vendor-lock-in and cheap-hardware constraints outright, it is not a variant of our design, it is the opposite bet.
- There is no web console. Every human surface is a CLI (bgagent) plus chat and ticket integrations (Slack, Linear, Jira); the one dashboard construct in the code is a CloudWatch operator view, not an end-user UI.
- The GitHub credential the agent pushes and opens PRs with is one long-lived PAT per onboarded repository, stored in Secrets Manager and shared by every task on that repo, not a token scoped to the single task, this is the one place their "identity per task" story does not hold.

### Cursor Cloud Agents (Background Agents, Automations, Bugbot)
- The agent process itself holds the real secret and reaches the network directly. Redaction scrubs output after the fact and is not an access boundary. Our gateway keeps key material out of the sandbox and brokers every call through a verified per-task identity.
- Their defaults are allow-first. Network access defaults to an allowlist plus a broad default set, desktop guardrails are documented as best-effort and not a hard security boundary, and a Bugbot or Security Review finding does not block a merge unless an admin turns that on. We default-deny egress and default-block merge until a policy-classified gate passes.
- Every plane, control, billing, model routing, identity, is Cursor closed SaaS, and since 14 August 2026 the whole company is a wholly owned SpaceX subsidiary. None of it can be re-seeded from Git or run on commodity hardware.
- Their spend cap is checked after usage is already recorded, the docs say enforcement is not instant. Our gateway takes a database-locked hold before the call.

### Minions (Stripe)
- Their trust boundary is environmental, a devbox with no path to production or the internet, not a scoped credential. That works because the devbox never needs to act on the outside world beyond git and their internal LLM endpoint. Our tasks must push code upstream and spend metered money per call, so we cannot get away with pure network denial and need the projected per-task token our design already has.
- Their harness is intentionally not interruptible, no steering commands, no confirmation prompts, the run either lands a good PR or a discardable bad one. We spend real engineering on a supervisor loop (stall and repeat detectors, a strongest-model call, redirect or retry) because a wasted attempt costs us a meaningful slice of a hard budget cap that Stripe has not disclosed having.
- Verification stops at deterministic CI plus a mandatory human review of every single PR, there is no automated model-review gate between CI and the reviewer. That is affordable with thousands of engineers absorbing 1300 reviews a week. We built the review-frontier gate and a conditional auto-merge rule specifically because we do not have that reviewer pool.
- Their coordination surface is still chat, the same Slack thread that started the run is where an engineer gives more instructions if the result is wrong. We deliberately removed chat as the exchange mechanism in favour of TaskBrief in, branch and PR out.

### GitLab Duo Agent Platform
- Sandboxing is execution-path-dependent, not universal. Only flows that run as a CI/CD job get the SRT-based (Anthropic Sandbox Runtime) network and filesystem restriction. The same agents running in the IDE, on a developer's machine, or in Duo Chat run with the user's own ambient filesystem and credentials, and GitLab's own docs warn the Software Development Flow can read untracked, gitignored files such as .env. Their control at that point is human review, not a sandbox. Copying this would break our security-without-half-measures constraint, which applies regardless of where an agent runs.
- Nothing here is open source by any normal definition. The Agent Platform's server-side code sits under GitLab's own EE licence (source-visible, but production use requires a paid Premium or Ultimate subscription, and copying, merging or redistributing it is explicitly forbidden), gated further behind GitLab Credits usage billing. Our best-of-breed-open-source-with-an-escape-hatch constraint rules out depending on any of this code; only the documented patterns and the LiteLLM/self-hosted-model conventions are transferable.
- Identity is a long-lived bearer-token chain, not a projected short-lived cluster credential. A request passes through a Cloud Connector JWT, a CustomersDot token, an OAuth access token, a Duo Workflow Service internal JWT and a GLGO exchange JWT, with TTLs from about one hour to roughly three days, held by the executor process. Composite identity is a strong audit and attribution model but the tokens themselves live longer and travel further than the kubelet-rotated, OIDC-verified, per-task ServiceAccount token our design uses, so it buys traceability at the cost of a wider blast radius per credential.

### Claude Code
- Claude Code's policy machinery (settings precedence, the hook and plugin tier system, sandbox filesystem and network rules) protects one developer's machine or one organisation's fleet of individually authenticated seats. Our policy question is different: a task, not a person, holds identity, and enforcement sits in a gateway in front of a disposable Kubernetes sandbox with no persistent settings.json at all. We cannot borrow the settings-precedence stack as infrastructure, only its ratchet idea, re-implemented against our own per-task ServiceAccount and policy-in-Git model.

### Claude Managed Agents
- Their control plane, session state and billing are entirely Anthropic's. Self-hosted sandboxes move only tool execution, and even then only memory_store resources are supported there, a file or github_repository resource returns a 400 error. Adopting this model at the control plane level would break our re-seedable and commodity hardware constraints outright, there is no path to rebuilding it from Git.
- Model choice is fixed to Claude. There is no multi vendor router comparable to our LiteLLM fronted model tier ladder. Building on this platform buys a single very capable model family and loses model independence and cross provider cost arbitrage entirely.
- The auto permission mode hands the allow or deny judgement for a tool call to a server side LLM evaluator rather than to a deterministic rule. That is a direct conflict with our principle that policy lives outside the model, even though it is audited and even though always_allow and always_ask remain code enforced.
- There is no native signals plane. Session creation is push only from the customer's own application, so the issue tracker, alert and webhook intake we treat as core still has to be built and hosted by us regardless of which worker plane we choose.

### Devin
- The control plane never leaves Cognition's cloud at any tier, including the VPC-branded deployment, so no version of Devin can be deleted and rebuilt independently the way our re-seedable constraint demands
- Policy is role-based and ambient (RBAC and org-scoped Security Profiles) rather than minted per task, so a granted role keeps working for as long as it is held instead of expiring with the task
- Verification is advisory by default. Devin Review only blocks a merge if the customer's own branch protection makes it required, our pipeline can auto-merge low-risk work without needing that external wiring
- Cost control is a monthly or per-cycle ceiling set by an admin, not a per-call priced hold, so it bounds blast radius over a period rather than before a single action runs

### Factory (Droids)
- The agent runs on the user's laptop, CI runner or a persistent sudo-enabled VM with the machine's own git credentials, and policy is enforced inside the agent client. We run one disposable sandbox per task and enforce policy in a gateway the agent cannot touch.
- The control plane, billing and model routing are Factory's SaaS. Self-hosting is an Enterprise sales conversation and nothing about the control plane can be rebuilt from Git.
- Human approval is a session dial (Autonomy Off to High) on an interactive agent. We treat the human as a rare tool call and an approval as a workflow state.

### Codegen
- Policy and credentials sit inside the agent's reach - rules are prompts "not hard constraints", repo secrets are env vars in the sandbox, and the agent may pass skip_trufflehog=true to bypass the secret scan

### AI Software Factory (coleam00)
- They run real repository work in a bare git worktree on the operator's own host, under the operator's own long-lived gh session, with full environment passthrough. No sandbox, no per-task credential. Copying this would break our security constraint outright.
- Nearly every control-plane script the consumer repository ships (dispatch, merge, gate, guard, tripwire, watchdog, state, ledger) is a stub that defers to an externally pinned, unvendored workflow engine, buying almost no first-party code to maintain at the cost of the version drift we found between the two of them
- No cost or spend ceiling exists anywhere, workable for a single hobbyist repository on the owner's own keys, disqualifying for our hard-capped, multi-tenant budget requirement

### ESF (Engineering Software Factory)
- ESF has no merge-and-deploy plane at all. internal/repository/provider.go states outright that the package never pushes, branches, or opens a pull request. The factory's output is a local patch file plus an evidence tree; a human or a separate process must apply, push, and deploy it. Our design goes all the way to a bot-merged PR and a GitOps rollout, so this is not something to copy, only a scope boundary to note.
- Cost control is evidence, not enforcement. Budgets are computed post-hoc from self-reported harness usage purely to set a condition on the manifest; nothing holds or blocks spend. Their own ADR admits this. Our constraint 4 (hard caps, no half measures) makes this pattern a non-starter for us.
- No per-task identity of any kind. Model credentials are static, operator-provisioned, named references, never rotated or scoped per run, and ADR 0006 itself lists OIDC, RBAC, and Vault-based leases as known future work. Copying their approach would abandon our own core principle that identity belongs to the task.
- The whole worker plane assumes one dedicated host (or a shared filesystem across hosts) running CubeSandbox, not a Kubernetes-native per-task namespace. This buys a solo maintainer a much simpler operational model, at the cost of the elastic, quota-scoped isolation our Kubernetes substrate gives us for free.

### Fabro
- Verification is an optional pattern a workflow author composes per graph (a Command node, a Conditional, a loop-back edge, an optional goal_gate), not a mandatory floor every task passes through
- Control-plane state (environments, automations, vault) lives in a mutable server database, not declared in Git, so it lacks our re-seed-from-Git guarantee
- Single-tenant with no roles or ACLs by design, every authenticated user has identical full access, which is incompatible with identity-per-task and trust boundaries
- Cost is tracked for observability only, with no hard cap, hold or ledger anywhere in the run configuration schema

### FactoryKit
- Pure hosted SaaS with no self-hosted or VPC path anywhere in the docs or FAQ. This is the opposite of our re-seedable, commodity-hardware constraint; there is nothing to adopt here, our whole model requires operator-owned infrastructure.
- No visible policy-as-code or capability/budget engine. Their one hard guardrail is architectural (the agent cannot call git); everything else (which repos, which secrets, how much to spend) is configured per integration, not expressed as a versioned policy document. Copying their minimalism would cost us the hard spend caps and audit trail our design treats as non-negotiable.
- Verification's second opinion is the agent reviewing its own diff, not an independent stronger-model reviewer. Cheaper, but weaker than our separate review-frontier gate; adopting it would reduce reviewer independence.
- The product identity itself has churned three times in seven months (Darkshift to FactoryKit to Buildful) with no dedicated security or architecture page at any point. That pace is incompatible with the stable, auditable posture this project's portfolio purpose needs to demonstrate.

### Fusion
- Fusion's default worker isolation is a git worktree on the operator's own machine with no OS sandbox at all; real sandboxing (Linux bubblewrap, macOS Seatbelt) is opt-in and the docs call it experimental. Our constraint treats the sandbox as the one thing that is never optional, so we cannot adopt this default without breaking "security without half measures".
- Fusion's secret-access tool (fn_secret_get) can place a plaintext secret value directly into the model's own context on an "auto" or approved "prompt" policy. Our identity-per-task principle is built to make that specific thing impossible, the agent gets a capability through the gateway, never the raw key. Copying their convenience here means giving up our stated principle, not a neutral trade.
- Fusion's own strategy document frames the product as one solo developer's mesh across their own laptop, Mac mini, server and phone, not a multi-tenant enterprise control plane. Several of its conveniences (a single shared secrets store, a single planner-oversight setting) assume one trusted operator and would need re-checking before they would be safe in a multi-project, multi-tenant setting like ours.

### Loki Mode
- Their default trust boundary is the opposite of ours. The agent process holds real provider credentials directly (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, or a Claude Code session in the same process), and the shipped Helm chart's NetworkPolicy is disabled by default with a default egress of an empty list, which the chart's own comment says means allow-all "because the engine must reach a hosted provider API" (deploy/helm/autonomi/values.yaml, around line 519). Adopting that default would break our "policy lives outside the model" and "identity belongs to the task" principles outright.
- The unsigned Evidence Receipt is honestly forgeable by its own generator (generator_trusted is true on the default path), and only an opt-in GPG or Ed25519 layer closes that gap (docs/SIGNED-RECEIPTS.md). Our audit rows are written by the gateway under its own authority regardless of what the agent does, a stronger default root of trust; copying their weaker default to gain portability would cost us that property.
- Coordination runs through .loki/ files on one machine or one Kubernetes Job (ARCHITECTURE.md calls this "filesystem as the system bus"), not a durable multi-tenant workflow engine. It is simple and resumable for one build, but has no equivalent of our per-task namespace, ServiceAccount or cross-project scheduler; retrofitting concurrent multi-tenant approval state onto a filesystem bus would be a rewrite of the coordination model, not an added feature.

### Machinist
- sandbox_isolation is the core difference: Machinist's runner is a plain exec.Command with only Setpgid for process-group kill (internal/runner/process_unix.go), and SECURITY.md says outright that commands run 'with the operating-system permissions, tools, environment, and credentials of the worker host user'; the one sandboxed pattern (examples/workflows/gvisor-pr, Docker plus gVisor) is an opt-in example, not a core guarantee, and it still hands the agent long-lived API keys with outbound network access inside the sandbox
- no identity-per-task: one shared worker bearer token authenticates every worker to the control plane (constant-time compared in internal/controlplane/server.go), and one shared CSRF token gates the browser UI; nothing mints a short-lived, task-scoped credential anywhere
- no cost control: token usage is recorded per run as a plain integer column (internal/controlplane/store.go) for display only; there is no budget, spend ceiling or spend-based kill switch anywhere in the control plane
- single machine by design, not a fleet: the control plane binds to 127.0.0.1:7331 by default, docs warn against exposing it behind a reverse proxy, storage is one SQLite file, and there is no Kubernetes packaging; our design assumes a Kubernetes substrate and many concurrent sandboxed workers
- 'never merges' is a convention enforced by one optional example script, not a structural property of the system; any other [executors.*] entry in worker.toml could run gh pr merge directly with no gate at all, because the core has no concept of a merge-incapable credential

### Open-Inspect
- Every sandbox backend (Modal, Daytona, E2B, Vercel Sandbox, OpenComputer) is a paid third-party vendor API. There is no self-hosted Docker or Kubernetes sandbox option anywhere in the codebase, against our "commodity hardware, no vendor-specific service" constraint. Copying their worker plane means giving up our own Kubernetes and gVisor entirely.
- The control plane's primary target is Cloudflare Workers, Durable Objects and D1, a vendor-specific serverless platform. The documented AWS fallback (packages/control-plane, docs/CONTROL_PLANE_CONTAINER.md) still leans on SeaweedFS and Litestream to re-implement the same primitives, against our choice to run our own durable execution engine on commodity boxes we fully control.
- Model-provider credentials are injected directly into the sandbox process environment. packages/sandbox-runtime/src/sandbox_runtime/harness/claude_env.py documents that the bridge inherits the supervisor's full environment, including the platform-delivered ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. The agent's own process holds real key material, the opposite of our principle that the sandbox holds no key material and every model call proxies through a gateway.
- No capability policy document and no sandbox network egress control were found anywhere in the docs or code. Whatever the agent's sandbox can reach on the open internet is left to the vendor's default network, against our default-deny egress and policy-outside-the-model principles.
- Merge safety rests entirely on ordinary human GitHub review. The optional "auto-review" (packages/github-bot) posts a comment and is not a required check, and "autofix" (packages/control-plane/src/autofix) only reacts after a human has already commented, so there is no CI gate, policy gate or reproduction check wired into the merge path the way our verification plane wires all three before a human is even asked to approve.

### Open SWE
- GitHub identity is installation-wide by default, a sandbox's proxy-injected token normally covers every repository the GitHub App can reach, narrowed to one repository only for threads a public-repo webhook or automation started. That is a materially wider blast radius than our per-task, audience-scoped ServiceAccount token, and copying it would break identity belongs to the task, not the machine.
- Beyond the one hardcoded workflow-file guard, what a sandbox may do is bounded by GitHub App scopes set once at installation and by prompt instructions, not by a versioned, database-backed capability policy evaluated per task. Adopting this as our model would break policy lives outside the model.
- Real standalone self-hosting needs a LangGraph Enterprise plan, a database URI, a redis URI, a LangSmith API key and a license key that phones home to beacon.langchain.com at startup for license verification, or else the LangGraph Platform SaaS. Our re-seedable, no-vendor-service constraint rules this out as a durable-execution layer, however good the agent code above it is.
- Cost is recorded after the fact for a usage leaderboard, not held or capped before a model call, there is a soft per-run model-call ceiling that posts a Slack notice, not a hard budget the gateway enforces. We would have to add our own budget layer entirely, nothing here transfers.

### OpenAI Symphony
- The tracker is the state machine and the agent itself moves issues between states through a tool, we keep state in Temporal and never let the agent own a transition
- Agents run as processes on one trusted devbox with host gh auth, we run one gVisor pod per task with no credentials in reach
- The agent merges its own PR with gh after a human moves the issue to Merging, we merge by a bot after gates in code
- Verification, PR creation and review handling live in a prompt and skills, not in the orchestrator, so nothing enforces them
- Codex app-server is the only harness and OpenAI the implied model vendor, we keep a model-independent gateway and harness interface
- No spend caps, the design assumes token-rich teams ("token billionaires")

### OpenHands
- Capability decisions run inside the same process as the model (SecurityAnalyzerBase, ConfirmationPolicyBase, sometimes an LLM call), against our principle that policy lives outside the model in gateway code and network policy.
- No NetworkPolicy manifests exist in any Helm chart we read, egress is left to the operator, against our default-deny-network requirement on every task.
- Sandbox identity is a shared static init key bootstrapping every pod in a pool, against our per-task, short-lived, audience-scoped ServiceAccount token verified by the cluster OIDC issuer.
- The default Agent Canvas mode runs the agent unsandboxed on the local host unless the user opts into Docker or Kubernetes sandboxing, against our constraint that everything always runs in a sandbox.

### Optio
- Policy is a handful of booleans (autoMerge, cautiousMode, networkPolicy.enabled) branched on inline in reconcile code, not a versioned capability document enforced by a separate layer.
