- Default network egress is permissive: the networkPolicy DB column defaults to unrestricted, the Helm flag defaults off, and even the restricted NetworkPolicy allows all TCP/443 and TCP/80 egress with no domain allowlist.
- Auto-merge can fire from CI-checks-passing plus config flags plus a subtask reaching COMPLETED state, not from any required approval verdict; an AI review agent can also post a real, binding GitHub APPROVE with no mandatory human in the loop.
- Cost is a ledger (costUsd) with a display-only budget field (budgetUsd, Jobs only) and no enforcement anywhere found, against our price-before-you-spend, hold-then-book model.

### Paperclip
- Default trust boundary is the host machine, not the task. Local adapters run unsandboxed with the operator's own long-lived CLI credentials, the opposite of identity per task.
- Scope is a general "AI company" runner (sales, support, finance, engineering), and the README states it is explicitly not a code review tool. Verification is left to the user.
- {'Policy enforcement is layered but partial': "a real deny-by-default gateway exists for MCP tool calls, but the adapter's own process execution path is host-trusted, and several critical advisories show agent-controlled config reaching raw shell execution unchecked."}

### Sortie
- a locally launched agent inherits the orchestrator's whole OS environment, including every credential in it, the opposite of a per-task scoped identity
- sandboxing is left entirely to the deployer, and Sortie's own docs show that tightening it does not stop a workspace-trusted runtime from launching host-level helper processes
- scope stops at merge, with no deploy plane, no staged rollout and no telemetry-to-signals feedback loop

### SWE-AF and CodeAF (AgentField)
- There is no per-task sandbox anywhere in the stack. SWE-AF runs every concurrent build as git worktrees inside one shared, root-user Docker container with no seccomp or filesystem hardening, and CodeAF runs tasks as plain OS processes on the operator's own machine. Copying this would mean giving up gVisor-per-task isolation entirely, which our security-without-half-measures constraint does not allow.
- Identity is bound to the agent node, not the task. AgentField issues one DID per node, derived once, and SWE-AF's real secrets ('ANTHROPIC_API_KEY', 'OPENROUTER_API_KEY', 'GH_TOKEN') are declared 'scope: global' and injected as plain container-wide environment variables shared by every concurrent build. Our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle would have to be dropped, not weakened, to run this way.
- Merge is agent-driven, not a fixed actor. SWE-AF's PR step is an LLM with a bash tool told to run 'gh pr create' and told not to merge. We use a separate factory-merge identity so merge authority is never a judgement call an LLM makes for itself. A real split on where automation should stop and a fixed, auditable actor should start.
- A wide agent taxonomy against our narrow one. SWE-AF names 22 roles and CodeAF assigns a worker, planner and checker crew to every task. We use one coder class plus a supervisor and a reviewer. Their bet is that narrow roles with typed handoffs shrink error surface. Ours is that one capable coder and a cheap escalation path are cheaper to build and reason about. Matching their granularity would mean widening our agent taxonomy, something we have deliberately deferred.

### Tembo
- Sandboxes are hyperscaler VMs inside the customer's own AWS, GCP or Azure account, not commodity self-owned hardware
- Cost model is a monthly usage allowance with overage billing, not a pre-flight hard cap with a database-locked hold
- No published capability policy outside the model, coarse RBAC plus a human-approval merge gate is the whole trust boundary
- Closed core with access-gated deep docs, opposed to a project meant to be inspectable end to end

### GitHub Copilot cloud agent and Agent HQ
- Everything runs inside one vendor's SaaS on GitHub-hosted Actions and Azure, no self-hosted path, breaks our reseedable and commodity-hardware constraint if copied wholesale
- Identity is org or repo scoped, long-lived secrets exposed as plain environment variables, close to the opposite of identity belonging to the task with short-lived credentials
- Cost control lives at the subscription and billing-cycle layer, not per task, with no hard per-task cap or spend ledger

### Centaur
- No spend governance anywhere in the platform. Only time and lifetime limits exist (SESSION_IDLE_TIMEOUT_MS, SESSION_MAX_DURATION_MS, apiRs.sandboxMaxLifetimeSecs default 259200 seconds). Copying this control plane as-is means giving up hard-cap budgets, holds and a cost ledger entirely.
- Egress defaults open (domains: ["*"] in services/iron-proxy/iron-proxy.yaml), tightened per deployment, against a default-deny-from-second-one constraint.
- No verification or merge plane. An agent's own say-so plus ordinary human PR review is the only gate before anything merges. There is no built-in test gate, risk classification or stronger-model review.
- Sandbox isolation documented as a plain Kubernetes restricted security context and NetworkPolicy, not a named syscall-level sandbox runtime. Not verified whether production adds one.
- Five languages and runtimes glued together (Rust, Python, Ruby on Rails, TypeScript, plus a Vocs docs site) against one Go module and two binaries, trading a single on-call skill set for each ecosystem's strongest tool.

### Coder Tasks / Coder Agents (with Boundary and AgentAPI)
- Identity belongs to the human who typed the prompt, not to the task; every PR, push and API call the agent makes carries the submitting user's own permissions and OAuth token, with no task-scoped credential and no audit boundary between a person's manual actions and their agent's actions

### Kelos
- Kelos puts raw, long-lived provider credentials (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google API keys, Codex OAuth bundles) straight into the agent pod as plaintext env vars or mounted files from a static Secret, with no broker, no scoping and no per-task minting outside the GitHub App path; this is the opposite of our 'identity belongs to the task, not the machine' principle and we could not adopt it without breaking our security constraint
- Kelos has no RuntimeClass or sandbox runtime and no controller-managed NetworkPolicy anywhere in the controller, so isolation is whatever the deploying cluster's default runtime and network already provide; our gVisor, default-deny-egress sandbox is strictly more expensive to build and operate, and that expense is the price of our security constraint, not something Kelos proves we can skip

### OpenSandbox
- Sandbox-only scope: no task state, scheduler, approvals or cost ledger. OpenSandbox could only ever replace pieces of our worker plane, never the rest of the loop.
- Its strongest network isolation (egress sidecar deny.always, Credential Vault) structurally requires runc or Kata, because gVisor's netstack has no iptables nat table; adopting it where we use gVisor today means trading syscall isolation for network isolation, not getting both.
- Provenance is unsettled: Alibaba-originated code and branding under a six-month-old neutral-looking org, with no primary source stating the long-term governance relationship; our best-of-breed-but-swappable constraint means treating this as an Alibaba project for now, not a vendor-independent one.

### Temps
- The credential that drives the coding CLI itself (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN) is decrypted and injected as a real, usable plaintext value into the sandbox environment in executor.rs::prepare_sandbox_workspace, and ai_cli/claude.rs copies it straight into the child process with no ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL redirection to the egress proxy's model-relay; the phantom-credential design in ADR-013 is real for a different surface (the customer-facing AI Gateway/sandbox product) but not for the harness's own driving key, unlike our identity-belongs-to-the-task rule.

### Google Agent Development Kit
- The trust boundary is the process, not the task. An ADK app is a long-lived multi-session service; isolation is opt-in per tool call (a code-execution sandbox) rather than mandatory per unit of work. Adopting ADK as-is would mean giving up per-task disposable pods unless we wrapped it in our own Kubernetes layer, which is the same conclusion most frameworks force.
- Identity means credentials the agent uses to call other APIs on the user's behalf (OAuth2, API key or service account stored per session via CredentialManager), not credentials scoped to one disposable task with no key material reachable from the sandbox. Using ADK's auth stack unmodified would put semi-durable tokens in the same process as the model loop, against our identity-per-task principle.
- There is no control plane. No task registry, scheduler, budget, approval state machine or audit trail; those are explicitly left to whatever hosts the ADK app, including Google's own Agent Engine product. Even in the best case ADK only ever replaces the innermost per-agent code, and there it competes with keeping opencode/Codex CLI behind our Harness interface, which is already model-agnostic without a large Python SDK that pulls in Google Cloud client libraries by default.

### GitHub Agentic Workflows (gh-aw)
- No durable control plane - a task is a GitHub Actions run, state lives in run artifacts and issues, and nothing schedules, retries, or supervises across runs
- Policy is per workflow file in each repository, compiled into Actions YAML, not a central versioned capability document enforced by one gateway
- GitHub Actions is the only substrate - no Forgejo or GitLab path, microVM isolation needs GitHub-hosted KVM runners
- Model credentials sit in an api-proxy sidecar on the same runner as the agent, not behind a central gateway keyed to a task identity
- The final security gate before writes is an LLM threat-detection job that fails open by default (continue-on-error true)

### CodeRabbit
- A custom pre-merge check's pass or fail is an LLM's verdict on a natural-language instruction, not a deterministic rule. Using this as a merge gate keeps the approve-or-deny decision inside the model, the opposite of our policy-lives-outside-the-model principle enforced in factory-gateway and network policy
- The review engine is closed source, including in the Enterprise self-hosted tier, which only relocates the agent container and the Git integration. This piece of a verification plane could never be deleted and rebuilt from our own Git the way our policy YAML and gate code can

### claude-mem and Letta Code (agent memory for coding agents)
- Letta's whole model rests on long-lived, named, identity-bearing agents that accumulate memory across unrelated tasks, the opposite of our principle that identity belongs to the task not the machine and workers are disposable.
- Neither project gates memory writes on who is allowed to write. claude-mem trusts any local process that can reach its loopback HTTP port, and Letta's pre-commit hook runs client-side inside the same sandbox doing the writing, so a compromised agent can skip it.
- Both projects treat an agent, not a task, as the unit of memory and cost, so our hard per-task and per-day caps would not transplant onto either model without first inventing a task-scoped accounting unit neither project has.

### Cline
- {'Policy lives inside the model. The requires_approval flag on a shell command is a field the LLM itself sets when it proposes the tool call, not a rule enforced by infrastructure. Our principle is the opposite': "capability must be enforced in gateway code and network policy, never left to a prompt or the model's own judgement."}
- No per-task credential scoping. A provider API key (or an enterprise-routed equivalent) is configured once per user or org and used directly by whichever session runs, with no audience-scoped, short-lived, per-task token and no forced key rotation between tasks.
- The unit of execution is a long-lived hub daemon plus worktrees on the user's own machine or CI runner, not one disposable, non-root, default-deny-egress sandbox per task. Multi-client convenience and session persistence are bought at the cost of the isolation boundary we require.

### Gemini CLI
- The model plane is locked to the Gemini and Vertex AI protocol family, with no generic OpenAI-compatible provider list. Adopting it as our Harness means giving up the open-weight coding-cheap/coding-frontier ladder, or routing every call through Vertex passthrough and paying full Gemini pricing regardless of task risk.
- {'Identity stays with the operator or the machine (a long-lived Google OAuth session or a static API key held wherever the CLI runs), not with the task. This is the opposite of "identity belongs to the task", and a maximum-severity workspace-trust vulnerability is the concrete cost': 'sandboxing is a runtime opt-in layered on an already-trusted process, not a precondition the platform enforces the way our sandbox pod and default-deny egress do.'}

### Goose (Block)
- Their capability check is partly a model call: permission_judge.rs asks the LLM itself whether a batch of tool requests is read-only, and the prompt-injection scanner (adversary_inspector.rs, classification_client.rs) is also LLM-based. Our principle that policy lives outside the model rules this out as a boundary; at most it is a hint layered on top of code-enforced policy, never the enforcement.
- Identity is the inviting human's own OS account and their own provider keys in a local keyring, not a task-scoped credential, and the shipped default is Autonomous mode: file writes, deletions and shell commands run with no approval at all unless the user turns a dial down. Our model (task-scoped ServiceAccount, ask-once-then-gated) is the opposite default; copying goose's default would fail our security-without-half-measures constraint outright.

### OpenAI Codex
- Codex CLI accepts only wire_api = "responses" now; codex-rs/model-provider-info/src/lib.rs turns wire_api = "chat" into a hard deserialization error. Any self-hosted model behind our gateway must be reachable through a Responses-API shape, not the more common Chat Completions shape most open-source inference servers speak natively. LiteLLM bridges this, but it is one more translation layer we would not need with a Chat-Completions-only harness.
- There is no task-scoped credential concept in the harness itself. Codex assumes one long-lived identity for the whole process: a ChatGPT OAuth session, a single env-var API key, or a command-backed token refreshed on a timer. Our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle would have to be entirely enforced at the gateway boundary; Codex itself contributes nothing toward scoping it further, unlike a harness built around short-lived per-invocation credentials.
- OpenAI does not accept external code contributions: CONTRIBUTING.md states plainly it does not accept external code contributions or pull requests. Apache-2.0 lets us fork and patch, but we can never upstream a fix into the fast-moving mainline; any adapter-side workaround for a Codex bug has to live as a permanent local patch, not a merge.
- The actual control-plane analogue (task/environment registry, approvals, internet-access policy per environment, cost) lives entirely in Codex cloud, which is closed OpenAI SaaS with no self-host option. There is no artifact to study or copy for our own durable control plane; everything usable from this project stops at the harness/worker-plane boundary.

### OpenCode
- opencode ships no execution sandboxing at all and is allow-by-default on every tool, trusting the deployer's machine or container for isolation, the opposite of our default-deny, gVisor-sandboxed, identity-per-task worker plane
- Its unit of coordination is a chat session (a saved, shareable transcript), not a structured artifact; our principle that agents exchange artifacts, not chat, would not survive adopting opencode's session model as anything more than an in-sandbox harness
- Policy and credentials live inside the same process that runs the agent, so a compromised opencode binary can bypass its own permission and policy checks, whereas our factory-gateway sits outside the sandbox and the agent never holds a real key

### SWE-agent and mini-swe-agent
- {'No sandbox network policy anywhere in the code we read': 'the default Docker deployment gets whatever network the container runtime gives it, there is no egress allowlist, and a web_browser tool ships in tools/. Adopting this harness as-is would mean building all of our default-deny egress at the factoryd/pod layer, which we already do, so the harness contributes nothing here.'}
- Identity is a shared, static API key or GitHub token read from environment variables for the whole run (sweagent/agent/models.py, sweagent/run/hooks/open_pr.py), not a credential scoped to one task. Using either project's harness changes nothing about our per-task ServiceAccount and per-task LiteLLM virtual key design; the harness would simply never see a raw key, same as opencode today.
- {'Policy is entirely a prompt-level and CLI-flag concern (blocked commands, budgets, review instructions all sit in the YAML agent config, not enforced by any separate reference monitor). This matches, not contradicts, our "policy outside the model" principle': 'it confirms the enforcement has to live in factoryd/factory-gateway regardless of which harness we run inside the sandbox.'}

### Amazon Bedrock AgentCore
- Their compute trust boundary still hands the sandbox an IAM execution role reachable from inside the microVM through the metadata service. AWS's own security guide says any code or actor running inside the microVM can access execution role credentials. Our principle is that the sandbox holds no key material at all, the gateway holds it. Copying their model would mean accepting the exact half measure we built the gateway to avoid.
- Their outbound-auth decorator, requires_access_token, fetches a bearer token and hands it to the agent's own function as an argument. The agent process holds the token. Our design instead proxies every model and tool call through factory-gateway so no token ever reaches agent code. Their pattern is simpler to code against but is a materially weaker boundary than ours.
- AgentCore has no signals plane, no task registry, no verification-before-merge step and no merge-and-deploy step, because its unit of work is a live request or streaming session, not an issue that becomes a reviewed, merged, deployed change. Four of our five planes have no equivalent here. It answers how do I run an agent that answers requests, we answer how do I turn an issue into a shipped fix.
- It is closed, AWS-only infrastructure. Every review that asked can I self-host this answers no. Adopting it as more than a design reference would break our commodity-hardware and re-seedability constraints outright.
- Cost control is pure metered billing across roughly a dozen separately priced components, left to AWS Cost Explorer and Budgets alarms after the fact. There is no documented per-task spend hold or hard pre-call cap of the kind our gateway enforces before it lets a call through.

### Vertex AI Agent Engine
- There is no task, issue or PR concept anywhere in the product. Its unit of work is a live invocation or session against a deployed agent, not a signal that becomes a reviewed, merged, deployed change. It could at most replace pieces of our worker plane, never the control plane, signals plane or merge-and-deploy plane.
- It is closed and bound to one GCP project with no self-hosted path for Runtime, Sessions, Memory Bank or Sandbox. Building any control-plane piece on it breaks our re-seedable and commodity-hardware constraints outright, there is no way to reconstruct it from Git and an offline seed bundle.
- The default identity model is a shared Per-Project, Per-Product Service Agent (P4SA), not a per-task credential. Unit 42's March 2026 "Double Agents" research showed any code running inside a deployed agent's execution context could read the P4SA token off the metadata service and use it to read every Cloud Storage bucket in the project, and even reach Google-internal container registries. That is the direct opposite of "identity belongs to the task, not the machine." Google's fix, Bring Your Own Service Account and an opt-in Agent Identity, is per-agent-deployment, still not per-task.
- Model routing is Gemini-first, Model Garden favours Google's own models with a few open-weight additions bolted on, unlike our tiered, vendor-independent ladder behind LiteLLM. Building here buys deep GCP integration, BigQuery, Workspace, IAM, at the cost of the model independence and cross-provider cost arbitrage our design treats as a first principle.

### Microsoft Foundry Agent Service, Agent Framework, Agent 365
- The whole platform is a proprietary Azure PaaS control plane. Copying the control-plane shape would mean giving up commodity hardware and re-seedability outright; there is no path to run Foundry Agent Service or Agent 365 outside Azure.
- Identity is per-agent, not per-task: a dedicated Entra identity is created once for an agent and rotated over its lifetime (Agent 365's create/rotate/decommission lifecycle), closer to a durable service account than our disposable, task-scoped, kubelet-rotated token. Their model suits a long-lived digital worker; ours suits a disposable one-shot job.
- Durable execution is delegated to a separate Azure Durable Functions extension repository, not built into the framework or the hosted runtime. It is a real answer to durability, but it ties you to Azure Functions the way we tie ourselves to Temporal, so it is not a reason to add a dependency, only a data point that others also factor this out.
- There is no notion of a task born from an issue with a PR and a merge gate at the end. Foundry publishes an agent version to a stable endpoint; it does not turn a bug report into a reviewed, merged, deployed change. The whole verification-before-merge loop we care about does not exist here.

### Ona
- Ona's UX is built around a live conversation pane, the April 2026 redesign explicitly frames it as a workspace, not a dashboard or terminal. Our principle keeps agents exchanging artifacts (TaskBrief, branch, PR, event stream) and treats a human's input as a rare tool call, not a running chat. Copying Ona's UX pattern would reintroduce the chat surface we deliberately avoided
- The management plane (identity, policy, coordination) can never be self-hosted, even in VPC mode; only compute moves into the customer account. That breaks our re-seedable constraint outright, since our rebuild can never depend on a third party's control plane staying up or priced the same
- Nearly every serious security or governance feature (Veto, OIDC workload identity, most org policies beyond one auto-stop timeout, VPC runners) is Enterprise-tier and sales-gated with no published price. Our own escape-hatch principle would force us to route around or drop each of these the moment we needed them, the same trap Ona's own customers are in
- Neither AWS nor GCP runner uses Kubernetes: AWS is ECS Fargate plus an NLB, DynamoDB and MemoryDB; GCP is a Compute Engine managed instance group. This is real evidence that our Kubernetes-as-substrate choice is a preference for a factory our size, not a necessity
- The whole open-source product (AGPL-3.0, 13.8k stars, since 2018) was deprecated, is no longer recommended for new users, and had its pay-as-you-go tier sunset on 2025-10-15. Its closed successor is priced per compute credit and the parent company is now (announced, unclosed) being folded into a single model vendor. This is the exact escape-hatch failure our own principle warns against: the open-source door existed and was walked away from once a better business model appeared, and the resulting single-vendor product is itself now being absorbed by a single model provider

### OpenAI Frontier
- Frontier's governance, evaluation and orchestration all run inside OpenAI's own infrastructure, for agents built by OpenAI, the customer, or third parties alike. Jason Stanley's critique (linked in sources) is the sharp version: the model vendor also runs the control plane that governs competing agents, an asymmetry we avoid by keeping factoryd, the policy file and the gateway entirely ours and model-vendor-agnostic.
- Nothing about Frontier is inspectable or self-hostable: no repo, no SDK, no on-prem control plane, sales-gated pricing, deployment via paired Forward Deployed Engineers. Adopting anything at the platform level would violate our re-seedable and commodity-hardware constraints outright, there is no artifact to rebuild from Git.
- Every mechanism claim (agent identity, permission scoping, evaluation loops) is asserted in marketing prose, not shown as code, a config schema, or an API payload. Our own principle that policy lives outside the model is falsifiable by reading the gateway and the policy YAML; Frontier's equivalent claim is not independently checkable from anything public.

### Atlassian Rovo Dev
- Identity is the invoking human's existing product permissions, carried through for the whole session, never a short-lived task-scoped credential. This is the opposite of identity belongs to the task, not the machine, and would cost us least-privilege per task if copied wholesale.
- Closed source, single-tenant SaaS with no self-host option. Nothing here can be re-seeded onto our own cluster; adopting Rovo Dev's mechanisms means re-implementing them, not running them.
- The product stops at a plan, a diff, or a review comment on a PR. There is no orchestrated state machine driving a task through merge and deploy; a human always runs the CLI turn or clicks merge. Our Temporal-driven workflow through auto-merge and staged rollout has no equivalent here.
- Two independently reported prompt-injection paths (RovoBlast, a PromptArmor technique) show the boundary between the user's instruction and untrusted content the agent reads is enforced by the model's judgement, not by a deterministic layer outside it, for that specific class of input. This is the one place their content policy is inside the model, against their own stated principle for tool calls.

### Augment Code Intent
- There is no sandbox. Agents run as local processes on the developer's own Mac with the same filesystem and network access the developer has. A git worktree gives directory isolation between parallel Spaces, nothing more. This breaks security without half measures outright and nothing here is worth adopting even partially.
- There is no task-scoped identity. GitHub is connected once, by OAuth, into the account, through what the product calls the Cosmos dashboard, and every Space reuses that one connection. This is the opposite of identity belongs to the task, not the machine.
- Policy lives in the human clicking through three UI checkpoints, not in a capability document enforced by code or network rule. That is adequate for one trusted developer approving their own work and not a substitute for policy enforced outside the model.
- Cost is a shared, pre-paid monthly credit pool metered after usage, with per-user caps only on the Enterprise tier. On Standard or Max, one teammate or one runaway Space can drain a whole team's month before anyone notices, the opposite of a per-call hold taken up front.
- Nothing here is re-seedable or self-hostable. It is a closed, cloud-backed macOS binary. Deleting and rebuilding from Git, S3 and an offline seed bundle has no equivalent here, and there is no path to run it on commodity Linux hardware at all.

### Blitzy
- The whole pipeline, reverse engineering, orchestration, QA, runs inside Blitzy's own sealed VPC that the customer cannot inspect, rebuild or re-seed. Even the "on-prem" and "black-box on-prem" tiers are a vendor appliance dropped into the customer's network, not something buildable from Git and OpenTofu. This is the opposite of our re-seedable constraint.
- Pricing is per line of code onboarded and generated (0.10 and 0.20 USD per line) inside 250K to 50M USD annual contracts. The product is shaped as a vendor batch service sold to enterprises, not a tool a small team runs and owns.
- Work is one giant asynchronous batch job per prompt, days to weeks long and not cancellable, with only two checkpoints (plan approval, PR review). There is no issue or alert intake, no telemetry loop, and no supervisor watching a running job, so the whole signals-plane and gate-supervision half of our design has no counterpart here.

### Charlie Labs
- No per-task identity: one GitHub App token and one bot account cover every run, no ServiceAccount-equivalent scoped to a single task, so nothing here informs our identity-per-task principle
- Model roster is the opposite of ours by design: the trust center states Charlie exclusively uses OpenAI, Anthropic and Google and explicitly excludes open-source and DeepSeek models, where we start on DeepSeek and Qwen and treat OpenAI and Anthropic as absent on purpose
- Everything is closed SaaS on GCP with no re-seed or self-host path; copying their product shape at the infrastructure level would break our commodity-hardware and re-seedable constraints outright

### Continue
- An agent run is a stateless prompt-plus-tools trigger with no pre-flight policy or budget gate; the model can be handed an allow-all flag and go. Our control plane checks capability, budget and approval state before a task's sandbox is even created. Copying Continue's trigger-then-run shape would break policy-outside-the-model, since nothing stops a run before it starts.
- No published sandbox for cloud agents (Mission Control) and no sandbox at all for local CLI runs by design, it is a developer's own machine or their own CI. Adopting that would break our default-deny, gVisor-per-task constraint outright; it is not a gap to close, it is a different problem (personal tool vs multi-tenant factory).
- No budget primitives anywhere in the product: pricing is bring-your-own-key with an optional per-seat SaaS fee, no per-task hold, no spend ledger. This is not a lesson to take, it reflects that a human always pays and approves per session, which our largely-autonomous design does not have.

### Cyrus
- Cyrus runs as, and with the full permissions of, the host machine's already-authenticated git/gh/glab user. There is no task-scoped or short-lived credential anywhere. This is the opposite of "identity belongs to the task, not the machine" and is not something to copy even partially.
- No sandbox. Git worktrees give filesystem isolation for parallel runs, not a security boundary, and the only network control is an optional, off-by-default HTTP/SOCKS egress proxy that filters Bash subprocess traffic and explicitly leaves the harness's own file and inference tools untouched. Adopting this would break "security without half measures."
- No control plane. State is one flat JSON file written from an in-process daemon, with an in-process semaphore for concurrency. There is no durable workflow, no audit trail, no approvals-as-state. Fine for a single operator's laptop, not a substitute for Temporal and Postgres.
- No cost governance anywhere in the code, no cap, no ledger, no hold. Cyrus is BYOK by design so this is a deliberate non-goal for them, but it is a hard requirement for us.
- No verification plane. Cyrus creates a PR and stops; there is no CI gate, no policy gate, no model review wired into the flow. All correctness checking is left to whatever the human reviewer or the target repo's own CI does.

### Gas Town and Beads
- Every agent runs as the operator's own OS user, with that user's real git credentials and push access, and Claude Code and Codex are started with vendor bypass flags (--dangerously-skip-permissions, --dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox) with no opt-out in the default path. Our identity-per-task and security-without-half-measures principles rule this out by construction
- Isolation between concurrently running agents is a documented non-feature, not an oversight, workers share one filesystem and one OS user, and the project's own SECURITY.md tells the operator to build isolation themselves. We sandbox by default, they sandbox by exception, opt-in and unfinished
- Policy is prompt-level (role directive files, TOML formulas) plus, only if an operator turns it on, a not-fully-wired mutual-TLS proxy with a certificate denylist. We put policy in gateway code and network rules a prompt cannot touch, as the only path, not an option
- There is no cost cap anywhere in the code, only a spend reporting ledger and a concurrency limiter aimed at avoiding provider rate limits, not at avoiding a bill. Our hard per-task, per-day, per-project caps are a precondition we treat as non-negotiable, not a nice-to-have
- State lives on one Dolt SQL Server process per "town", a single point of failure the project's own architecture doc names as such, and the whole town runs on one host. We accept the operational cost of Kubernetes specifically so that no one process, pod or node is a single point of failure

### Jules
- Jules auto-approves a plan on a timer if the human walks away ('no need to babysit'), we treat approval as a state a human must actively move, never a timeout default
- Jules gives every session a cloud VM with general internet access by design, exploited in practice for exfiltration and remote code execution through prompt injection, our default-deny egress trades that convenience for the attack surface
- Jules authenticates its whole API with one static per-user key (X-Goog-Api-Key), not a rotated per-task credential, adopting that would break identity per task outright
- Jules has no self-host path, it is pure SaaS bundled into a consumer subscription, our re-seedable commodity-hardware constraint bets that owning the substrate is worth the operational cost

### Kilo Code and Kilo Cloud
- Cloud Agent's docs state 'Auto/YOLO mode is always on': there is no confirmation step during a cloud session, only an after-the-fact diff view and an optional auto-commit. This is the opposite of our principle that approval is a workflow state; they buy throughput and simplicity by removing the gate entirely for the paid product, where we keep an explicit approval or auto-rule state before merge
- Kilo's own docs admit webhook triggers are 'susceptible to prompt injection attacks' and tell users to restrict them to trusted sources, i.e. the trust-boundary problem is handed to the operator rather than solved in code. Our design marks any outside-account text as untrusted for the agent and the reviewer; copying Kilo's trigger model as-is would reopen a hole we close deliberately

### Attractor (StrongDM Software Factory)
- Sandbox isolation, credential handling and capability policy are explicit "out of scope, nice to have" extension points in the specs, not core requirements. Copying that permissiveness would break our security-without-half-measures constraint outright.
- Their published cost posture is spend more (at least 1000 USD of tokens per engineer per day, uncapped). Ours is hard caps, holds and a per-project envelope on three Hetzner boxes. Matching their spend would violate our commodity hardware constraint.
- Scenario holdouts need someone to author and maintain scenarios plus a Digital Twin Universe of every third party dependency. That standing cost is real but absent from their own headline number, and we are one developer, not a three person team.
- The rule that code must not be reviewed by humans is a stronger claim than our design makes anywhere. We treat human approval as a workflow state to minimise, not a step to delete, because that approval is also the accountability trail a security product needs.

### Sweep
- No durable task state. Working state lives in process-memory dicts (events, on_ticket_events) and Python threads that get force-killed with ctypes on timeout. A backend restart silently drops every in-flight task, which is the opposite of our per-task Temporal workflow and would break the re-seedable constraint.
- {'Open-core gating is wired directly into the agent code path': 'on_ticket.py asserts validate_license() before doing any work, and self-hosting starts from a 14-day trial key from a company that has since stopped selling this product. Our rule is to pick tools with an escape hatch from enterprise gating; this is the gating itself, not a tool that has it.'}
- No sandbox for the agent. It edits a locally cloned repo and calls the GitHub API from inside the same container as the webhook server, with no per-task isolation and no egress control. Copying this shape would drop our default-deny sandbox constraint entirely rather than trade it for something else.

### Terragon
- Per-repo user secrets (the environment table's environmentVariables, decrypted and injected as plain env vars into the sandbox) reach the agent directly, and GitHub identity is one 30-day, repo-scoped App installation token reused across every task on that repo, not minted per task. Copying either breaks our identity-per-task and no-keys-in-the-sandbox principles; only the model-credential path, proxied, key stays server-side, matches our approach.
- The product's job stops at "PR opened". No CI gate, no policy evaluation, no model review, no merge or deploy step exists anywhere in the code read. That is a smaller product, not a worse implementation of ours, and it shows a background-agent tool can succeed without ever touching verification. Our factory's whole premise is unattended merge, so we cannot shrink scope the way they did.
- The sandbox is a fat, root, general-purpose Ubuntu 24.04 image with Docker-in-Docker, Rust, PHP and more preinstalled (packages/sandbox-image/Dockerfile.hbs), optimised for "any repo boots fast" rather than a minimal attack surface. Matching our non-root, read-only root FS, default-deny egress sandbox means giving up their instant multi-language support; matching their image costs us the hardening our security constraint demands.
- It is one company's single Next.js monolith over Postgres and Redis with one small WebSocket side-service, sized for one team's paying users, not a multi-tenant control plane. That simplicity is exactly why three people could build and then shut it down in eight months; our heavier shape exists because we have to survive being deleted and re-seeded as a whole company's infrastructure, a requirement they never had.

### Warp Oz
- Even in "self-hosted" mode, session transcripts, orchestration metadata and LLM traffic still route through Warp's servers under a ZDR agreement. There is no path to running the control plane itself from our own Git and Postgres. Copying this dependency would break our re-seedable and no-vendor-service constraints outright.
- No declarative, versioned policy document was found anywhere in the docs. Access is granted per integration or per agent ad hoc, and "least privilege" is stated as a principle, not enforced as a checked artifact. This is the opposite of policy lives outside the model as a file in Git that a prompt cannot alter.
- Sandbox strength is a deployment choice, not a guarantee. The "Direct" backend shares the host kernel and the "Unmanaged" backend explicitly inherits the host's network access, tools and credentials. Our default is one gVisor pod with default-deny egress for every task, with no configuration that removes it.

### Linear for Agents
- Policy is a markdown guidance document injected into the prompt context for the agent to follow voluntarily; Linear enforces nothing about what the agent actually does, because Linear never runs or sandboxes the agent itself

### Agyn
- Chat-only intake by design, no issues, telemetry, DAG or batch UI, is a different product shape from our signals plane, not a maturity gap, and copying it would cost us structured task specs and ticket-tied audit
- No verification or merge and deploy plane at all, the platform's job ends when the agent replies in a thread, so its simplicity is not available to us if we want to keep shipping tested and reviewed changes
- Cost governance as post hoc metering rather than a pre call hold is cheaper to build, which is likely why the marketing outruns the code, but it breaks our hard caps constraint outright
- Identity scoped to the workload for its whole lifetime rather than minted per task, with a shared namespace and no Kubernetes ServiceAccount at all, buys a lighter identity story at the cost of the namespace level isolation we get by default

### kagent
- kagent is an interactive, chat/A2A-driven runtime for infrastructure operator agents (Kubernetes, Istio, Helm, Argo, Prometheus, Grafana, Cilium tools), not an autonomous issue-to-PR factory. It has no CI, no test gate, no reviewer-model verification, and no merge/deploy plane at all, so at most it could ever inform our worker and control planes, never verification or merge/deploy.
- No budget, spend cap, or cost ledger exists anywhere in the code or docs. LLM cost control is left entirely to the operator's own provider account limits, which conflicts with our "hard caps with holds" principle; we would have to build the whole cost-control axis ourselves regardless of anything borrowed here.
- The default auth mode ships as insecure, and even trusted-proxy mode decodes JWT claims without checking signature or expiry, trusting an upstream boundary. Our gateway verifies task identity against the cluster's own OIDC issuer at every hop; adopting kagent's identity code as-is would break "identity belongs to the task."

### agentgateway
- it is a data-plane proxy only: no task registry, workflow, sandboxing, CI gate or merge step. It fills at most the LLM/MCP-facing slice of our worker plane, never the four planes around it
- identity is per-request or per-workload (JWT claims, SPIFFE SVID, virtual keys), not a credential minted and torn down for one task's lifetime; fine-grained enough to key budgets, not to satisfy 'a task, not a machine'
- Kubernetes-native by design: its richest policy and identity features (AgentgatewayPolicy CRDs, Gateway API ReferenceGrant, SPIFFE) assume a full Gateway API mesh, complexity our three-box constraint does not otherwise need

### Bifrost (Maxim)
- Bifrost keeps all governance state (provider configs, keys, budgets, usage) in memory per node and never re-reads it from the database after boot; OSS explicitly refuses multiple nodes against one Postgres backend, only Enterprise (with a modified RAFT) syncs nodes. Our control plane's entire premise is the opposite, Temporal plus Postgres as the durable source of truth factoryd resumes from after a crash, which our re-seedable constraint requires; copying Bifrost's in-memory model would break it
- Identity is a caller-supplied, optionally-expiring virtual key (sk-bf-*), not a short-lived credential minted per unit of work. That fits a stable pool of developers and services calling a shared gateway, but it is the opposite of identity belonging to the task, not the machine; the closest fit in our stack is a stable key for our own operators or CI calling factory-gateway, not a substitute for the projected ServiceAccount token we mint per agent task
- The guardrail suite, RBAC, audit logs, SSO/identity federation, clustering and adaptive load balancing are entirely Enterprise-gated behind a 14-day trial; the docs say plainly that is part of how we draw the OSS vs Enterprise line. Our own escape-hatch requirement for a tool means we could use Bifrost's OSS tier (already well above our RPS needs) but we would hit the same wall they name if we ever wanted multi-node HA or content guardrails without paying, a gate LiteLLM does not impose on budgets or routing

### Envoy AI Gateway (now Agent Router)
- It hard-requires Envoy Gateway's own extension-server hook (manifests/envoy-gateway-values.yaml, config.envoyGateway.extensionManager.hooks.xdsTranslator, pointed at the ai-gateway-controller service) to patch xDS at translation time. Cilium's Gateway API support is a separate, self-contained pipeline: cilium-operator owns GatewayClass cilium (controllerName io.cilium/gateway-controller), turns Gateway API objects straight into CiliumEnvoyConfig, and each Cilium agent programs its own embedded or DaemonSet cilium-envoy over xDS, with no equivalent extension-server hook for a third party to plug into. Agent Router cannot attach to Cilium's existing Envoy.
- Fitting it in means installing Envoy Gateway as a second, independent Gateway API implementation next to Cilium: its own GatewayClass, its own CRDs (EnvoyProxy, EnvoyPatchPolicy), its own Envoy Proxy Deployment per Gateway, its own namespace (envoy-gateway-system plus envoy-ai-gateway-system). Cilium keeps doing CNI, kube-proxy replacement and default-deny NetworkPolicy underneath regardless, since that enforcement wraps every pod including Envoy Gateway's, so nothing actively conflicts, but it doubles the L7 proxy fleet and the control-plane surface we would have to reconcile against policy in Git.
- Cost control is a token-count quota against Envoy's global rate-limit service (Redis-backed), scoped per Gateway/AIGatewayRoute, not a dollar ledger with a hold taken under a database lock and booked after the real cost is known, and not fleet-wide. Adopting it as our budget mechanism would regress our per-task, per-day, per-project envelope.

### TensorZero
- TensorZero only ever sees a single inference call plus later feedback events. It has no concept of a multi-hour task with state transitions, so however good its budgeting and auth are, a model gateway cannot be our control plane; LiteLLM's role in our design stays a thin proxy under a separate Temporal-backed task layer, and this project is two years of funded engineering confirming that boundary rather than blurring it.
- {"Autopilot, its one agentic feature, only ever calls TensorZero's own typed tools (crates/autopilot-tools": 'config, evals, datasets, feedback) through an in-house durable-tools executor. It never touches a filesystem, a shell or arbitrary code, so nothing here informs sandbox or worker-plane design, only the evaluation plane.'}

### Weave Router
- It decides per single upstream call (an 'action'), not per task or session outcome. It would slot in where LiteLLM sits today, not replace factory-gateway's task-identity or budget layer.
- Its most sophisticated routing policy (the HMM sidecar strategy) leans on a proprietary embedding API (Gemini) and a separately shipped frozen artifact, unlike the fully local ONNX default. Picking that mode reopens the vendor-lock question our constraints try to close.
- Multi-replica production self-hosting is hardcoded to the real GCP Pub/Sub client for cross-replica cache invalidation, not an abstracted broker interface. Single-instance self-hosting avoids it but is not the horizontal-scale story.
- It has no concept of a disposable per-task sandbox, a verification gate, or a merge step. Adopting it only ever touches one line item inside our control plane (model tier choice), never the worker or verification planes.

### Emdash
- {'Emdash is single-user and local-first by construction': 'task state is a per-machine SQLite file, credentials sit in the OS keychain, and the "control plane" is a connection supervisor to remote hosts, not a multi-tenant scheduler. Copying this would mean giving up the durable, shared server-side state we already have.'}
- No sandboxing or network egress control anywhere. Agent CLIs run as ordinary node-pty child processes with the full permissions and network reach of the host user. This breaks our security-without-half-measures constraint outright and is not something to borrow.
- Work starts when a human clicks Add Task and picks an issue or a branch. Automations are cron-triggered, not event or alert-triggered. Turning this into a factory posture would require adding the webhook-to-task and alert-to-task pipeline our signals plane already has.
- Credentials are long-lived, account-scoped OAuth tokens reused across every task in a project, not minted per task. Fine when the task and the human share one trust boundary (one desktop, one user), incompatible with our identity-per-task principle once more than one human or an untrusted contributor is involved.

### HumanLayer and CodeLayer
- They treat the operator's own workstation as the trust boundary, filesystem permissions are the entire security model; identity-per-task and sandbox-isolation are incompatible with that at the core, so we can copy the interaction pattern, not the deployment shape.
- Approvals never time out and carry no policy content beyond one human decision; a durable control plane needs default decisions, multiple channels and an audit trail behind the same tool call.
- Every session starts from a human-typed query, there is no signals plane at all, copying their UX still leaves the entire intake side of our loop to build ourselves.

### Superset
- Harness adapters observe an unmodified CLI agent through its own hooks and transcripts and never mediate tool calls, so enforcement is whatever the agent itself provides, or nothing. Incompatible with policy living outside the model.
- The one place they do isolate, cloud sandboxes, defaults to allow-all egress through a credential-injecting proxy rather than default-deny. Incompatible with security without half measures.
- The default, free, higher-usage tier, local desktop mode, has no process isolation at all, by the README's own admission. The opposite of our always-sandboxed-per-task rule.
- No ledger or cap on coding-agent model spend anywhere in the product, by design, since Superset states it never touches model traffic. A structural difference from our budget-holding model gateway, not something to import as is.
- Every non-local surface depends on Superset's own hosted accounts, relay, gate and billing with no self-host path shipped, despite the licence permitting internal self-hosting. The opposite of our re-seedable, commodity-hardware requirement.

### Agent Orchestrator (AO)
- Host-trusted, unsandboxed execution by design versus our default-deny sandbox: AO runs the agent directly on the developer's machine with real credentials and full network access on purpose. Our security constraint rules this out. The price we pay is worse local ergonomics for a single interactive developer.
- Bring-your-own, long-lived personal credentials versus identity per task: AO has no capability policy or credential brokering because every session already runs as the user. Copying this breaks our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle outright.
- Single-machine, single-user architecture (SQLite, loopback daemon) versus our multi-tenant, re-seedable, Kubernetes-native fleet: AO's stronger multi-tenant design (Postgres control plane, per-session JWT fencing, container sandboxes) exists only in the closed, non-GA Cloud product, not in anything inspectable or self-hostable today.
- Feed feedback into the still-running session versus our new session per attempt: cheaper and faster for small corrections since context is not rebuilt, but AO ships no supervisor-style circuit breaker on a nudged session, and its own users hit that gap the day this report was written.

### Vibe Kanban
- Zero process or network isolation of its own is the opposite of "security without half measures." Any UI pattern we copy has to keep running inside our gVisor pod with default-deny egress, copying their host-trust model along with the UI would break that constraint outright.
- No credential brokering at all (the operator's own gh, git and model CLI logins are used as-is) is the opposite of "identity belongs to the task, not the machine." Their 30-second zero-setup onboarding is bought with exactly the raw-credential-to-agent path our design forbids.
- Multi-user only exists in the now-shutting-down Cloud tier, so none of its auth code (JWT, OAuth, a shared bootstrap password) is reusable. We already build our own multi-tenant auth (Forgejo OAuth2) and would keep doing so regardless of anything here.
- The product stops at "open a pull request." There is no deploy step and no GitOps concept anywhere in the codebase, so copying its UX buys nothing for our re-seedable constraint or our merge-and-deploy plane, that half of our loop has no analogue to study in this project.

### LangWatch
- LangWatch isolates a conversation's workers from each other by Unix UID inside one shared pod and network namespace, we give every task its own gVisor pod and its own network namespace, copying their density model would trade our harder tenant boundary for lower sandbox cost.
- Their AI Gateway checks a budget against a config snapshot refreshed once a minute and books spend after the provider replies, we hold a worst-case price under a database lock before the call and reconcile after, copying their model reopens the overspend window our hold exists to close.
- Their coding-agent session feature exists because an externally run agent must tell LangWatch what it did after the fact, our tasks already carry their own repository, branch and issue before a sandbox exists, so we do not need their attribution machinery at all.
- Their SSO, RBAC, SCIM, audit log and gateway webhooks sit behind a paid Enterprise licence even in the self-hosted image, funding a team that ships weekly and writes a dated decision record for almost everything, our own build-it-ourselves stance means we do not collect that revenue and have to earn the same hardening by our own effort.

### Promptfoo
- Promptfoo is a point tool that assumes an external CI system calls it per change. It does no task intake, scheduling, sandboxing or merging of its own, so adopting it changes nothing about our five-plane loop, it only adds one more check inside the verification plane.
- Its default trust model phones home to api.promptfoo.app for test generation and grading unless the user supplies their own model credentials and sets PROMPTFOO_DISABLE_REMOTE_GENERATION, and a fixed list of red-team plugins and multi-turn strategies are hard-coded remote-only and cannot run air-gapped at all outside Enterprise On-Prem. That is the opposite of our default-deny egress principle, and it means the red-team half would have to run from a narrow-allowlisted CI runner, never inside an agent sandbox.
- The company is now under agreement to be acquired by OpenAI. Sending adversarial-generation traffic, and potentially fragments of our own prompts, to a lab that also sells a competing model tier we deliberately exclude early is a real tension for the remote-only red-team path specifically. It does not apply to the plain eval and regression-gate path run with our own configured provider.
- Their self-hosted server is explicitly not recommended for production, has no HA, no shared database across replicas, and the Helm chart is experimental with replicaCount fixed at 1. It does not meet our re-seedable, immutable bar as a running service, but we do not need it as a service, the CLI writing JSON in a CI job is enough for a gate.

### Arcade
- Arcade's model assumes a signed-in human behind every OAuth grant, consenting once per provider; our tasks start from an issue with no human in the loop, so their delegated-user-scope story does not map onto an unattended coder task without inventing a synthetic pre-consented identity
- The parts that actually enforce policy, store secrets and keep the audit trail (Govern, in their language) live in the closed-source Arcade Engine SaaS, not in this MIT repository, so depending on Arcade for identity would put a component outside our re-seed-from-Git boundary

### Deno Claw Patrol
- Default-allow when no rule matches, not default-deny: Claw Patrol only gates what an operator explicitly wrote a rule for, which is the opposite of our default-deny-egress, capability-must-be-granted posture.

### Infisical Agent Vault
- Identity is a long-lived, shared bearer token per named agent, hashed and stored server-side, with no expiry unless the operator rotates it by hand. Ours is a projected, audience-scoped, kubelet-rotated Kubernetes ServiceAccount token, minted per task and verified per call against the cluster OIDC issuer. Their model authenticates a standing actor, ours authenticates a single unit of work. Copying their model would mean giving up per-task credential lifetime for a simpler but weaker durable-service-account trust anchor.

### Keycard
- {'Their coding-agent enforcement point is a CLI process on the same machine as the agent (laptop or CI runner), wrapping the agent and trusting its own hooks not to be bypassed by the process it constrains. Ours is a network boundary the agent cannot reach around even fully compromised': 'default-deny egress plus a gateway that never lets key material into the sandbox. Copying their model into our worker plane would weaken us, not strengthen us; their reach across laptops and arbitrary CI is a problem we do not have, because every task already runs in a namespace we control.'}
- {'Keycard is itself a hosted multi-tenant SaaS dependency. We cannot make "call an external STS for every tool call" part of our core identity path without breaking re-seedability': 'it is a fourth thing we would not own and could not rebuild from Git, S3 and a seed bundle. Self-hosting exists only behind an undocumented Enterprise tier.'}
- Their four-part identity (user, device, agent, task) assumes a human is normally delegating into an agent session, with SSO federation and a consent screen per resource. Most of our tasks start from an issue or an alert with no requesting human at all; the closest match we have is the approver at the merge gate, not a per-call delegator. Adopting their consent-chain machinery would require inventing a human for every task we currently run without one.
- {"Task, as an identity dimension, is not a claim in Keycard's own token schema": 'the documented JWT claims are `iss`, `sub`, `sub_profile`, `keycard_app_id`, `client_id`, `aud`, `scope`, `sid`, `exp`, `iat`, `jti`, none of them a task identifier. The dimension is carried informally through scope and session id and asserted in blog prose, not in the wire format. Our task already has a durable Temporal workflow, a Postgres row and a budget ledger behind it; an external broker like this would only ever be a credential-issuance layer on top, never a replacement for that state.'}

### OneCLI
- TLS interception is the trust boundary, our path never terminates the agent's own TLS session
- Real credentials are long-lived and centrally decrypted, ours are minted short-lived per task
- Cost caps and budgets are an enterprise-only feature there, a non-negotiable core floor for us
- The unit of work is an open-ended chat conversation, ours is a gated pipeline ending in a reviewed PR

### Stacklok ToolHive
- ToolHive's unit of work is a long-lived MCP server process serving many callers; ours is a disposable per-task sandbox serving one agent once. Adopting ToolHive's model would mean pooling and multiplexing tool servers across tasks, which trades our task-scoped blast radius for their operational efficiency: a security posture we do not want to give up for a cost saving we do not need at our scale.

### CubeSandbox
- Their strongest isolation tier needs either bare metal or a Tencent-published host kernel fork (PVM, x86_64 only, validated mainly on Tencent's own infrastructure), a bigger and less reversible operational commitment than gVisor's stock-kernel model, which is exactly why we chose gVisor
- It has no durable task state, budgets, approvals or verification, it is a stateless execution primitive driven synchronously by an external caller, so adopting it replaces one plane's implementation, never the orchestration around it
- Its egress control is a full TLS-terminating MITM with a proxy-trusted root CA in every sandbox, more powerful than our gateway-mediated per-call proxying but a materially bigger trust surface, the proxy itself sees plaintext traffic to every allowed domain

### Docker Sandboxes, MCP Gateway, cagent
- There is no task registry, scheduler, approval workflow, budget or audit trail anywhere in the product family. docker-agent persists session state per sandbox but never claims durable multi-task orchestration. Adopting any of this would replace at most our worker-plane sandbox, never factoryd. It is not a competing control plane.
- Identity is developer- and session-scoped, not task-scoped. One host proxy per developer machine holds long-lived provider API keys and injects them into whichever sandbox is running. There is no short-lived, audience-restricted, per-task token verified against an external identity provider. Copying this pattern as-is would pull us back from 'identity belongs to the task' toward 'identity belongs to the laptop'.
- The parts that would make this an operable multi-developer platform, not just a personal dev tool, are paywalled: centralised network/filesystem/MCP policy enforcement and audit logs (Docker AI Governance) are a separate subscription reached by contacting sales, with no self-hosted open equivalent shown in the docs. Depending on that layer would violate our 'escape hatch if maintainers gate needed features' constraint.
- Cloud sandboxes run only on Docker-managed infrastructure with no bring-your-own-cloud or on-prem multi-tenant mode. It could never sit inside a 'delete everything, rebuild from Git and S3' pipeline as anything but an optional, rentable accelerator for one developer or one project, the same ceiling Namespace hits in the ci-build category.

### Kubernetes SIG agent-sandbox
- The controller deliberately implements zero isolation itself: it is a thin, declarative passthrough of runtimeClassName onto a PodTemplate. All the actual hard work (gVisor, Kata) is exactly the RuntimeClass mechanism we already hand-configure; adopting this project would not change our isolation posture, only how the pod spec around it gets assembled and templated.
- No task or job model at all: a Sandbox/Claim is a long-lived-until-claimed environment, not a unit of work with a lifecycle, retries, or an audit trail. Everything in our control plane (task state, scheduler, policy engine, approvals, cost ledger) would have to be built on top, unchanged in shape, with this project supplying only the pod underneath it.
- No automatic per-task identity: SandboxTemplate defaults automountServiceAccountToken to false (deny by default), but there is no mechanism to mint or bind a distinct ServiceAccount per claim. examples/sandbox-ksa/README.md shows this wired by hand, and roadmap.md lists dynamic per-claim identity association as Planned, not built. Our factoryd's automatic namespace+ServiceAccount-per-task is currently ahead of this project on the one axis the hint asked about most directly.

### microsandbox
- {'Single host, no daemon, one trusted operator': 'each machine keeps its own SQLite catalog (crates/db) of sandboxes, images and volumes, and there is no scheduler, queue or multi-tenant admission control. Adopting it as our worker-plane VMM would not remove the work of building fleet scheduling, per-task namespace/quota and bin-packing across our three Hetzner boxes; it would sit inside a pod, not replace one.'}
- Identity is a static, developer-held secret, not a task-scoped credential. Microsandbox stops a bound credential from leaking to the wrong host; it never mints a new, short-lived, audience-scoped credential per workload the way factory-gateway's projected ServiceAccount token does. Copying its secrets model without our own identity layer would be a step back on "identity belongs to the task, not the machine."
- {'No budgets, no cost ledger, no verification stage': 'entirely consistent with being one component rather than a loop, and confirms this project is a worker-plane idea source only, not a control-plane or cost-control one.'}

### NVIDIA OpenShell
- OpenShell has no task registry, budgets, verification or merge/deploy loop. It is a sandbox-plus-policy-plus-credential runtime, not a competing factory, so adopting it only ever answers the worker-plane and identity/policy questions, never the whole system.
- Its Kubernetes driver depends on an external CRD (kubernetes-sigs/agent-sandbox) and the docs warn Kubernetes accepts NetworkPolicy objects even when no CNI enforces them. Running it on our Cilium cluster is safe, but it adds a second policy authority next to our CiliumNetworkPolicy-in-Git model unless we pick one as canonical.
- Its default credential store is its own AES-256-GCM envelope database, a second secret store next to our OpenBao standard, unless we adopt its Vault-compatible credential driver, which we have not checked for OpenBao compatibility.

### Kiro
- On the IDE and CLI, the agent runs with the user's own ambient credentials and Kiro's own docs push isolation onto the user (separate profile, container, dedicated account) instead of the product. Copying this would break our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle, which keeps the sandbox credential-free by construction.
- Kiro is single-tenant SaaS with cloud sandboxes fixed to us-east-1 and no exposed control-plane source. Nothing here is adoptable as infrastructure, only as a format or UX reference. It fails our commodity-hardware and open-source-with-escape-hatch constraints outright.
- Cost control is a monthly credit pool per seat or org with one overage toggle, not a per-task hard cap with a database-locked hold and a real-cost ledger booked afterward. Copying it would remove the per-task and per-project budget envelopes our design treats as load-bearing.
- Kiro's own docs call generated property tests evidence for correctness, not formal verification, and the whole tier is optional and IDE-only. Our verification plane assumes cheap deterministic checks run before any model judgement, so a Kiro-style property tier would have to sit above that floor, not replace it.

### Superpowers
- Every rule here is prompt text enforced by rhetorical pressure ('Iron Law', tables of rationalizations to reject) and by the model's own willingness to comply, with no code-level backstop. This is the exact opposite of our 'policy lives outside the model' principle: nothing in Superpowers stops a model that decides to skip the skill, and none of it is a substitute for gateway or network-policy enforcement
- The unit of isolation is a git worktree on the operator's own machine, not a sandboxed, credential-scoped pod. There is no default-deny egress, no per-task identity, no untrusted-input boundary. The human partner and the codebase are both implicitly trusted. Adopting the workflow wholesale would still leave our worker-plane isolation problem completely unsolved
- The whole design assumes one synchronous human partner narrating a single interactive session, with 'ask your human partner' as the default escape hatch throughout. Our factory is built for autonomous, asynchronous, many-tasks-in-parallel operation where a human is a rare tool call; several of Superpowers' stopping rules need 'ask' rewritten as 'escalate_to_human and move on to other tasks' before they fit us

### Cleric
- Identity is one long-lived credential per integration, supplied once and reused for every investigation, the opposite of our per-task, short-lived, audience-scoped token; a compromised key stays valid for every future investigation, not one task.
- Cleric never runs or edits code in a sandbox. It only reads existing systems and proposes a PR, so it sidesteps our whole worker-plane and sandbox-isolation problem instead of solving it; copying its shape would mean permanently limiting agents to diagnosis and suggestion.
- Closed-source SaaS with no self-host path. Taking a dependency on it, or copying its exact design as a component, would put part of our telemetry-to-fix story permanently outside the re-seed-from-Git and commodity-hardware constraints.

### Datadog Bits AI (Dev Agent and SRE)
- Identity is one persistent, org-wide GitHub App install, GitLab personal access token, or Azure DevOps service principal shared by every session and every repo, not a credential minted and scoped per task. It is the opposite of our 'identity belongs to the task' principle, and a leaked or over-scoped install compromises every repo it touches, not one task's blast radius.
- There is no self-host path at any tier, including the government cloud sites, which the docs explicitly exclude. The whole product is welded into Datadog's own hosted telemetry, billing, and identity systems, which rules it out entirely against our re-seedable, commodity-hardware constraint, not just as a style difference.
- Cost control is a shared, consumption-based AI Credits pool metered after usage (Datadog's own telemetry puts the average investigation at about 6.5 credits, roughly USD 6.50 on an annual commit), not a per-call priced hold taken before an action runs. It bounds a month's spend, not a single action.

### Latitude
- Latitude never runs the fix. It is a trigger and context provider that hands a prompt, sample trace ids and a deep link to a coding agent product the customer already owns (Claude Code cloud routines, Cursor, a generic webhook) and stops. There is no sandbox, no egress control, and no task-scoped identity for that step, because Latitude does not hold the step. Adopting this would mean giving up our whole worker plane for whatever agent product the customer already pays for.
- The replay-and-check step is a documented pattern the customer wires into their own CI, not an enforced gate inside a durable workflow. Latitude supplies the dataset, the SDK and the scores API; pass or fail is whatever pipeline the customer built around them. Our reproduction gate is a workflow activity that blocks merge on its own; theirs is a recipe.

### Sentry Seer
- Seer is a single purpose, SaaS only, closed core pipeline bolted onto one existing product; it never has to be a general task system for arbitrary work across arbitrary repos and forges the way our factory does.
- Cost control is a flat per seat subscription with no visible hard per task spend cap, which only works because a large customer base absorbs the variance; our per task, per day, per project budget with holds exists because we have no such base.
- The gate before opening a PR is a confidence score, not test evidence; we require a reproduction gate, fails at base and passes at head, because we treat that evidence, not a probability estimate, as load bearing before a human reviews the diff.
- Where visible, execution identity borrows the initiating human's own OAuth token, the GitHub Copilot handoff, rather than a credential scoped to the task itself, the opposite of identity belongs to the task.

### ChatDev (DevAll)
- No sandbox. Python and function nodes shell out via subprocess.run with the parent process's full environment (functions/function_calling/code_executor.py, runtime/node/executor/python_executor.py). Copying this breaks our default-deny, per-task sandbox constraint outright.
- Single shared API key for the whole backend (.env BASE_URL/API_KEY), no per-task identity or capability policy. Any node can do whatever the process can, the opposite of policy living outside the model.
- No durable task state. A session is a folder under WareHouse/<session>/, not a workflow engine row with audit trail. Fine for one desktop user, not for a re-seedable, audited factory.
- The unit of exchange between nodes is a chat message, not a merge-ready artifact. That fits its general-purpose scope but is the exact shallow-verification pattern independent research found broken for coding tasks.

### CrewAI
- CrewAI has no sandbox, no network policy and no credential brokering anywhere in the codebase. A Task's tool is plain Python that runs in the calling process with the calling process's full environment (crewai_core/tool_credentials.py copies os.environ verbatim into tool subprocess calls). Adopting CrewAI as a harness would not reduce our sandboxing or identity work by one line; every tool call would still need to go through our own gateway and gVisor pod.
- security/security_config.py states its own intent plainly: 'Authentication credentials *TODO*', 'Scoping rules *TODO*', 'Impersonation/delegation tokens *TODO*'. Policy enforcement outside the prompt does not exist in the open-source library; the commercially load-bearing half (CrewAI AMP: RBAC, audit trails, governance, webhook triggers) is entirely proprietary SaaS reached only through a 'book a demo' page, with no pricing, no self-hosted technical detail and no security specifics published. That fails our 'open source with an escape hatch' bar the moment governance is actually needed.
- The Crew abstraction is autonomy-first: agents role-play and can enter a hierarchical manager's multi-round delegation loop with no deterministic path guarantee. Independent 2026 reviews (SimilarLabs, AgileSoftLabs) describe this trading away debuggability and cost predictability at scale, agents can 'enter extended deliberations' that burn budget with no built-in cap. That is the opposite bet from our supervisor-plus-hard-budget model, which exists precisely to bound that failure mode.

### LangGraph and LangGraph Platform
- The execution unit is shared mutable state (channels merged by reducers) read and written by every node in one process, not artifacts handed between isolated workers. Using LangGraph as a worker-plane substrate would mean giving up per-task process and network isolation.
- No identity-per-task or credential brokering exists anywhere in the library. A tool call inside a node inherits whatever ambient credentials the hosting process holds. Any task-scoped token model has to be built entirely outside LangGraph, which is what our design already does.
- The business model splits an MIT loop from an Elastic-2.0 server: the CLI's own production path (langgraph build/up) pulls the proprietary langchain/langgraph-api image and, at scale, a commercial LangSmith key. That is the open-core gate our constraint on open source with an escape hatch warns against; the escape hatch is to never run their server and write a thin one ourselves, since the library needs nothing but Postgres.

### MetaGPT
- No sandbox anywhere. ExecuteNbCode runs a Jupyter kernel and tools/libs/shell.py calls subprocess.run(shell=shell) directly in the same process as the framework. Reusing this worker code means wrapping it in a sandbox first, not adapting it.
- Identity is one static provider API key in ~/.metagpt/config2.yaml, read by every role in every run. There is no task-scoped credential to copy, only a pattern to strip out before reuse.
- Policy is whichever Python functions are registered in tool_registry and whichever of them the LLM's free-text command the role parses (parse_commands in role_zero.py) happens to name. Nothing enforces a capability outside that parse step.
- Cost control is a single in-process float compared once per round (Team._check_balance), not a pre-call hold. The budget can already be blown by the time it is checked.

### OpenAI Agents SDK
- It is a stateless client library embedded in your own process, not a service. Anything durable across restarts is the app's problem, which OpenAI's own community example proves: examples/sandbox/extensions/temporal/temporal_session_manager.py and temporal_sandbox_agent.py wrap the SDK inside literal Temporal workflows and activities. That is our factoryd-plus-Temporal shape already; the SDK gives us nothing to replace there.
- Identity and credentials are entirely the embedding application's problem. SECURITY.md states plainly that 'applications choose credentials, providers, tools, storage, and execution permissions' and that the SDK trusts the host app to isolate untrusted execution. Using it buys zero task-identity machinery, the opposite of our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle.
- The reference local sandbox backend (UnixLocalSandboxClient) runs commands directly on the host with no OS-level confinement, and the docs say outright that 'a session does not itself guarantee OS-level confinement' -- isolation is whichever backend you pick. If any agent of ours ever used SandboxAgent we would have to hard-forbid that backend and always force Docker/E2B/Daytona, the opposite of safe-by-default.
- Tracing ships on by default and exports to OpenAI's own hosted platform.openai.com/traces dashboard unless you call set_tracing_disabled() or register a custom processor, and it is unavailable entirely under OpenAI Zero Data Retention accounts. A vendor-coupled default that conflicts with our no-vendor-specific-service constraint until rewired to a custom exporter.

### Lovable
- Lovable puts almost no policy between the model and the generated backend: default Supabase schemas without Row Level Security plus client-embedded anon keys caused CVE-2025-48757 (170+ apps with world-readable databases) and an April 2026 BOLA flaw in Lovable's own API that exposed other users' projects and credentials for 48 days. Their trust model assumes a human reviews the generated code, but the product is sold to people who cannot; copying their speed would cost the policy-outside-the-model and default-deny posture that is one of our four constraints, not an implementation detail
- The whole product assumes one human is present and driving continuously: chat, preview and 'publish to a live URL' are the same button for the same person, with no separate approval role and no staged rollout. Our factory is triggered by an issue with no human present, and treats merge as a gated workflow state. Adopting Lovable's immediate-publish loop for anything but a human's own live editing session would break the human-approval guarantee in our merge and deploy plane

### Replit Agent
- A Replit project always has exactly one human watching one chat session. There is no issue, alert or telemetry entry point anywhere in the product, so Replit never had to build unattended approval, escalation or audit machinery for work nobody is currently watching. Copying its UX would not give us anything for the signals plane, which is most of what our design has to solve that Replit does not.
- The sandbox has no documented default-deny egress. An app builder has to let the agent install packages and call third-party APIs the built app itself needs, so open-by-default networking is close to a product requirement for Replit, not an oversight. Our default-deny-with-allowlist worker plane is the opposite trade and we would not adopt theirs.
- Cost control is a hard credit cap that the agent's own failures eat into: a Capterra reviewer wrote that most of their credits go to checkpoints for errors in a loop, and other users report 70 USD in one night from repeated fix attempts. Our gateway prices the worst case and holds funds before the call; Replit's model punishes the user after an expensive loop rather than bounding it before one starts.
- Fully proprietary, cloud-only, and even the Enterprise tier is a Replit-managed single-tenant GCP project, never software you run yourself. Nothing here is re-seedable or portable to commodity hardware; only the product ideas transfer, not any code, since there is no repository to read.

### Dagger and container-use
- container-use has no control plane at all: no task queue, no scheduler, no multi-tenant identity, no audit trail, no budget. It assumes one human driving one laptop's Docker daemon for one or a few agents at a time.
