Copying its model wholesale would mean giving up the entire reason our control plane exists (durable state, policy, unattended operation across many tasks and projects); it solves a narrower problem, local multi-agent git hygiene for one developer, not a fleet-scale factory.
- Isolation is a plain container from Dagger's BuildKit-based engine (runc-level namespaces and cgroups), not a hardened sandbox, and nothing in environment.go, config.go or the MCP tool surface sets up a default-deny network policy: a container can reach the internet unless the base image or the surrounding network happens to block it. Our default-deny egress and gVisor pod are the more expensive, deliberately stronger answer to the same worker-isolation problem; matching container-use's isolation level would be a regression against our own security-without-half-measures constraint.

### Depot
- Depot draws its trust line at fork PR versus same-repository PR: same-project builds share a writable cache and full builder access, and only fork PRs get an isolated, cache-less builder. Every one of our builds is agent-authored code from an untrusted branch by definition, so we could never adopt Depot's default; we would always be in their fork-PR case. This does not change our design, it confirms why our own build cache is already read-only for everything except trusted main.
- Depot's coding-agent sandbox hands each session a standing org-level secret (CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN or ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, plus GIT_CREDENTIALS for non-GitHub remotes) that sits inside the sandbox for the life of the session and is reused across sessions. That is the exact standing-credential shape our identity belongs to the task, not the machine principle exists to remove; adopting Depot's sandbox for our own agent execution would put a long-lived model key and a long-lived git credential inside every worker.
- Depot's control plane (billing, scheduling metadata, secret storage, session state) is closed source and stays with Depot even on the Business self-host tier, which only relocates compute into the customer's AWS account. Taking a dependency on Depot for build or sandbox infrastructure would put a piece of our loop permanently outside the re-seed-from-Git-and-S3 boundary, unlike our own OpenTofu-provisioned, throwaway build VM.

### Namespace
- Namespace sells compute, not policy. Nothing in its docs evaluates what an agent or job may do once it has a runner; RBAC and SSO gate the dashboard for humans, not tool or network access for a running workload. Adopting Namespace would not remove a single line of our own capability-policy or gateway code; it is a worker-plane hardware vendor, not a control-plane substitute.
- There is no self-hosted or BYOC mode. Both Namespace's own docs and an independent competitor review (Northflank, July 2026) agree the whole product runs exclusively on hardware Namespace owns and racks itself. That is a hard conflict with our re-seedable, commodity-Hetzner constraint: we could at most rent it as an optional CI accelerator for one project, never fold it into infrastructure we can delete and rebuild from Git and S3.
- Egress control is an allow-listable stable outbound IP range (plus enterprise dedicated pools), open by default until a customer configures a filter. Our sandbox network policy is default-deny, opened only to specific hosts a task's capability grants. Their model optimises for 'let CI reach package registries without a support ticket'; ours optimises for 'an agent reaches nothing it was not explicitly given'. Namespace's per-workspace filtering is a coarser, opt-in version of the same idea, not an equivalent.

### Nx Cloud Self-Healing CI
- Nx Cloud sells shared multi-tenant compute plus a credit-metered SaaS AI fix, with no documented default-deny egress and no capability policy scoped to the agent itself. Its launch templates (read `.nx/workflows/agents.yaml` in this repo: `apt-get install`, `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile` against public mirrors) assume open outbound access. Copying this would mean giving up policy-outside-the-model and identity-per-task, because their business model needs an agent that can freely reach package registries inside a shared cluster, the opposite of our default-deny sandbox.
- The actual self-healing intelligence is closed source. The OSS repo ships only thin plumbing: `nx-cloud apply-locally`, the `nx fix-ci` CLI stub, and an `nx mcp` command that just runs `npx nx-mcp@latest` (packages/nx/src/command-line/mcp/mcp.ts), a separate proprietary package. There is no self-host escape hatch beyond bringing your own Claude API key to their closed orchestration loop, which conflicts with our 'open source with an escape hatch' constraint.
- Two real supply-chain compromises in 13 months are directly relevant to our threat model: s1ngularity (Aug 2025, a poisoned pull_request_target workflow leaked the npm publish token, malicious nx/@nx/* versions harvested credentials from 1,079 developer machines and ran locally installed Claude Code and Gemini CLI with --yolo/--trust-all-tools to hunt for secrets) and the Nx Console v18.95.0 VS Code extension compromise (May 2026, traced to a stolen contributor gh token from an unrelated TanStack npm compromise, which cascaded into a GitHub employee device compromise and roughly 3,800 exposed internal GitHub repos, CVE-2026-48027). Both confirm why our sandbox holds no key material and identity is projected per task instead of living on a developer or CI machine.

### Greptile
- Every rule, severity judgement and learned convention lives inside the model prompt. There is no enforcement in code or network policy behind any of it. Copying this wholesale for anything that gates merge or spend would break our policy-lives-outside-the-model principle. Fine only as advisory input to our own gate, never as the gate itself.
- There is no task or session concept, each PR is a stateless review request against a cached index, not a durable multi-step worker. Nothing resembles per-task identity, budgets or an audit trail, so it is not a model for our control plane or identity layer, only for the narrow review step.
- The default posture sends code to OpenAI's and Anthropic's hosted APIs and opts code into model training unless a customer turns that off. Self-hosting with your own model requires a sales conversation, not a documented self-serve path. Matching our no-vendor-specific-service, own-the-box stance means only the enterprise BYOC tier is usable, and that tier is undocumented in public.

### Plannotator
- Plannotator's whole mechanism assumes a synchronous, attended session: a hook blocks the agent's own process until a human acts in a browser on the same machine. Our worker plane is disposable and headless, with no human display attached during a run and approval delivered as an async Temporal signal; we cannot adopt the blocking-hook mechanism itself, only the artifact format it produces.
- Every trust decision in Plannotator collapses to 'the reviewer is the developer sitting at this machine': no sandbox, no per-task identity, no capability policy, because the human IS the thing being protected against nothing. Our trust boundary is the reverse (the agent is the untrusted actor), so the review surface has to live in the control plane we run, not next to the agent.
- Team collaboration is deliberately kept out of the open-source core and pushed to a proprietary hosted SaaS (Workspaces, waitlist-only, lifetime-free-access carrots for contributors). That open-core split is fine for a product monetising collaboration; it is not a model we can lean on, since our multi-user approval flow has to be fully ours, in the console, with no hosted fallback.

### PR-Agent
- PR-Agent has no loop and no state. Every command is one LiteLLM completion call of about thirty seconds, triggered fresh per webhook, with no task registry, no retries across sessions and nothing to resume. It maps to a thin, optional slice of our verification plane and to none of our worker, control or merge and deploy planes, so its architecture cannot be adopted wholesale, only its prompt and config techniques.
- There is no control plane and effectively no trust boundary. Any comment on an open PR can trigger a run (the only gate is DefaultIdentityProvider.verify_eligibility, which unconditionally returns ELIGIBLE in the open-source build) and credentials are static long-lived API keys and a GitHub token in repository secrets, not per-task identity. Copying this as-is would reintroduce exactly the raw-credential, ungated-trigger problem our identity-per-task and policy-outside-the-model principles exist to avoid.

### Aider
- No sandbox anywhere. aider/run_cmd.py runs suggested shell commands with subprocess.Popen(shell=True) directly on the host, gated only by a confirm_ask prompt the user can switch off globally with --yes-always. Fine for a human running their own machine, disqualifying for an unattended worker under our default-deny egress rule.
- No durable task state. A session lives in process memory and the git log; nothing survives a crash beyond commits already made, and there is no resumable workflow or audit trail. Matching aider's simplicity here means giving up the recoverability our Temporal-backed control plane exists for.
- The user's own long-lived provider API key is passed straight to the process that edits files (flag, env var or ~/.aider.conf.yml), and PostHog/Mixpanel telemetry is on by default. Both are the opposite of "identity belongs to the task" and "policy lives outside the model."

### Plandex
- No sandbox at all: commands run with exec.Command(shell, -c, scriptPath) directly in the user's own working directory (app/cli/lib/apply.go), with only an optional systemd cgroup scope for resource cleanup on Linux (apply_cgroup_linux.go), no filesystem jail, no network restriction. Our whole worker plane exists to prevent exactly this.
- Single-user, single-machine trust model: whoever runs the CLI holds the provider API key in an env var and can approve any auto-mode. There is no separate identity for the agent, no capability policy layer, and RBAC (app/server/db/rbac_helpers.go) is only org owner/member for a human team, not agent permissions.
- No budget or spend cap anywhere in the code (checked db/data_models.go, db/models.go): cost is left entirely to the user watching their model provider's own dashboard.

### DBOS
- DBOS has no sandbox, no egress control and no per-task credential concept. It durably executes your own trusted service code, not code an LLM agent just wrote. We cannot adopt it as a worker-plane substitute; at most it is a library our own trusted processes could use.
- Identity is an app-supplied string (authenticated_user, assumed_role columns) carried through workflow context, not a short-lived, audience-scoped token verified by a gateway. Adopting DBOS's model as-is would break 'identity belongs to the task, not the machine.'
- The parts that would make DBOS a full control plane (Conductor's dashboard, pause/resume/restart, multi-executor fleet view) are proprietary, licensed per seat, and phone home for licence checks with no source access. Our 'open source with an escape hatch' constraint would be violated the moment we wanted that UI.

### Hatchet
- Workers authenticate with one long-lived HATCHET_CLIENT_TOKEN per tenant (docs/self-hosting/worker-configuration-options.mdx), stored in a Kubernetes secret and shared by every worker process. That is the opposite of our per-task, audience-scoped, kubelet-rotated ServiceAccount token; using Hatchet's own client auth as our identity layer would put a standing credential inside every agent sandbox.
- A Hatchet task is just a function your own worker process runs. There is no sandbox, no egress control and no concept of a disposable per-task environment anywhere in the engine. Hatchet would only ever be a Temporal alternative in our stack; it does not reduce anything we build for the worker plane.
- Its authorisation model is dashboard RBAC for humans (OWNER/ADMIN/MEMBER/VIEWER), not runtime policy for agents. Nothing in the engine evaluates whether a task may call a given tool or reach a given host, so adopting Hatchet would not remove the need for factory-gateway sitting between a worker and the network.

### Inngest
- Inngest orchestrates calls into code that runs entirely on the user's own infrastructure; it provides no sandbox, no network egress control and no credential broker for that code. Function auth is one pre-shared HMAC signing key per environment, not a short-lived, task-scoped credential. Adopting Inngest's execution model would not give us any of the sandbox_isolation or identity_per_task guarantees we require; we would still have to build the entire worker plane ourselves on top of it.
- The unit of work is a short HTTP request/response step, with a bounded per-step duration, replayed from the top of the function on each step boundary using memoized results. That fits request/response backend jobs well but is a poor match for a long-lived, stateful gVisor agent session waiting on a human approval for hours; Temporal's worker-polls-task-queue model, which already anchors our control plane, fits that shape better.
- There is no concept of policy, capability or approval gating anywhere in the engine; SSO, audit trails and RBAC are Enterprise-tier SaaS features, not code paths in the open server. Our capability-rules-in-Git model has no equivalent to adopt from here.

### Restate
- the programming model is inverted from ours: Restate calls into HTTP-registered handlers and replays their journal server-side, where our factoryd workflow is in-process Go code that Temporal's SDK replays locally; adopting Restate would mean restructuring the control plane around inbound HTTP handlers, not swapping one library for another
- BUSL-1.1 is not OSI open source; self-hosting our own production use is explicitly permitted by the Additional Use Grant, so it would still clear our open-source-with-escape-hatch bar, but Temporal's plain MIT licence is the strictly better fit for the same constraint, so there is no licence reason to prefer it
- Restate only ever covers the durable-execution slice of one plane; it has no signals, sandbox, verification, policy or identity concept at all, so it could replace only the state and replay half of what factoryd plus Temporal already does, never factory-gateway, gVisor or the policy engine

### Temporal
- Every 2026 Temporal agent integration (OpenAI Agents SDK, Google ADK, Vercel AI SDK) puts the agent loop itself inside Temporal workflow and activity code, one Activity per model call and per I/O tool call. Our harness interface runs the coding agent (opencode, later Codex CLI) as an opaque external CLI subprocess we never rewrite. Copying Temporal's fine-grained pattern would trade "swap the harness binary" for "reimplement the agent loop against one specific SDK," a different worker plane, not an upgrade to the current one.
- Worker Versioning's Pinned mode assumes a workflow finishes inside one deployment's lifetime; a long Pinned execution blocks that deployment from draining, which Temporal's own docs admit. Our task workflows can wait three days at zero cost for a human, so Pinned-by-default would fight our own workload shape; Auto-Upgrade avoids it but keeps exactly the patching discipline we already carry.
- Self-hosted Temporal ships no RBAC and no audit logging; both are Temporal Cloud extras or a "build it yourself" line item on Temporal's own production checklist. That matches our principle of keeping policy and audit outside the workflow engine, so it costs us nothing, but it means Temporal itself will never become our policy or audit layer.
- Several of the exact features this report was asked to focus on (Workflow Streams, External Payload Storage, Standalone Activities, Nexus for TypeScript/.NET, serverless Lambda workers) are Public Preview or Pre-release as of 2026-09-29, not GA. Adopting them now means tracking a moving API.

### codebase-memory-mcp & Sourcebot
- Both tools bind trust to the caller, not the task. CBM has no identity at all and Sourcebot authenticates a human user, so a short-lived, task-scoped ServiceAccount token has nowhere to plug in. Exposing either through our gateway means our gateway, not the tool, has to become the place that maps a task to a capability and an audit line
- Sourcebot's default licensing path needs a recurring outbound Service Ping to Sourcebot's own licence server to keep paid features alive, a permanent vendor-specific dependency our no-vendor-service constraint is written against. Staying on the free Basic plan avoids it but drops code navigation and Ask Sourcebot

### Ralph (the Ralph Wiggum technique)
- Ralph's worker model has no sandbox, no network policy and no scoped identity, because it runs the CLI tool with its permission checks turned off directly on the operator's own machine, with the operator's own credentials. Disabling the CLI's own permission system is the load-bearing move that makes an unattended loop possible at all. We cannot adopt any part of this without breaking 'security without half measures', it is not that Ralph is ahead of us here, it is that Ralph only works because one trusted developer is watching their own laptop.
- Ralph is deliberately monolithic: one process, one task per loop, and Huntley argues multi-agent orchestration is premature complexity ('a red hot mess'). Our design is the opposite bet, many disposable workers behind a durable control plane, because we need concurrent tasks across many projects with budgets and approvals. Adopting Ralph's stance would mean giving up the fan-out our factory exists to provide.
- Verification in Ralph is the same agent grading its own homework: it decides which checks to run and sets its own completion flag. Our verification plane exists specifically to avoid this (independent CI on untrusted runners, then a separate stronger-model review). Copying Ralph's self-graded model anywhere would reintroduce the exact failure Hacker News critics name as the technique's weak point: an agent presenting a wrong answer as correct with nobody independent checking.

### Dapr Agents
- No sandbox: tools and MCP calls run in the same long-lived process as the agent, not in a disposable per-task container. Incompatible with our default-deny-egress, one-sandbox-per-task worker plane.
- Identity is Dapr mTLS plus declarative component scopes, granted to a whole running app deployment, not minted per task. A compromised agent process can reach everything its app-id is scoped to for the deployment's lifetime.
- Policy is Python code (the Hooks) shipped inside the same repository as the agent, not a separately governed, GitOps-applied document. A single PR can change both the agent and its own guardrails.
- No cost control anywhere: no budgets, spend holds or a cost ledger. Dapr's resiliency policies (retry, circuit breaker, timeout) are about reliability, not spend.

### LiteLLM
- LiteLLM philosophy is maximal, one project now also covers a2a, OCR, RAG, realtime audio, assistants, batches, fine-tuning, vector stores, images and video, none of which we call. Running it means that whole surface sits inside our trust boundary for the slice we use, which is what 2026 CVE run actually hit.
- LiteLLM owns its own Postgres schema and migration job for keys, budgets and spend, a second source of truth our gateway must poll and settle against rather than a single ledger we own outright.
- Real per-task identity (mapping a caller own JWT to a key) is an Enterprise-only feature, so a design that wanted identity belongs to the task through LiteLLM own auth alone could not get it on the free tier. We solved this by never handing LiteLLM the task real identity at all.

### Portkey AI Gateway
- Budget enforcement is a stub in the open-source repo. The hook point PreRequestValidatorService (src/handlers/services/preRequestValidatorService.ts) reads c.get('preRequestValidator') and does nothing if it is unset; nothing in src/ ever sets it. Real spend caps live only in Portkey's closed, centrally hosted Control Plane. Our hard-cap-with-holds design has to be real in code we own; Portkey's open gateway cannot be that on its own.
- The conf.json integrations[].rate_limits field is accepted but never read anywhere in src/ (grep finds no consumer). It looks like enforced policy and is not, which is the opposite of our 'policy lives outside the model, enforced in code' principle: here policy sits in a config file that nothing enforces.
- Even the 'self-hosted' enterprise deployment keeps virtual keys, configs, prompts and the transaction database on Portkey's hosted Control Plane, synced to the VPC-side gateway every 60 seconds with a 7-day local cache TTL (per portkey.ai/docs/self-hosting/hybrid-deployments/architecture). That is a permanent external dependency our 're-seedable, no vendor-specific service' constraint rules out.
- Identity is a static API key or virtual key resolved from that hosted Control Plane, not a short-lived, audience-scoped, task-bound token. There is no equivalent of our per-task ServiceAccount token verified against a cluster OIDC issuer.
- The project is now owned by Palo Alto Networks (closed 29 May 2026, about $117M per PANW's 10-K) and points at Prisma AIRS. A neutral open-source dependency became a security vendor's product line in the same week its main-branch commits stopped.

### Claude Squad
- No sandbox at all. The chosen CLI runs as the developer's own OS user in the worktree directory, with the developer's full network access and ambient credentials. Git worktrees separate code, not machines, processes or network. Our gVisor-pod-per-task and default-deny egress is a hard requirement claude-squad has no equivalent for.
- No task identity, no control plane, no cost ledger. State is one local JSON file; credentials are whatever gh, git and the model CLI already have configured on the host, shared by every instance. Fine for one trusted developer on one laptop, incompatible with identity belongs to the task at any multi-project or semi-autonomous scale.
- Single-user, single-machine by design. It solves one developer runs several coding agents from one terminal, not a signal comes in and a reviewed, deployed change comes out with minimal human involvement. Verification, merge policy and deploy are left entirely to the developer's own judgement and a manual gh push.

### Conductor
- No sandbox, no policy layer, no per-task identity. The agent runs with the full authority of the developer's own Mac, their own long-lived GitHub token and their own model API keys or subscription. That is the opposite of "identity belongs to the task" and "policy lives outside the model", and it only works because the trust boundary is one already-trusted developer's own machine, not an autonomous agent started by anyone who can file an issue.
- There is no control plane. Conductor is a UI shell over existing harnesses (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, OpenCode) with local app state, not a durable task registry with budgets, approvals or an audit trail. It solves the worker-plane UX problem only; it has nothing to say about signals, verification policy, cost, or deploy.

### Crystal
- No sandbox of its own. Every agent process runs on the developer's own machine with their own filesystem and network. Isolation is a different worktree directory, not a security boundary. Copying this would break our sandbox_isolation and policy_outside_model principles outright, it only works because Crystal assumes one trusted developer on their own laptop.
- No control plane. State is a local SQLite file plus a Bull queue in the same Electron process, and there is one identity, the developer's own CLI login, for every session. There is no durable task state that outlives the app, no multi-tenant scheduling, and nothing to audit across users.

### Google Antigravity
- Identity is the developer's own machine and account for every action. There is no per-task credential, scoped or otherwise, the opposite of 'identity belongs to the task, not the machine.' Nothing here transfers to our per-task ServiceAccount design.
- Verification is self-verification: the same agent that wrote the code also drives the browser and narrates the walkthrough. There is no independent reviewer model and no CI gate before a human sees the result, which is weaker than our stronger-model review plus reproduction-gate, not stronger.
- Sandboxing runs on the developer's own machine and is opt-out: a 'Turbo' preset removes it entirely, and Secure Mode (the strictest preset) was bypassed in the wild through a tool-level RCE. A viral incident also has the agent running a drive-wide delete instead of a scoped cache clear. Our default-deny gVisor pod has no all-or-nothing 'trust everything' toggle to disable.
- Nothing here is re-seedable or commodity-hardware hostable. It is a proprietary desktop client wired to Google's own models, with a forced breaking upgrade (2.0 silently removed the code editor for existing users with no rollback) that our 'delete everything and rebuild from Git and S3' constraint exists precisely to survive.

### Happy
- Happy trusts a device once. QR pairing binds a phone to a running terminal session indefinitely. We need identity bound to a task and a short-lived credential, not to a paired device. Copying their pairing model would give us persistent human-to-agent trust where our design wants ephemeral, revocable, per-task trust.

### Orca
- Zero credential brokering. Orca holds no keys and reads the agent CLI's own local auth state (for example ~/.claude), so the agent runs with the user's full ambient identity. This is the opposite of 'identity belongs to the task'; we could not adopt it without giving up per-task scoped tokens.
- No sandbox by default. Isolation is a git worktree (directory separation only) on the same OS, same user, same network. The optional SSH/cloud-VM modes are bring-your-own and Orca provides no sandboxing itself. Copying this would remove our gVisor-pod, default-deny-egress boundary entirely.
- Best-of-N is human-judged with no automated evidence and no cost accounting: N full agent runs, a human reads N diffs and picks one. Layering this on our metered LiteLLM gateway as-is would multiply real spend by N with nothing for the budget ledger to reason about.
- No durable server-side task state. State is a local orca-data.json plus an experimental in-process orchestration layer (Run/Task/Dispatch). There is no re-seedable-infra story because there is no shared infra: it is a single-user desktop tool, not a multi-tenant control plane, so its architecture answers a different problem than ours.

### Arize Phoenix
- Phoenix ships its own embedded AI assistant (PXI, src/phoenix/server/agents/) that holds long-lived provider API keys as server-side environment variables or in a self-hosted secrets store (model_factory.py reads OPENAI_API_KEY, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, GEMINI_API_KEY and similar directly) and can reach GitHub and the open web (github.py, web_access.py) on its own account, not a per-session token. That is the opposite of our identity-belongs-to-the-task principle; copying the embedded-copilot idea would force it through factory-gateway's per-task virtual keys instead of a static server credential.
- License is Elastic License 2.0: source-available, not OSI-approved, and it explicitly forbids offering the software to third parties as a hosted or managed service. Fine to run internally, but not the escape-hatch kind of open source our stack constraint asks for if a component like this ever became load-bearing rather than a reference.
- Cost is computed and displayed everywhere but never capped: no hold, no per-task or per-day budget, nothing blocks a call. Treating a Phoenix-style ledger as equivalent to our cost-control principle would quietly drop the hard-cap half of it.

### Braintrust
- Braintrust's self-hosted Enterprise plan only moves the data plane (API, Postgres, Redis, object storage, the Brainstore trace engine). Auth, org settings and the web UI always stay in Braintrust's own SaaS control plane, linked by a service token. That is the opposite of re-seedable: no self-hosted deploy can be rebuilt from Git and S3 alone.
- Brainstore's stated Kubernetes footprint (one dedicated node per pod, NVMe with at least 150000 IOPS) is priced for an enterprise fleet, not three cpx42 boxes.

### Future AGI
- Default-allow egress: the nsjail sandbox passes -N (network allowed) for both Python and JS evaluator code, and falls back to a plain, unsandboxed subprocess.run when nsjail is missing (futureagi/code-executor/server.py, lines 361-364). Our default-deny-egress, gVisor-pod-per-task model exists specifically to rule this out; adopting their shortcut would reopen the exfiltration path our design closes.
- Cost control is check-then-deduct, not hold-then-book: agentcc-gateway/internal/plugins/credits/credits.go blocks a request only if balance is already <= 0 before the call, then deducts the real cost afterwards. The ForceDeductMicros post-request path has a comment admitting the balance can go negative between the pre-check and the deduction ('spend must be recorded even if balance dropped to zero between pre-check and now'). Our hold-then-book model reserves the spend before the call precisely to avoid this race; theirs is simpler and admits the trade-off in its own code.
- Open-core gating sits inside self-hosted deployments, not just around the cloud offering: LICENSE-EE covers the ee/ directory (voice-agent simulation, agenthub specialized evaluator agents, some scanners), and code there needs a paid Enterprise subscription for production use even when self-hosted. Our one-licence, no-holdback stance means every self-hosted capability is actually available, at the cost of not having an enterprise-tier revenue lever.
- Their five-stage PR review pipeline, including a second-model check, governs contributions to their own product, not an end-user-facing 'agent opens PRs against your repo' capability. It is a development-process pattern we could borrow the shape of, but it is not the verification plane our factory needs: it never touches a customer's codebase.

### Harbor
- Harbor has no signals plane and no durable cross-run control plane. It is a batch evaluation tool that scores a fixed task set once, not a system that ingests live issues or telemetry and carries task state, approvals and audit across time. At most it supplies a verification-plane component, not an architecture to copy wholesale.
- Harbor Hub's default direct credential mode injects real provider API keys straight into the agent sandbox. Scoped gateway-proxy credentials are opt-in. This is the opposite of identity-per-task with no direct credentials, and would need to be forced off by us if we ever touched the Hub itself.
- No hard spend cap exists anywhere in the OSS CLI. Cost is computed and reported after the fact via LiteLLM pricing, never gated before or during a run, so our own spend-limit enforcement gets no help from this project beyond the cost-ledger arithmetic.

### Helicone
- Identity is a long-lived static API key (sk-helicone-...) proxied through a central vault that decrypts provider keys server-side, the opposite of our per-task, audience-scoped, no-raw-credentials model. CVE-2026-85178 (a missing organisation filter in the vault's key-lookup endpoint) shows the failure mode: any org admin could read another tenant's plaintext OpenAI or Anthropic key.
- There is no task or workflow concept. Helicone observes calls an application already decided to make; it has no scheduler, no approval state and nothing resembling our control plane. Adopting it adds a log and cost sink next to our ledger, not a replacement for any plane.
- The AI Gateway's core proxy is a Cloudflare Worker using Cloudflare KV and Durable Objects for rate limiting (worker/src/lib/rate-limit, durable-objects). Self-hosting it ties the gateway to Cloudflare-shaped primitives, in tension with our commodity-hardware, no-vendor-specific-service constraint.

### Laminar
- Laminar is a metered multi-tenant SaaS with billing woven deep into the schema (four places that must stay in sync per tier, Stripe webhooks, per-workspace soft and hard usage limits). Our control plane bills no one; copying this stack would import a billing domain we do not need.
- The one feature that overlaps our supervisor design, Signals, is not in the Apache-2.0 tree. "app-server/src/signals/mod.rs" compiles a no-op "public.rs" unless the "signals" cargo feature links "signals/private", which the code comments say typically lives in a separate private repo, named directly as "lmnr-private" in "docs/internal/auth.md". We would have to build the equivalent ourselves regardless, which our design already plans to do.
- It has no task, sandbox, or credential model at all. It assumes the watched workload runs and is secured elsewhere, so nothing here substitutes for our worker plane, policy engine, or per-task identity. At best it is a bolt-on to the signals plane, and only for visibility, not enforcement.

### Langfuse
- Open-core licensing with a commercial carve-out: ee/, web/src/ee/ and worker/src/ee/ are gated by a separate Enterprise licence, and the free self-hosted 'oss' plan is missing project-level RBAC roles, audit logs, admin API and UI customisation until a 'self-hosted:enterprise' licence key is applied (web/src/features/entitlements/constants/entitlements.ts). Our best-of-breed-open-source principle rules out ever putting core factory capability behind a licence key ourselves.
- Storage sprawl by design: Postgres, ClickHouse, Redis/Valkey and S3-compatible storage for one plane, with traces written to S3 first and only a reference queued in Redis before ClickHouse ingestion. That buys load-spike durability but is four stateful systems for a slice of what we run on three servers; we would pick one store, not copy the stack, unless trace volume forced our hand.
- Long-lived, project-scoped API key pairs and org/project RBAC roles, not short-lived task-scoped credentials. Fine for a human dashboard, but the opposite of identity-belongs-to-the-task; nothing in Langfuse's own auth model is portable to how our agents authenticate.

### LangSmith
- LangSmith is downstream observation, not a control plane: it holds no task registry, no scheduler and makes no decision about what an agent should do next. It cannot replace factoryd or Temporal, only sit beside them the way SigNoz does.
- The entire backend, storage engine (SmithDB) and UI are closed source, and both self-hosting and BYOC are Enterprise-only, gated by a license key that phones home to beacon.langchain.com for validation unless run in an offline mode. That conflicts with our open-source-with-an-escape-hatch and fully re-seedable-from-Git constraints: a component we could not run at all without a live vendor relationship and a sales contract.
- Self-hosting needs Postgres, Redis and ClickHouse (or the early-access SmithDB) plus 16 vCPU/64GB minimum, a fourth and fifth stateful service on top of our three-cpx42 budget, for a slice of functionality SigNoz already covers for infra telemetry.
- Identity is a workspace API key or a human login (SSO on Enterprise), not a short-lived credential scoped to one task; adopting LangSmith as infrastructure would mean it sits behind our perimeter as an internal tool, never a boundary we hand external trust to.

### Opik
- Opik has no task registry, scheduler or workflow engine anywhere in the code. It answers "what happened" after the fact; it never decides what an agent should do next. Adopting it changes nothing about our control plane, it is purely an add-on next to SigNoz.
- Authentication is off by default in the open-source build (authentication.enabled defaults to AUTH_ENABLED:-false in apps/opik-backend/config.yml, basicAuth is false in the Helm values) and, when turned on, delegates to a proprietary "React service" that is part of Comet's own platform, not shipped in this repository. Self-hosting Opik does not give you real multi-user identity or RBAC; that only exists on Comet's cloud. This directly contradicts our "identity belongs to the task, not the machine" principle, so Opik can only ever be an internal tool behind our own perimeter, never a component we point external trust at.
- The self-hosted stack needs five datastores side by side (MySQL for state, ClickHouse plus Zookeeper for analytics, Redis for queues, MinIO for blobs). That is heavier than the slice of it we would actually want (traces and eval scores), and is a real capacity cost against our three-cpx42 budget if we ran it as a fourth stateful service next to Postgres, Temporal and SigNoz.

### Cedar
- Cedar's full safety story lives in Rust. The Go port drops the schema validator, templates and partial evaluation, so getting all of it needs a language boundary our single Go module does not otherwise have.
- Cedar is a stateless decision function with no policy storage, versioning, approvals or budgets of its own. It would replace one line inside the gateway, not the machinery factoryd already owns.

### Composio
- Composio's product is breadth: 1,500+ pre-integrated apps reached by centralising every customer's OAuth tokens in one multi-tenant credential store. Our principle is the opposite, identity belongs to the task and no standing credential exists anywhere the agent can reach. We could use Composio only as an optional third-party tool catalogue behind our own gateway, never as a place to let real credentials live.
- Composio's May 2026 breach (disclosed 2026-05-21: about 5,241 API keys and 5,001 GitHub OAuth tokens taken from internal systems, one account tracing it to a compromised internal agent's remediation tool reaching code execution in their own execution sandbox) is close to the exact failure our sandboxing and default-deny egress exist to make structurally impossible: one compromised internal path exposing every customer's long-lived tokens at once. Our per-task gVisor pod holds no key material and is destroyed after use, so an equivalent compromise cannot reach another task's credentials by construction.
- A Composio 'session' is an app-integration execution context (which SaaS apps can this call touch, with which stored token), with no concept of a git branch, a PR, a task registry or a merge gate. It solves 'let an agent act on Gmail, Slack or GitHub' as an end in itself; it has no path to becoming a task orchestrator, only a candidate tool-and-auth layer bolted onto our worker plane for calling third-party SaaS apps.

### Cycles
- enforcement is cooperative, not inline. the agent's own SDK or MCP call chooses to invoke reserve/commit; nothing stops code that skips it from reaching the provider directly. Our network-level default-deny egress makes that bypass structurally impossible, which is the whole point of policy living outside the model
- Cycles never prices a call. the caller supplies estimate.amount in whatever unit it likes. Our gateway is the one place that knows the model price list and clamps to actual free headroom, so a hold means something even if the agent is wrong or lying about cost
- identity is a static, long-lived per-tenant API key issued once via the Admin API, not a short-lived, task-scoped, audience-bound credential. adopting this as-is would break identity belongs to the task, not the machine
- the ledger's only backing store is a single Redis instance with no documented HA or clustering story. acceptable for a budget component, not for a system of record, which is why our ledger sits in CloudNativePG Postgres instead

### Invariant (Guardrails, Explorer, Gateway)
- Invariant has no task, identity, sandbox or budget concept. It covers only the content and behaviour slice of "policy outside the model," so at most it is a library called from inside factory-gateway, never a stand-in for our control or worker plane.
- Its enforcement key is a second static API key alongside the real provider key, not a short-lived per-task credential. Copying its gateway model as-is would reintroduce the long-lived-secret problem our projected ServiceAccount tokens specifically avoid.
- The company folded into Snyk about a year after founding and killed its only hosted product eight months later. Treat this project as reference material to learn a DSL shape from, not as a dependency to run.

### iron-proxy
- default routing is DNS-interception, which their own docs call easy to bypass from inside the workload; our Cilium default-deny is enforced below the workload at the CNI and cannot be opted out of from inside the sandbox, so iron-proxy would have to sit behind that perimeter, never replace it
- identity is per-proxy-instance or per-fleet-principal, not per-task: nothing issues, verifies or scopes a credential to one task the way our OIDC-verified projected ServiceAccount token does
- the part that would matter at our scale, central policy push and aggregated audit across many proxies, lives behind the separate 'iron.sh' product, sold or self-hosted as 'source available' with no licence stated, so we would only ever take the Apache-2.0 binary, never that layer

### Agent Governance Toolkit
- AGT's own docs admit the policy engine and the agent share one process boundary, and that a bypass exists whenever code calls an unwrapped model or tool reference directly, so it recommends the operator add container isolation itself. That is the architectural opposite of policy lives outside the model: we assume the agent process is untrusted and cannot be relied on to invoke its own guard, and enforce at the gateway and the network instead. We would only ever use AGT as an in-process linting layer, never as the security boundary, without breaking our security without half measures constraint.
- Identity is agent-scoped and long-lived (a DID plus an Ed25519 keypair, SVID TTLs configurable up to hours) rather than task-scoped and freshly minted. The SPIFFE/SPIRE integration is documented as production behaviour but the code in agentmesh/identity/spiffe.py fabricates SVIDs locally with comments reading in production, this would request an SVID from the SPIRE server, and docs/LIMITATIONS.md separately admits AGT does not revoke credentials at task boundaries within a session. Copying this would reintroduce the identity belongs to the machine pattern we deliberately rejected.
- AGT is a horizontal guardrail library, not an orchestrator: it has no signals plane, no build or test verification plane, and no merge or deploy plane. It assumes a pipeline already exists and drops governance calls into it. Three of our five planes are simply not comparable to anything in this project.

### Anthropic Sandbox Runtime
- The isolation boundary is an OS namespace (bubblewrap, Seatbelt) or account-scoped ACLs (Windows), not a kernel-level VM boundary like gVisor. Our own security constraint chose gVisor over namespace sandboxes for exactly the failure mode independent researchers later found in srt (see section 8); adopting srt's boundary on our worker plane would be a downgrade even though its per-path and per-domain policy language is richer than what we express today.
- It is a single-host, single-process fence with no durable state, no scheduler and no task identity. It answers how to wrap one command, not how to run one task under one disposable credential across a cluster, so only the fencing primitives are portable, not the shape of the system.

### Daytona
- Isolation shares a kernel across sandboxes: Linux namespaces plus a hardened container runtime (Daytona's own CVE-2026-31431 advisory names Sysbox), driven straight through Docker/containerd (apps/runner go.mod, pkg/docker). No gVisor, Kata or Firecracker anywhere in the source or docs. We pay the gVisor systrap tax, and plan microVMs, specifically to avoid the shared-kernel failure mode that CVE stressed.
- 'Self-hosted' means bring-your-own-runner while Daytona's control plane still coordinates everything; the org, billing, auth and scheduling layer was never something you could run yourself, even before the freeze. Our 'delete everything and re-seed from Git, S3 and one offline bundle' constraint has no equivalent here.
- Daytona went closed source specifically to deny attackers a copy of its isolation code, betting secrecy beats review for a security boundary. Our whole design bets the opposite: policy and mechanism live in Git, in the open, enforced in code. Worth naming as a live test of the opposing bet, not something to copy.

### E2B
- E2B answers only the worker-plane question; copying it replaces one plane at most and leaves signals, verification, merge/deploy and control-plane task state entirely ours to build
- its real production topology (autoscaled node pools, external Postgres/Redis/ClickHouse, GCS/S3, historically Nomad) is heavier than our three-server shape, and is not itself published; Embed is explicitly a single-node evaluation package, not a deployment pattern
- the security feature regulated users care about most, orchestrator-ee's secret resolution, is closed source, which breaks our policy-lives-in-auditable-code principle if we ever depended on it directly
- Firecracker needs /dev/kvm: bare metal or nested virtualization, which our Hetzner Cloud servers do not expose, the same fork in the road our own target-architecture.md already names for its own microVM tier, not a new cost E2B introduces

### Edera
- The part that actually works (Edera Protect: daemon, CRI, zone image management, installer) is closed source and sold per node on a sales-negotiated, undisclosed price; adopting it for the worker plane reintroduces the single-vendor, opaque-billing dependency we avoid everywhere else
- Edera isolates by replacing the node's boot and kernel layer (Xen dom0 baked into the node image); our gVisor sandbox needs zero node changes. Adopting it means giving up Talos's minimal, extension-only, immutable model for a custom Xen-enabled kernel image with no official Talos integration, not a drop-in RuntimeClass swap
- Edera is built and priced for Fortune-500 and federal multi-tenant and GPU isolation at enterprise scale; our project is a three-node, roughly 210 EUR/month commodity setup, a budget tier Edera's go-to-market does not appear to serve

### Fly.io Sprites
- Egress is open by default; ours forbids by default. Their DNS-allowlist mechanism is portable, their default is not.
- A Sprite is a persistent, reusable machine with save points; our sandbox is disposable per task, recreated from git plus findings. Copying checkpoint/restore does not require copying persistence-as-default.
- No task, approval or policy-version concept exists at all. It can only ever replace the pod/VM under one task's sandbox, and only if we hosted on Fly.io rather than Hetzner and Talos, which the no-vendor-lock-in constraint rules out.

### Modal Sandboxes
- The whole product is a closed, cloud-only SaaS with no self-hosted or BYOC path; the execution engine, scheduler and file system are proprietary and run only on Modal's own infrastructure. This is the direct opposite of our re-seedable, commodity-hardware constraint. Nothing here can be adopted as infrastructure, only copied as a design pattern.
- No task or workflow concept, the same gap as every other sandbox-runtime project reviewed: an App and a Sandbox are a compute primitive, not a unit of work with retries, approvals or an audit trail. Taking Modal on, hypothetically, would still leave 100 percent of our control plane (task state, scheduler, policy, budgets, cost ledger) to build ourselves.
- The execution environment is defined at runtime through Modal's own Image API (a Python builder chained onto modal.Image), not an arbitrary Dockerfile or OCI image. Northflank's review calls this out as inflexible next to E2B or Daytona. It is a vendor-specific coupling our no-vendor-specific-service constraint rules out even before the self-hosting question comes up.

### BMad Method
- No sandbox, no network egress control, no per-task identity anywhere in the design. BMad assumes a trusted human is present in the same coding session and workspace for every step. It is a planning and building co-pilot, not an unattended worker. None of its mechanisms could be adopted inside our worker plane without breaking the "security without half measures" constraint, because there is nothing there to adapt.
- Coordination is one continuous persona-plus-shared-files session in the user's own checkout, not disposable workers exchanging artifacts. This is the direct opposite of "agents exchange artifacts, not chat", BMad's whole value proposition is a long-lived conversational identity (Amelia, Winston, Mary) that carries context turn to turn.

### OpenSpec
- OpenSpec enforces nothing at runtime. Every rule ("write a proposal first", "use deltas") lives only in a prompt or skill file a model is free to ignore, the opposite of our "policy lives outside the model" principle. Its spec format is safe to borrow, its trust model is not.
- Specs are optional and advisory with no automatic check that code matches spec ("enablers not gates"). Our reproduction gate needs acceptance criteria to be machine-checked, not just human-read, so we would have to make specs authoritative in a way OpenSpec deliberately avoids.

### GitHub Spec Kit
- no sandbox, no policy outside the model, no per-task identity, because it assumes a human approves every command; that premise cannot survive an unattended factory
- fully synchronous, one-command-at-a-time UX by design, the opposite of human attention as an escalation

### Tessl
- Tessl's only enforcement mechanism is prompt context: rules carry alwaysApply: true and are loaded into the agent's context window, with nothing checking that the agent actually followed spec-before-code or that a chat message really was an approval. This is the opposite of our 'policy lives outside the model' principle; adopting it as a real gate would mean a prompt alone decides whether code gets written.
- The whole workflow assumes one human live in a chat with one agent at a laptop, asking one question at a time and answering in the same session. It has no notion of a disposable per-task worker, a task queue, or a credential scoped to a task, so it cannot be lifted into our headless, one-shot sandbox model without being rebuilt as something else.
- Tessl itself walked back from being a spec-driven orchestrator: it paused Tessl Framework development and removed Framework functionality from a rebuilt CLI in 2025, then repositioned in January 2026 around a skills package manager and governance registry. The spec-driven tile we analysed sits stale since 2026-03-30 while the company's real investment went into skill governance, which we read as market evidence that spec-driven methodology alone is a feature, not a product, for this category.

## Verdict lines

| Slug | UX verdict | Slop verdict |
|---|---|---|
| aws-autonomous-coding-agents-sample | CLI-first, with real watch, nudge and scoped-approval primitives that are ahead of a typical chatbot loop, but nothing for a human who does not want a terminal. | Real and actively engineered (22 contributors, roughly 900 merged PRs in six months, design docs that name the exact files they describe), but read the phasing tables before quoting the architecture diagram, much of the governance story (tiered PR-risk validation, team budgets, DNS-firewall enforcement, AgentCore Identity token vault) is marked Next, Later or Future, not shipped. |
| cursor-cloud-agents | Far ahead of our console in reach and live session detail, web, mobile PWA, Slack, IDE, API, mid-session follow-ups, remote-desktop takeover, but recurring outages and undisclosed per-minute VM costs undercut it. | Real and used at multi-billion-dollar revenue scale, but its frontier Composer 2 model was an undisclosed fine-tune of a Chinese open-weight model until a public callout, and Bugbot publishes a resolution rate rather than precision or recall. |
| stripe-minions | The lowest-friction intake seen in this survey, tag a bot in a Slack thread you are already in, but nothing public shows how a run is watched or how one time-boxed sandbox is inspected mid-flight. | Real and used in anger. A named engineering manager and a named head of AI platform put figures behind it, an independent conference talk did a live demo, and Hacker News engaged critically rather than dismissing it as vapor. The gap is disclosure, not substance, cost, model choice and credential handling are simply not published. |
| gitlab-duo-agent-platform | A real, embedded UX with flow sessions, checkpoints and Approve/Reject/Modify cards inside the issue or merge request that started them, but the feature surface is fragmented across dozens of separately flagged doc pages and add-ons, the marketing site shows no real product screenshots, and Reddit users comparing it to Claude Code still call its raw interaction quality worse even while praising the integration. | Real and used in anger. It reached general availability in GitLab 18.8, drove a company-wide pricing change (GitLab Credits), and GitLab shipped and documented a response to an actual remote prompt-injection exploit reported by Legit Security in 2025. It is not README-slop, but several flagship governance features (the AI Governance Dashboard, some trigger types, webhook callbacks) are still beta or explicitly "not ready for production use", and the open-core gating is aggressive. |
| claude-code | The best-documented developer-facing agent product surveyed. Every mechanism in this report has an exact, versioned, dated public doc page; our own console has nothing comparable. | Real and heavily used, not slop. Live commit activity across all four repos on the day of writing, a $2.5bn run-rate business, and enterprise data show over 1,000 customers spending $1m or more a year on it. One asterisk: the flagship repo (148k stars) ships no CLI source at all, only docs, plugins and examples under an all-rights-reserved licence, so "open source" claims about Claude Code from the star count alone are wrong. |
| anthropic-managed-agents | Four API calls, agent, environment, session, stream, reach a working sandboxed coding agent in every major SDK language, and the chat framework quickstarts render an inline tool approval gate out of the box. Faster time to first result than our console, but it is a developer experience to build a UI against, not a ready dashboard to watch and intervene from. | Real and used in production, named customers, a maintained cookbook repo and a maintained reference skill repo, but still beta five months after launch, ineligible for zero data retention and HIPAA, and self-hosted sandboxes cover far less of the stack than the name suggests. |
| devin | The live multi-pane session view and DeepWiki onboarding beat our console on every point we admit weakness on, but the scheduler and policy engine underneath stay invisible SaaS internals. | Real, funded and in enterprise use, with actively maintained integration tooling, but the clearest public security evidence found this session is a documented prompt-injection exploit against it, not the compliance marketing. |
| factory-ai | Far ahead of our console. Desktop app, CLI, Slack, Linear, mobile and a Mission Control view with live worker logs and a pause button, first result in about five steps. | Real, heavily funded and shipped almost daily, but closed. Security and deployment docs promise more than a buyer can check, and the benchmark and customer claims are self-reported. |
| codegen | Trigger from Slack, Linear, GitHub or the web, watch a live trace, talk to the running agent in the same thread, open its editor or preview, a clearly smoother loop than our console | A real, paid product with named customers that was retired on 2026-01-16 after the ClickUp acquisition, the open SDK is an archived 100-line wrapper over a generated REST client |
| coleam00-ai-software-factory | Zero-GUI, agent-mediated setup that is unusually coherent for a text-only flow, but nothing in this repository shows a live view of a run, only terminal output and the eventual pull request. | Real and actively worked for a one-month-old repository, but thin. Most of what makes it function is an external pinned dependency, not code in this repository, and the two places that name the pinned revision already disagree with each other. |
| esf | CLI-first and CLI-only for every action; a read-only web UI shows evidence but cannot approve, intervene, or show a live stream of what the agent is doing. | Real, tested, and unusually well-documented for a ten-day-old project, but it is a one-person effort with no merged external contributions and the project's own docs say plainly that no production deployment is qualified yet. |
| fabro | CLI, web UI, SSH into a live sandbox, Slack and an MCP server give a smoother zero-to-first-result path and better live visibility than our console, but human intervention is limited to the choices a workflow author wired as edges. | Real and actively self-developed, not a landing page, but a one-person project (about 88 percent of commits) whose load-bearing engine lives in unlicensed sibling repositories tied to a newly funded, separately run company. |
| factorykit | Zero-setup SaaS onboarding (GitHub App plus Linear/Jira/Slack, describe a task, watch live narration, review a PR with an attached QA recording) is more polished than our functional-but-undesigned console, which has no live stream and no video evidence today. | Real and dogfooded in production (runs Hashnode and Bug0 itself), from a funded, multi-year team, but the platform is entirely closed and unverifiable, and three rebrands in under seven months with no security or architecture documentation page is a genuine stability red flag. |
| fusion | A real kanban board, a live Command Center dashboard and native desktop and mobile apps make watching and approving an agent's work several steps easier than our server-rendered console, which by our own admission has no real-time streams, no diff view and no mobile view. | Real and heavily used by its own author, roughly 14,000 commits and over 3,000 test files in five months, weekly releases, and one independent source-level audit that mostly corroborates the docs. The same evidence also shows one person shipping very fast with light review, and the architecture doc's long list of narrow race-condition postmortems reads more like continuous firefighting than settled design. |
| loki-mode | One command (loki quickstart) gets a running sample app in minutes with a cost quote shown before any spend, and the CLI surface is far richer than our console for a single developer on a single repo, but there is no multi-project, multi-user, approval-queue view of the kind our console exists to provide. | Real and used in anger by its own author (803 npm releases and thousands of commits in nine months, working signed-receipt and forgery-defense tests, an unusually honest limitations doc), but the marketing has outrun the code before, a January 2026 Hacker News post claimed 99.67% on SWE-Bench with no independent verification and drew no outside engagement, and the changelog for 2026-09-28 admits a release gate shipped broken the day before this research. |
| machinist | a competent local, config-first tool (TOML and Markdown, no GUI authoring) with one small polling dashboard (2 second refresh) that shows task and run state, token use and duration, and puts approve, cancel and a retry-safety checkbox on the same page, but zero to a first PR still means cloning, building a Go binary, running init, and hand-writing a command on the CLI | not slop, it is a real and carefully built tool (fsync-safe state, no-shell subprocess handling, regex input allow-lists, 30 test files, SECURITY.md that admits its own lack of sandboxing) actively developed by one engineer at high velocity, but it is early access, unreviewed outside its own ecosystem, and its own maintainer has an open pull request (#494) to delete the GitHub issue intake this report describes |
| open-inspect | A fast, multiplayer web app with a live terminal, a diff view and Slack, Chrome-extension and in-sandbox VS Code front doors beats our console's complete absence of real-time streams or diffs today, at the cost of nine required setup steps (fork, cloud credentials, GitHub App, secrets, Terraform deploy, web deploy, verification, owner bootstrap) before the first prompt, against our "open a URL" onboarding. | Real and actively used, not a landing-page repo. Continuous shipping with a dated changelog, over 2,100 merged PRs in eight months and 3,296 stars, but almost entirely the work of one maintainer (Cole Murray, 1,316 of roughly 1,600 commits) with an explicit "single-tenant only, trusted organisation" security disclaimer, so treat it as one person's production system rather than a hardened multi-tenant product. |
| open-swe | More invocation surfaces than our console (Slack, GitHub, Linear, dashboard, CLI, desktop) and a genuine small-diff fast-approval flow, but the docs ship no dashboard screenshots and the project is not yet seeking outside users. | Real and heavily engineered, not a landing page, but it is LangChain's own internal tool released as reference code rather than a community project, GitHub Issues are off and there is no CONTRIBUTING.md. |
| openai-symphony | Strong idea, weak surface. The tracker (including its mobile app) is the UI and a workpad comment shows progress, while Symphony's own dashboard is read-only. | Real and used inside OpenAI, but a deliberately thin prototype. The orchestrator is honest code, the safety and done-ness live in a long prompt, and OpenAI says it will not maintain it as a product. |
| openhands | The web UI's live terminal, diff view and conversation screen beat our console today, but "zero to first result" now means installing a local Node 24 agent-server rather than opening a URL. | Real, large, funded and actively shipping, with a responsibly disclosed CVE and working Kubernetes sandbox code, but a disruptive rebrand about four months old and unverified enterprise cost-control claims make current marketing fluid. |
| optio | Broader interface surface than our console (web, CLI, iOS, Android, Slack) but a multi-step self-hosted setup and screenshots that lag the current feature set. | Real and actively maintained by one dominant author, with genuine CI and a detailed changelog, but its docs contain a broken cross-repo reference and its "secure self-hosted" pitch overstates the shipped defaults. |
| paperclip | Fast and polished to first result (one command, or npx test-drive for zero commitment), with a designed UI (Radix, Tailwind, Storybook baselines) well ahead of our console, but the ease comes from skipping the sandboxing and review gates we treat as non-negotiable. | Real, large and actively used, not a landing page. But twelve published GitHub security advisories, including one CVSS 10.0 unauthenticated RCE, landed in a single batch on one day, which reads as a post-hoc security audit bolted onto explosive growth rather than security built in from the start. |
| sortie | a zero-account, six-step local demo and a live read-only dashboard, but no approval or diff-review surface, since every run is unattended by design | real and shipping weekly with a heavier-than-usual test suite, but the bus factor is thin, one person authors every architecture decision and most of the commits |
| swe-af | SWE-AF has no dedicated interface beyond one API call and the pull request it produces at the end. CodeAF's terminal UI, a cross-project task tree, a needs-you queue resolved with one keypress, and phone access over SSH to a session running on another machine, is the more developed and more transferable half of this project's UX. | Not slop. Both repos carry real, tested code, Go and Python unit tests in both, and SWE-AF's deployment doc lists known issues tied to live GitHub issue numbers, the kind of detail a landing-page project does not bother to write down. SWE-AF rewrote its core from Python to Go, and CodeAF merged over 1650 pull requests in under two months, though CodeAF is explicitly labelled early preview and is close to a one-person effort so far. |
| tembo | Ahead of our console on live session streaming, redirect and cancel, and chat-native entry points, but behind us on transparency since its queueing and prioritisation logic is unpublished marketing language. | Real and actively shipped, with daily commits, a live changelog and a genuine funding and pivot history, but its deepest architecture claims sit behind gated docs and NDA'd audits, and its own 1267-star flagship repo was quietly wiped to a single commit. |
| github-agent-hq | Best-in-class session UX, live steering, natural-language session search, multi-surface reach, well ahead of our undesigned console. | Real and actively maintained, but "Agent HQ" is a marketing umbrella ahead of the shipped surface. Only Claude and Codex are confirmed third-party agents. Jules and Devin remain unconfirmed despite the October 2025 announcement naming them. |
| centaur | Zero to a verified result is five commands with no UI step (docs/pages/quickstart.mdx), and day-to-day intervention is a chat reply in the thread the agent already streams into, better than a console for watching and steering, but Console itself (services/console) is only a permissions and workflow-run admin panel with no task board, diff view or cost view. | Real and used in anger: 59 contributors, commits most days including five within a few hours the day before this report, named internal workflows (github_issue_triage, trivy_vulnerability_intake), third-party forks by other companies, and a documented four months of internal production use before release, against two open gaps: no cost governance at all, and an unresolved licence inconsistency between the repo, GitHub's API and the product site. |
| coder-tasks | Fastest onboarding in this comparison set for a human pairing with one agent (open a chat, type a prompt, watch a live diff), but the thing being reviewed keeps changing under the user: three architectures (Tasks, Coder Agents, Agent Relay) shipped or deprecated in 2026 alone. | Real, funded, and in production at named enterprise customers (KKR, QRT), but the product itself churns hard. Tasks shipped, was deprecated within roughly a year, and was removed from new releases on 2026-09-01, the same week a critical (CVSS 9.0) supply-chain compromise of Coder's own module registry (GHSA-vx42-ghc9-gw65) shipped malicious Terraform modules to self-hosted deployments. |
| kelos | a browser Console with a live per-Session chat, tool-call trace and diff tab, plus a first-party agent skill that removes hand-written YAML entirely, gets a user from zero to a watched, running task faster and with a richer live view than our console offers today | real, dogfooded on itself daily and shipped on a near-weekly release cadence, not a marketing shell, but it is a single maintainer's side project with no company, no funding and no disclosed external production adopters, so treat it as a high-bus-factor bet rather than a supported product |
| opensandbox | SDK, CLI and MCP quickstart reach a running command in about five lines once a server and one image are pulled, faster onboarding than our console flow, but there is no web UI at all; watching or steering a live sandbox means execd's terminal-share and SSE streams, not a page a human opens. | Real and fast-moving, not a landing page - 3,225 commits and 30 tags in under three months of releases, every architecture-doc claim checked against working code - but the neutral opensandbox-group branding is ahead of the facts (Alibaba names still fill the packages, module path and one registry), and adoption evidence outside Alibaba's own Lingyang business is self-reported evaluation, not confirmed production. |
| temps | A standard Sentry-style error list is one click from a project dashboard, but the analyse/fix/PR buttons and any live reasoning view live one screen deeper than the screenshots this survey captured, and the flow is undocumented outside the source: it appears in no public doc page and the word autofix appears nowhere in the README or the homepage. | Real, huge and committed to daily, not a README with a landing page, but it is an 11-month-old, pre-1.0, effectively one-person project (2070 commits against 47 for the next human) with zero independent reviews found anywhere and one public user report of undocumented rough edges. |
| google-adk | A solid single-developer inner loop (adk web, adk run, adk eval, adk deploy) but no fleet view, nothing shows several agents or tasks at once, and human-in-the-loop is a paused chat turn, not an approval queue. | Real and actively used, not a landing page. The 1.x to 2.0 graph rewrite (May 2026) broke prior agent code and several 2026 reviews still call parts of the new stack beta and Google-Cloud-flavoured. |
| gh-aw | The best onboarding in this category for GitHub users - one Markdown file, a wizard, a first issue in about ten minutes - but no dashboard, and a 1,400 to 2,600 line generated YAML per workflow that few people will review. | Real and used in anger. GitHub runs about 300 agentic workflows on the repository itself and merged about 580 PRs in September 2026, much of it agent-written, with high churn and one critical credential-leak advisory in August 2026. |
| coderabbit | Installed once, a review appears as an ordinary PR comment within minutes of opening or pushing, with one-click-apply suggestions and no separate dashboard to learn, but large or unusual diffs draw noisy comments that independent reviews say take a team one to two weeks of .coderabbit.yaml tuning to quiet | Real and heavily used, most-installed AI app on GitHub and GitLab, 50M USD ARR by mid-2026, 223M USD raised, but an independent 2026 benchmark scored it 1 of 5 on completeness and 2 of 5 on depth, and its own commit-status integration can report false "success" while rate-limited |
| agent-memory | Both beat our console on visibility into agent memory with no extra UI work from us, but neither adds an approval or intervention step a human can act on. | claude-mem is real, heavily used code with careful engineering, but its star count is hard to trust given its embraced crypto token and a CRITICAL prompt-injection report still open nine days on. Letta Code is thin on independent scrutiny at eleven months old, but is funded, MemGPT-lineage code, not a landing page. |
| cline | The strongest thing in this report. A kanban board with parallel worktrees, checkpoint diffs, inline comments and phone access over Tailscale beats our console on every UX axis we admit to being weak on, but it assumes a trusted single operator, not our multi-project, multi-human approval model. | Real and heavily used (69.5k stars, funded, enterprise customers named), but the 2026 security record (a supply-chain compromise plus two critical WebSocket RCEs) shows the "let the model judge risk, run on the host" model failing in production, not just in theory. |
| gemini-cli | A fast, well-documented terminal agent with real undo (rewind, checkpoint) and a clean headless JSONL contract, but strictly single-session and single-project, with no multi-task board or approval queue of its own. | Real and heavily used, over a hundred thousand stars and daily commits, Google's own issue triage runs on it, but its trust boundary has failed at the maximum severity rating at least once in production, so treat "battle-tested" and "secure by default" as separate claims. |
| goose | onboarding to a first result is about five steps for one developer on one machine, better than our console for that single-user case, but there is no multi-task board, no team approval queue, and no audit trail beyond a local session log. | real and heavily used (54.8k stars, LF governance, near-weekly releases, ~59 provider/auth source files), not a README with a landing page, but three CVEs disclosed in the last month for unsandboxed command execution show the ask-unless-the-mode-says-otherwise model has exploitable gaps, and Block's own pre-launch red team compromised a real employee laptop. |
| openai-codex | The CLI is a one-line curl install to a TUI with ChatGPT sign-in, and the same JSONL event stream feeds VS Code, JetBrains, a web app and Slack/Linear triggers; our console is behind on live streams, diffs and mobile, but none of Codex's surfaces are ours to operate or brand. | Real and heavily used, not a landing page. 154 Cargo crates, multiple tagged releases a day, a 22 KB AGENTS.md the team uses to run itself. The same pace is the risk: wire_api chat and codex mcp-server were both removed within the observation window, so pinning a version and re-testing on every bump is mandatory. |
| opencode | faster to a first result than our console (one binary, a TUI in seconds, no cluster) and richer to watch live (TUI, a mirrored web UI, opencode attach, shareable session links), but none of that surface is a governed, queued, cost-tracked task the way our console's kanban view is | real, heavily used and actively maintained (210k stars, 43 releases in three months, commits the same day as this research), but several evocatively named directories are hollow next to their names, and the vendor's own stated security posture, plus three real 2026 advisories, are a genuine caution flag |
| swe-agent | CLI-only, config is a hierarchical YAML plus flags, a Textual/HTML trajectory inspector for after-the-fact review; fine for a single ad hoc fix, no fleet view, no live approval flow, nothing our console needs to match. | Real, peer-reviewed (NeurIPS 2024) and widely used historically (20k stars), but the maintainers themselves call SWE-agent maintenance-only and point everyone to mini-swe-agent; treat this report as a design sample of a 2024 approach, not a maintained target, and read mini-swe-agent as the live one. |
| aws-bedrock-agentcore | Two CLI commands get a single hosted agent running and streaming traces to a CloudWatch dashboard, but there is no equivalent of our kanban board, human lane or approval workflow, because AgentCore has no task concept to show one for. | Real and heavily used in production, 771 stars, weekly releases, AWS reports over two million SDK downloads since preview, but the open source framing is cosmetic. The repo takes no external pull requests and the services it wraps are entirely closed. Several 2026 security findings, including a Code Interpreter sandbox network-isolation bypass, show the deterministic security marketing gets ahead of the shipped controls in places. |
| google-vertex-agent-engine | A five-step SDK or CLI quickstart (adk deploy, or client.agent_engines.create) reaches a traced, running agent faster than our console's time to first result, and Cloud Trace call-chain tracing is reported clearer than competing clouds, but G2-sourced reviews describe days lost to service accounts and IAM roles, and there is no kanban-style view of running work to watch or intervene in. | Real, metered, billed production infrastructure since March 2025 with a steady GA cadence through 2026, but the pricing structure has changed at least three times in a year, the product has been renamed twice (Reasoning Engine to Agent Engine to Agent Runtime), and a March 2026 Unit 42 disclosure showed the default identity model handed any compromised agent read access to the whole GCP project and to Google's own internal container images. |
| microsoft-foundry-agents | Portal-first onboarding (create in ai.azure.com, test in a playground, call from code) is faster to a first result than our console, but it is onboarding into an Azure subscription, not a self-hosted product. | Real and heavily used. MIT-licensed SDK with weekly releases and hundreds of contributors, but it is the third Microsoft agent framework in three years (Semantic Kernel and AutoGen both now in maintenance mode) and the hosted protocol has already had one breaking rework (AgentServer 1.0 to 2.0) in months, so today's integration code is not guaranteed to survive the next one. |
| ona | Three steps from signup to a running agent in a browser IDE, a live conversation stream, mobile access and one-keystroke parallel launches, clearly ahead of our console on every dimension we already know we are weak on. | Real and heavily used (2 million developers historically, paying enterprise logos, SOC 2), but the open-source escape hatch is dead, most of what is worth copying is Enterprise-gated and unpriced, and the company is mid-acquisition by a single model vendor. |
| openai-frontier | sales-gated with no self-serve trial; a user reaches Frontier's agents through interfaces they already have (ChatGPT Enterprise, the Atlas browser, embedded business apps) rather than a dedicated console, so onboarding is a Forward Deployed Engineer engagement, not a quickstart | real, with named large customers (HP, Intuit, Oracle, State Farm, Thermo Fisher, Uber) and a services arm doing the integration work, but the public surface is entirely marketing copy and third-party guesswork; no docs, no API reference, no pricing and no way to verify a single mechanism claim independently |
| atlassian-rovo-dev | A CLI and VS Code extension with Claude-Code-like permission prompts, sessions, memory and worktree mode; its edge is Jira and Bitbucket context, not a better terminal experience. | Real and revenue-generating, not a landing page. Marketing oversells (SWE-bench Full quoted where rivals quote the harder Verified split) and two real, since-patched prompt-injection findings sit alongside it. |
| augment-intent | A checkpointed, living-spec workflow with a real diff, PR and merge view inside one app is ahead of our console's admitted gaps, but it is a single-developer macOS beta with no LSP, test runner or debugger, not a multi-user task queue. | Real and shipping, not a landing page, with a funded company and paying customers, but independent validation is thin. Two G2 reviews, close to no Hacker News or Reddit discussion of Intent specifically four months into its beta, and most security and performance claims trace back to Augment's own marketing. |
| blitzy | Fire-and-forget batch UX, submit a spec, get notified days or weeks later, with no live view of agents at work and no visible way to pause or redirect a running job. Weaker than our console's kanban board and worse than most other factory-orchestrator products in this survey. | A real, well-funded product (1.4B USD valuation, SOC 2 Type II, ISO 27001, named enterprise customers in press coverage) but its headline benchmark numbers are self-reported and were checked only by a firm Blitzy itself hired and pointed at 20 of 731 tasks; treat "record" SWE-Bench Pro scores as marketing until an independent party reproduces them. |
| charlie-labs | Two required steps to first use (GitHub sign-up, install the scoped GitHub App) and work happens inside tools people already use, which beats our console's own onboarding, but nothing we read describes a live view of an agent mid-run | Real and technically detailed, not a landing-page shell, but commercially dead. The company announced on 2026-09-21 that it is closing, with the service switched off entirely on 2026-10-05 |
