| continue-dev | Zero to a first agent response is three commands (install, cd project, run cn, log in or paste a key) and the TUI/headless split is clean, genuinely faster onboarding than our console, but nothing public shows a live multi-agent view or a stop button for a run already in progress | Was real and used in anger, 36k stars, YC-backed, paying enterprise customers, a genuine TypeScript agent runtime, not a landing page. It is dead now, acquired by Cursor in June 2026, repository frozen read-only, cloud data deleted after the 2026-07-15 export deadline, no future patches |
| cyrus | The live "workpad" stream and inline approvals are genuinely better than our console for Linear issues, but Cyrus quietly downgrades to a start-reaction plus one final comment for GitHub and GitLab issues, which is worse than the workpad the marketing implies. | Real and actively used, not a README with a landing page, 66+ releases on a near-weekly cadence, 26 contributors, a funded company selling a paid tier on top of it, and customer names on the homepage, but the security marketing (SOC 2 badge, "enterprise-grade") outruns a codebase that gives the agent the operator's own git identity. |
| gastown-beads | Ahead of our console on live observability, a merged multi-agent feed and a per-agent tmux session a human can attach to directly beat our lack of any real-time view, but there is no approval workflow before merge, only reactive nudge or restart of a stuck agent, and no screenshots exist in either repository to check any of this directly. | Real and used in anger, not a landing page, two live, git-versioned, issue-tracked codebases with dozens of releases, thousands of stars, real production incidents and real recovery docs. But the README overstates at least one safety claim the code contradicts, cost and isolation are left almost entirely to the user, and the creator's own memecoin episode and a still-open "who benefits from your tokens" dispute are real reputational costs separate from the code. |
| google-jules | A polished, multi-surface flow (web, TUI, CLI, API) built around plan, approve, diff, PR, ahead of our console on real-time visibility and diff review | Real and actively used with a fast changelog cadence, but the client tooling oversells its official status and the product's own FAQ still says Public Beta more than a year after Google announced it had left beta |
| kilo-code | Zero to a first suggestion is one CLI install or IDE marketplace click with no API key required, and a Cloud Agent session can be watched live in a browser-embedded terminal (ghostty-web) or streamed as JSONL from the CLI, both ahead of our console's static kanban view; but Cloud Agent shares one container across a user's chats, keeps up to 15 minutes per message with no mid-task pause, and its own docs warn that YOLO mode is always on | Real and heavily used (27k GitHub stars, 391 contributors, committed today, Anaconda states 3M-plus developers and about 10 trillion tokens a month routed through it), but the actual "factory" half of the product (Kilo Gateway backend, Cloud Agent orchestration, budgets) lives in the closed Kilo-Org/cloud repository, not in the MIT tree, was folded into Anaconda in July 2026, and a third-party breach at its analytics vendor Metabase exposed some users' Slack tokens and account or prompt data in August 2026 |
| strongdm-attractor | No shipped product to use, onboarding is pasting one README line into your own coding agent and waiting, the one real UX idea is that a DOT graph renders to a diagram for free, which our console still lacks. | Real, technically dense specs (about 5700 lines read in full) behind a viral manifesto, but the released repo has no code, no releases, four contributors and has been silent for six months, and the one thing they did ship as actual code (cxdb) was shown on Hacker News to have the review gaps the whole pitch is about. |
| sweep | Lower friction than our console for a single fix (comment "Sweep: fix the login bug" on an issue and watch a checklist update in place) but there is no fleet view, no cost view and no approval workflow beyond normal GitHub PR review. | Real and once popular (YC S23, seed funded, 7.7k stars) but abandoned as a factory-orchestrator. The README says the team is "now building an AI coding assistant for JetBrains", the org's repo list shows no meaningful backend activity since around September 2025, and self-hosting the old product still needs a licence key from a company that no longer sells it. Read every finding here as a snapshot of a 2023 to 2024 design, not a maintained target. |
| terragon | Fewer steps to a first running task than anything else in this survey (connect a GitHub App, type a task, pick a model, hit Execute), and the live checkpoint stream plus terry CLI handoff directly answer our console's admitted gaps, but the product never grew a place to review, gate or merge the work it produced. | Real and once paying, not a landing-page project (259 stars and 43 forks on the post-shutdown snapshot, a working npm CLI published for eight months), but it is dead. shut down 2026-02-09 for lack of traction, its own maintainers call the code an unmaintained as-is snapshot, and an independent write-up pins the failure on a moat thin enough for Anthropic's and OpenAI's own CLIs to absorb. |
| warp-oz | Ahead of our console today. CLI, a web app with a mobile view, API and SDK, Slack/Linear/Jira/GitHub triggers, live session links and a sub-agents tab beat our lack of real-time streams and diff views, but almost none of this has been exercised by independent users yet. | Real and shipping fast from a funded, revenue-growing company, with working Docker Hub images and enterprise docs, but seven months after launch it has one zero-comment Hacker News thread, no independent reviews beyond a sponsored newsletter post, and it has already renamed and half-deprecated itself. |
| linear-for-agents | Best-in-class transparent session lifecycle and activity trace for an issue tracker, but live intervention (stop, pause, redirect mid-session) is thin to absent in the published docs. | Real and heavily used (Linear claims agents installed in 95 percent of paid workspaces, 50 percent of issue volume agent-created), actively shipped, but the third-party Agent API is still a Developer Preview and its own docs defer the full object schema to a linked GraphQL explorer. |
| agyn | A strong per run trace view and a polished console beat our console's complete lack of one, but chat only intake with no kanban or diff view means it reads as a governed chat product, not a work tracking one | Real and shipping, the OpenZiti, egress and RBAC claims hold up in code, but small (three named contributors, no disclosed funding, no tagged releases) and its headline spend cap claim is not backed by the code |
| kagent | A real kubectl-native console (chat, dashboard, agent forms) but built for watching a conversation, not reviewing a diff; there is no PR or code-change view because kagent does not produce one. | Real and maintained (CNCF Sandbox, daily commits, deep internal architecture docs with file-level citations, an E2E suite), but young in hardening, its own docs admit an insecure default and a forgeable interim identity header. |
| agentgateway | nine steps and one curl from a fresh binary to a chat completion, then a spend dashboard and playground with no code, well ahead of the JSON-log-only feel of a pure proxy | real and heavily used, not a readme project: Linux Foundation and AAIF governed, 300+ contributors from AWS, Microsoft, Cisco and Red Hat, daily commits, four published CVEs/GHSAs each fixed within about one release cycle, fuzz targets and e2e UI tests in the tree; the linked Enterprise page is empty |
| bifrost | three commands to a first response (npx or docker run, open the web UI, curl a completion), and a genuinely built-out governance and analytics dashboard, but nothing to watch or approve an agent doing multi-step work because that is out of scope for a gateway | real, actively maintained (commits land daily, per-module semver releases weekly), used in the open by a funded company, but its headline "50x faster than LiteLLM" benchmark is run against a 60ms mocked provider response by Bifrost's own admission, and no independent benchmark against a real provider was found |
| envoy-ai-gateway | aigw run is a genuinely fast local try-it path, but there is no admin UI, no platform RBAC or SSO, no prompt or response logging and nothing to watch or approve, because it is a wire-level proxy with no task concept at all. | real and used in anger (multi-vendor maintainers, quarterly releases, a written API-stability promise, three commercial products built on it per an outside review), but it just underwent a governance and branding change (Envoy sub-project under CNCF to standalone Agentic AI Foundation project, new org, new domain) in the same month as this research, worth re-checking link and doc stability in a few months. |
| tensorzero | A clean observability and dataset UI for people who already think in "functions" and "variants," but no task board, no approval step and, since the shutdown, the docs site itself 301-redirects to the GitHub repo. | Was real and used in anger (claimed roughly 1% of global LLM API spend, a public reference customer, 11.7k stars honestly earned as GitHub's number one trending repo). Not slop, but dead -- the company wound down and archived the repo on 2026-06-12, so it is a frozen Apache-2.0 snapshot, not a maintained dependency. |
| weave-router | Fast to trial (one npx command patches Claude Code, Codex or opencode in place) with a live dashboard, a session-cost endpoint and a route dry-run endpoint, but all of that UX is scoped to routing transparency, not to running or supervising agent work. | Real and actively engineered: a release roughly every one to two days, a reproducible benchmark harness shipped in the repo, and a documented production incident that changed the architecture. Not a README with a landing page, though its own marketing numbers are looser than its own bench data. |
| emdash | The best diff, checks and multi-agent terminal UX we reviewed, built entirely around one human watching and steering, not around unattended autonomy. | Real and actively maintained. 125 contributors, weekly releases, a positive independent review, and a codebase mid-rewrite (three ADRs replacing the same workspace abstraction inside months) rather than a README with a landing page. |
| humanlayer-codelayer | The best-built part of the project, a fast keyboard-driven desktop console with real approve, deny and fork controls, undercut by running with zero sandboxing on the operator's own machine. | Real and used in anger for about seventeen months, then abandoned in place rather than removed, real commits stop 2026-01-07, a lone deprecation commit lands 2026-06-19, and the maker's own README now points users to a closed, paid rebuild. |
| superset | Richer moment to moment surface for a human watching a handful of agents than our console offers today, but built around a present human, not unattended task throughput. | Real and actively shipping but eleven months old, and its own and outside marketing round a source-available Elastic licence up to open source. |
| untrivial-agent-orchestrator | Clearly ahead of our console on watching and steering a running agent, with real, independently reported rough edges around stale status and idle approval waits. | Real and actively maintained, not a landing-page project, but corporate lineage and domain naming have churned twice in months and the strongest security architecture is locked behind an unreleased, closed-source Cloud tier. |
| vibe-kanban | Live WebSocket log and diff streaming, an embedded preview iframe with click-to-component feedback, and batched inline review comments are exactly the three things our htmx console admits it lacks. | Real and used in anger (28k stars, 64 contributors, a claimed 100,000 PRs and 30,000 active users, an active GitHub debate about its own UX changes), but its commercial backer folded in April 2026, its commit history shows a 4.5-month gap after that, and its Security page's vault, SSO and zeroize claims describe a Cloud backend that is not in the public repository and is itself being deleted. |
| langwatch | The Sessions and Pull Requests pages are close to the replay and cost view our console is missing, well built but read only, a human still approves and merges on GitHub or in their own terminal, not inside LangWatch. | Real and actively worked, a commit landed today, a point release shipped two days ago, and 152 numbered decision records sit behind a nine-person, single-round seed company, not a landing page. |
| promptfoo | npx promptfoo@latest init, eval, view is three commands to a working diff-capable result, and the web viewer's per-cell pass-fail matrix is the diff view our console lacks, but it is a single-purpose eval viewer with no kanban, no cost-per-task view and no approval lane. | Real and used in anger: 25.6k stars, 355 contributors, 426 releases, daily commits, a genuine internal layered-architecture ratchet and a 256-test scheduler subsystem, not a README with a landing page. The open question is roadmap independence once the pending OpenAI acquisition closes. |
| arcade-dev | CLI-first and fast to a first tool call, but the run-and-watch surface is the cloud-only dashboard, unverified from the repo | real and actively maintained, well-funded, used by named customers, but the advertised authorization platform is split between this thin open SDK and a closed SaaS control plane |
| clawpatrol | A working local dashboard and a five-command CLI path from install to a wrapped agent, but approval and audit both live behind one UI with no exportable log by default. | Real and actively developed, not a landing page. Alpha by the maker's own admission, with an open default-allow gap and an unresolved approval-retry UX still in review. |
| infisical-agent-vault | A working wrapped agent behind the proxy in about five CLI commands, with a clean browser page for approving proposals, but nothing to watch once it is running because the product has no notion of a task. | Real and actively built, not a landing page. Thirty releases in six months, a large test suite, 16 contributors. But Infisical's own blog calls it an experimental "research preview" expecting a redesign, and most non-bot commits come from a single author, so treat interfaces as unstable. |
| keycard | A tight, single-developer CLI loop (`keycard run -- claude`, then ask Claude in plain language what is allowed or why a tool was blocked) with no visible equivalent to a fleet-wide task board, since watching and steering many concurrent agent runs is simply outside what this product does. | Real company, real funding, real and actively committed-to open SDKs, but the coding-agent product is early access as of its March 2026 launch. Codex is named in launch marketing with no corresponding open plugin or hook code found anywhere in the `keycardai` GitHub org, only Claude Code has one. |
| onecli | A one-command self-hosted install and a chat dashboard with inline approval cards beat our functional console, but the design assumes one human driving one agent by chat, not watching many autonomous tasks queue toward a PR. | Real and actively maintained, 355 commits and 30 releases in about six months with a genuine Show HN launch and at least one independent adopter, the ee split is narrow and disclosed and the core security code stays Apache-2.0. |
| toolhive | CLI- and YAML-only in the open-source core (thv, kubectl, Helm), no screenshots or in-flight call visibility in the docs, no approval step before a tool runs; a separate closed-by-us-unverified desktop app (toolhive-studio) targets individual developers but was not inspected. | Real and used in anger. Daily commits, a release every one to two days, 132 contributors, 306 forks, and a genuinely deep internal design (dual MCP protocol revisions, a documented Cedar edge case in conflict resolution). Not a README with a landing page. |
| cubesandbox | A more polished single-purpose UX than our console for its own narrow job (live sandbox list, template catalog, streaming logs, command palette), but it manages microVMs, not coding tasks, so nothing here replaces our missing task kanban, diff view or approval flow | Real and active (1000+ commits, 100+ contributors, biweekly releases, a named production adopter in MiniMax), but several performance numbers are self-reported or uncited and Kubernetes support is an explicit preview untested against Cilium, our own CNI |
| docker-sandboxes | The fastest zero-to-first-result path in this survey for a coding-agent tool (Docker Desktop ships the plugin, one API key, docker agent run), and Board's Kanban-plus-diff-plus-live-tmux-attach TUI is closer to what our console still owes its users than anything else surveyed so far, even though it manages developer sessions, not factory tasks. | Real and shipped by a large, active vendor (near-daily docker-agent releases, 3.3k and 1.6k GitHub stars across the two open repos, Cloud Sandboxes launched five days before this report), but it is three fast-moving products under one name with a mid-2026 rebrand already behind it (cagent to Docker Agent) and its governance and audit story sold, not published. |
| k8s-agent-sandbox | A working sandbox is close in Python (pip install, SandboxClient, create_sandbox, commands.run) once a cluster, CRDs, and a WarmPool already exist, but that prerequisite setup is kubectl/YAML work with no UI; a dashboard is a roadmap item, not shipped. | Real and actively used, not a landing page; weekly releases, a working GKE production consumer (GKE Agent Sandbox GA May 2026, Google states 16x sandbox growth and named adopters LangChain and Lovable), a formal KEP process, and a threat-model doc that names its own unfixed gaps rather than hiding them. |
| microsandbox | `curl | sh` then `msb run debian` is a two-command path from zero to a shell inside an isolated microVM, no server to stand up; ahead of our console for a developer driving sandboxes directly, but there is no web UI, task board or approval flow at all. | Real and shipping weekly (v0.7.4 landed the day this report was written), 30 contributors, used by third parties (Vercel's Eve, a US GSA coding-agent quickstart), but still labelled beta software, the company renamed itself mid-flight (Zerocore AI to Super Rad Company), and its own AGENTS.md documents a real shipped SDK/runtime incompatibility between v0.6.9 and v0.6.10. |
| nvidia-openshell | CLI and TUI only, no web console. The policy.local self-service denial API is a good idea we lack, but there is nothing here like a kanban board or a human approval lane. | Real and actively maintained, 1558 commits, 99 releases, near-daily cadence, 13 named maintainers, a 28 Sep 2026 NVIDIA GA launch and positive independent hands-on coverage from Red Hat. Not a landing-page project. |
| kiro | A real multi-surface product with checkpoints, session replay and wave-based task execution beats our console today, but independent reviewers still call it a rough, credit-hungry v1.x tool. | Real and used in anger. AWS sells it as a paid product, a Financial Times-reported AWS outage was attributed to it, and its issue tracker carries over a thousand open issues, not a landing page with no substance behind it. |
| superpowers | zero-config once installed (a SessionStart hook fires the bootstrap on every session and after every compaction), narrates itself as it works ("Using [skill] to [purpose]"), and a user intervenes by typing, not by clicking through a dashboard | real, in daily use including by the person who wrote this report, actively released weekly with named eval evidence attached to skill-behaviour changes, and the 94%-PR-rejection contributor bar in AGENTS.md is itself evidence the maintainers are not coasting |
| cleric | Read-only credentials connected in an afternoon, Slack-native alerts with confidence scores and an approve-or-request-changes PR flow are materially ahead of our console's current lack of real-time streams and diff views, but the whole surface is a closed SaaS product tour, not something we can inspect end to end. | Real and funded (9.8M USD seed, a Gartner Cool Vendor citation, a named production customer since 2025) with technical depth beyond the landing page in two ZenML case studies, but there is no public code, an empty trust.cleric.ai page despite a SOC 2 Type II claim on the homepage, and no independent user discussion found on Reddit or Hacker News. |
| datadog-bits-ai | From an existing Datadog estate, a user reaches a proposed pull request in minutes with one click on an alert or issue, which is materially ahead of our console's admitted lack of a live stream or diff view, but the whole experience only exists inside an estate already fully instrumented with Datadog. | Real and shipping revenue for a public company, not a landing page, but every reliability number found (up to 95 percent faster resolution, 70 percent MTTR cut) comes from Datadog's own marketing or hand-picked customer quotes, and no independent benchmark of its root-cause accuracy was found. |
| latitude | Fast, polished self-serve path (four lines of code to a first trace, a clean signals and trace UI, CLI/MCP/web parity), but the product is blind to the fix itself, once dispatched it only shows accepted-or-not and an outbound link. | Real and actively worked (daily commits, weekly releases, 4.7k stars) but built by a small core team at a roughly 13-person company with a documented history of pivots (data apps, then a prompt playground, then Latitude Agents, then the current agent-observability-only V2), and the "verify the fix" claim in the README overstates what ships, the gate is a pattern the customer assembles, not an automatic block. |
| sentry-seer | Zero extra onboarding for an existing Sentry user and a live reasoning drawer beat our static console today, but Seer only ever shows one workflow per issue, never a cross project queue. | Real and used in anger, with production code level fixability gating and a reported 38000 plus auto fixed issues, but the once public core agent repo went private in 2025 and self hosted users have no date to ever get it, the sharpest open core bait and switch found in this survey. |
| chatdev | The web canvas plus real-time WebSocket log streaming beats our static kanban for watching an agent work, but the only intervention point is an unscoped free-text human node, not a typed approve, redirect or deny. | Real and actively maintained, but no longer the software-factory project it is famous for. That identity now lives on a comparatively frozen legacy branch, and independent research found the original's reviewer agents shallow and its benchmarks irreproducible. |
| crewai | Fewer steps to a first local multi-agent run than our console needs for a first task (scaffold, edit two JSON files, `crewai run`), but once a crew is executing there is no view into it beyond stdout and a blocking input() call for human-in-the-loop; CrewAI AMP's dashboard, tracing and approval UI could not be verified beyond marketing screenshots and copy. | Real and heavily used, 59k stars, weekly patch releases, a claimed 2 billion agent executions in the last 12 months, and 336+ contributors. The paid AMP layer, which is where governance and security live, is pure marketing copy behind a demo gate with no pricing, no self-hosting detail and no independent verification found anywhere. |
| langgraph | Minutes from pip install to a running graph in code, but nothing to watch or approve without buying the separate, proprietary LangSmith/Studio layer. | The library is real and heavily used (42.5k stars, weekly releases, cited by Klarna, Uber, Elastic, Replit), but the production server it steers you toward is a closed, source-unavailable binary, not the open-source deal the product page implies. |
| metagpt | Genuinely fast from pip install to a generated repo (three commands, one idea string), but it is a batch run watched as scrolling terminal output, not a workflow with approval or mid-run intervention beyond Ctrl-C or swapping a role's LLM for HumanProvider. | Real and heavily used code, not a landing page, but the open-source repository has gone quiet since mid-2025 (README edits and one Windows-terminal patch are the last commits, pushed 2026-01-21) while the same team's funding and attention moved to its closed commercial product, renamed Atoms in January 2026. Treat it as a mature but now lightly-maintained research artifact. |
| openai-agents-sdk | Excellent developer experience for writing an agent (clean quickstart, a REPL via repl.py, graph visualisation via visualization.py, a hosted trace viewer), but there is no operator-facing surface at all for watching or approving a fleet of tasks; that is entirely the embedding app's job, unlike our console. | Real and heavily used, OpenAI's own first-party SDK with 29.7k stars, near-weekly releases and a same-day-fresh commit, but its own CONTRIBUTING.md says external pull requests are not accepted at all, only issues, so "open source" here means readable and forkable, not community-governed. |
| lovable | best in class for one person continuously steering a single visual project by chat, click-to-select and image reference, with almost no onboarding friction; it has no concept of a human who is absent when work starts or who is reviewing many unattended tasks at once | real and used at large scale (reported 600M USD ARR run-rate, 8M+ users, named enterprise customers), but its default security posture produced two well-documented breaches in thirteen months, so the "AI tests your apps" and enterprise-security marketing outruns what ships by default |
| replit-agent | Checkpoints, bidirectional rollback and a per-sub-task kanban board with an explicit merge gate put Replit's single-project experience well ahead of our console for one person watching one agent, but there is no cross-task fleet view, and the July 2025 incident shows the safety story was retrofitted onto that UX, not designed into it from the start. | Real and heavily used at a 9B USD valuation with 150,000+ paying customers and near-monthly feature shipping, but it marketed itself as "the safest place for vibe coding" for roughly a year before the one hard safety boundary it now has, dev and production database separation, existed at all. |
| dagger-container-use | onboarding is one CLI install plus one MCP-server line per repository, then the agent is told to use it via a rules file; from there container-use list/log/diff/terminal give more granular, cheaper-to-check observability into what an agent did than our console currently offers, but it is a single-developer CLI with no web view, no kanban, no approval workflow and no cost or audit view. | real and used in anger (33 contributors, its own git-history path-traversal bug fixed via a PR co-authored by an AI agent, listed in independent MCP-tool directories and 2026 agent-sandbox roundups), but still labelled "early development" 16 months after its June 2025 launch, no tagged release since August 2025 despite commits continuing on main into August 2026, and 20 open feature/dependabot branches against a main branch that has not moved in 48 days as of this report. |
| depot | depot claude to a running sandbox with a shareable, resumable session is fewer steps and a better persistence story than our console offers today, but sessions are async-only with no live interactive control and no diff or approval view, so it is a better session log than an agent cockpit. | the company and its CI/build product are real and funded (YC W23, a 2026 Series A, named customers running real volume such as PostHog and Astral), but the headline "Claude Code sandbox" feature is young (launched Aug 2025), the docs never name the isolation technology, and competitor write-ups (Northflank, Blaxel, so read with that bias in mind) describe the original release as plain containers, not microVMs, a gap Depot's own July 2026 Depot Metal post appears to close without saying so explicitly. |
| namespace | The fastest zero-to-first-result loop seen in this survey for a ci-build tool, one label swap in a workflow file, plus SSH/VNC debugging into a live runner and a usage dashboard broken down by repository and shape, ahead of anything our console shows for a CI run today. | Real and used in anger. 23m USD raised, a changelog shipping every few days through all of 2026, SOC 2 Type II, 1,000+ claimed customers with named references, and paid integration partnerships with Cursor and Cognition (Devin) for agent compute, not a landing page over a thin product. |
| nx-self-healing-ci | The fastest approval loop in this survey for a single fix: reasoning, diff and Apply/Reject land directly on the PR or in the IDE, ahead of anything our console offers today, but it covers exactly one workflow (repair a broken CI task), not a board of tasks across a project. | Real and used in anger: 29.4k stars, near-daily commits, $24.6M raised, named enterprise customers, and a public incident record. But the self-healing feature itself is unverifiable from outside: no independent reviews found, only Nx's own blog, webinar and staff social posts claiming '50% faster time to green', and the pricing page even contradicts itself on the Team tier's starting price. |
| greptile | Zero-setup review that lands as comments inside the PR the developer is already looking at, with in-PR chat and one-click handoff to a coding agent, the lowest-friction UX in this survey, bought by being narrow, review only, no task board, no cross-repo view. | Real product with paying customers and institutional funding (YC W24, Benchmark Series A at 180M USD), but every bug-catch and noise number in circulation is either vendor-published or from a competing vendor's comparison page, and the figures contradict each other between sources. Treat all comparative numbers as marketing until reproduced independently. |
| plannotator | best-in-class synchronous review UX for one attended human watching one agent session; nothing here addresses watching or approving an unattended, queued fleet of async tasks the way our console's kanban view has to. | real and actively maintained, near-daily releases, 9k stars, a working SLSA/SBOM release gate, Show HN traction and third-party comparison pages, with a dominant but genuine solo maintainer, not a landing page over an empty repo. |
| qodo-pr-agent | Fastest onboarding of anything looked at so far, one workflow file and two repository secrets, or one pip install and one CLI command, but there is nothing to watch or approve because each command is a single short completion, not a run. | Real and very actively maintained, weekly releases, 378 contributor pages and a commit dated today, but mid governance transplant, Qodo handed it to a new community org in April 2026 and its own marketing site no longer names it, so its next year of maintenance is unproven. |
| aider | Three commands from zero to a first edit, a visible diff and an exact cost line after every turn, but everything happens in one terminal with one human watching one session, nothing like our queued, parallel, kanban model. | Real and heavily used (7M+ pip installs, 15B tokens/week claimed) but strained. One maintainer, 512 open PRs, no tagged GitHub release since August 2025, and a community fork (aider-ce, now cecli) formed in late 2025 specifically because upstream stopped merging. |
| plandex | A capable single-developer terminal tool (REPL, chat then tell mode, streaming TUI) that is easy to start (curl install, one env var, plandex in a project dir) but has no team-facing console, no web UI, and depends on a user actively present at a terminal. | Real and was used in anger (15.7k stars, a genuine architecture, 50 migrations over two years), but now dormant. The company behind it wound the hosted product down in late 2025, plandex.ai and docs.plandex.ai no longer resolve (checked 2026-09-29), the last feature commit is from 2025-07-16, and issues keep arriving in 2026 with no maintainer response. |
| dbos | Conductor's workflow list, retry and cancel actions are a clean narrow tool for one job (workflow ops) but it is paywalled past one executor and has no idea an agent or a task exists. | Real and maintained, daily commits, credible founders (Stonebraker, Zaharia), but a small team (about a dozen people per Tracxn) on one $8.5M seed round, and the commercial product is a classic open-core wrapper around the free library. |
| hatchet | The dashboard gives a live run list, a per-run trace and log timeline, and one-click replay from a checkpoint, which is materially ahead of our console's current no-live-stream, no-diff-view state, but it is watching Hatchet's own task graph, not an agent's diff or reasoning. | Real and used in anger, 8k stars, 89+ contributors, near-daily SDK releases, and testimonials from agent-heavy customers (Cursor, Greptile, Distill) running large volumes of tasks; independent comparisons treat it as a genuine Temporal alternative, not a thin wrapper. |
| inngest | npx inngest-cli@latest dev to a working dashboard with live step traces and replay in under a minute is a strong bar for local dev loop speed, but the dashboard watches request/response steps, not an agent working through a codebase, so it has nothing to say about diff review or approval UX. | Real and used in anger: active daily commits, a 2026 Series A, a deep Redis queue implementation with real production concerns (backlog normalization, shadow queues, scavenging). The SSPL license and Enterprise-gated SSO/audit/HIPAA are open-core gating dressed as 'self-hostable', not slop, but worth reading past the README's plain 'open source' framing. |
| restate | brew, npx or docker to a running server and a first traced invocation in about two minutes per the Quickstart, with a built-in web UI and CLI to inspect, pause, cancel and restart individual invocations, which is a stronger single-task trace view than our console has today, but the developer still writes and deploys handler code, there is no board or human-approval surface out of the box | real and actively developed, not a README project: a large multi-crate Rust workspace with its own raft-based metadata store and replicated log, a paying customer list (Replit, Capital One, Maersk) on the homepage, and a commit landing on the day of this report, but still a single seed-funded company (one 7M USD round from June 2024, about 20 staff per Tracxn) with the BSL clock running on every release |
| temporal | Temporal ships no agent-facing UI. Its Web UI is a workflow-execution debugger, and even Workflow Streams, its newest AI-adjacent feature, is a plumbing primitive for someone else's console to consume, not a console itself. | Real and used at large scale in production (OpenAI and Replit are named reference customers, 43 million open-source installs and 4,300 paying customers claimed in the Series E announcement); the MIT-licensed server core is untouched by the funding run-up, but several of the specific agent features asked about here are recent Public Preview or Pre-release additions riding a valuation that tripled in about seven months, not features proven at GA. |
| code-index-for-agents | codebase-memory-mcp reaches a working MCP server and a 3D graph view in under a minute with no account. Sourcebot needs Postgres, Redis, a config file and a paid seat before any agent tool works at all | codebase-memory-mcp is real, working code with an unusual growth pattern for one person (about 45k stars in 7 months, one dominant committer, marketing that outruns its own arXiv paper's numbers). Sourcebot is a normal two-year-old YC-backed product used in production, with a standard and fairly aggressive open-core paywall, not vapourware |
| ralph | Zero-account, CLI-only, about five steps from a written PRD to a running loop, but watching Ralph work means reading raw terminal scrollback, there is no pause, approve, or multi-run view, only Ctrl+C. | Real and genuinely viral (21.9k stars on the reference repo alone, a wave of independent blog and video coverage through early 2026, half a dozen independent reimplementations), but folklore around one person's blog posts, not a maintained product, with no company, no support and no roadmap behind it. |
| dapr-agents | No UI beyond a raw HITL REST endpoint and generic OTel traces; watching or approving a run means writing a client or curling the sidecar yourself. | Real and maintained, not a landing page. v1.0 GA in March 2026, monthly point releases, a DCO- and lint-gated CI, and named production users. |
| litellm | There is no agent-facing UX here, only an admin dashboard we do not deploy, our own console reads LiteLLM spend through its API instead. | Not slop. 374 contributors, several releases a week, named production adopters and a maintained, funded bug bounty are the opposite of a README with a landing page, but the 2026 security record shows real attackers now treat it as a serious target too. |
| portkey | The two-minute npx quickstart and local log console beat our console's onboarding for a lone developer, but the console only shows request logs, not agent runs, and there is no approval or intervention model at all because there is no task concept. | Real and used in anger before 2026 (1T+ tokens/day and 24,000+ orgs claimed around the Series A), not a README project, but development looks stalled since the Palo Alto Networks acquisition closed: no commits to main and no release since before the deal. |
| claude-squad | Best-in-class terminal UX for one developer juggling several coding-agent sessions, but it is a session manager, not a factory. Nothing happens unless a human is attached. | Real and used. 8500-plus stars, 620-plus forks, 20 contributors, steady releases since March 2025 through August 2026, plain AGPL-3.0 licence, no landing-page hype. Not slop. |
| conductor | The most polished per-task console we found, a workspace per branch with its own terminal, live diff and a merged Checks pane, checkpoints for rollback, one-click PR, and a mobile app to follow along, but it is a human-supervised multiplier for one developer, not an unattended factory console. | Real and funded (22 million dollar Series A, March 2026, Matrix and Spark), SOC 2 Type II attested, shipping a public changelog in the 0.8x range, and reportedly used daily inside several YC companies. Proprietary and closed source throughout, and the promised self-hosted offering does not exist yet, so "enterprise-ready" is aspirational on that axis. |
| crystal | fast, opinionated, single-user desktop UX for running several coding-agent sessions in parallel git worktrees, the best public example found of the diff plus stream plus inline approve pattern our console still lacks. | real and used in anger, 3.1k stars, 15 contributors, granular changelog across eight months, but now frozen. Deprecated February 2026 in favour of a commercial successor, Nimbalyst, with a different scope and a separate repository. Treat as a closed reference, not a moving target. |
| google-antigravity | Agent Manager, Artifacts and browser-verification recordings answer three gaps our console admits it has (no live stream, no diff view, no replay), but reliability complaints, unpredictable quota cutoffs and a forced breaking 2.0 upgrade show the UX is still unstable in production use. | Real and heavily resourced, backed by a 2.4bn USD talent deal and still shipping point releases into mid-2026, but a documented RCE that bypassed its strictest security mode, a viral drive-deletion incident, and a backlash-triggering breaking upgrade all landed inside its first seven months. |
| happy | A phone push that opens straight into a diff and an approve or deny button beats anything our console offers today, and it took the team roughly four steps of onboarding to get there. | Real and actively maintained by a small independent team, not a marketing shell, but it is a personal remote-control tool for one human's own coding-agent session, not a factory. |
| orca | The best UX we have reviewed for watching and steering several coding agents at once; it has no equivalent of an approval workflow, an audit trail, or a cost view for a second reviewer. | Real and heavily used, with daily releases and 390+ contributors, but the security model is a marketing feature ("bring your own subscription, no sandbox") rather than an oversight, and a live, unresolved GitHub issue shows that trade-off breaking in the wild. |
| arize-phoenix | pip install and phoenix serve get a running trace UI in under a minute, and the trace, eval and playground screens beat our console for watching and replaying a single model call, but there is no task queue, no approval flow and no kanban of parallel agent work anywhere in the product. | Real and used in anger: 11.6k stars, near-daily releases across seven packages, and it is the tracing layer behind several named coding-agent integrations. Not a landing-page project. The open question is roadmap independence once the pending Dynatrace acquisition closes. |
| braintrust | Strong for what it does, live trace search, prompt and model comparison, one-click trace-to-dataset, natural-language search over logs, all absent from our console. But it is a viewer for calls a user's own code already made, not a place to watch or steer a running agent task. | Real and used in anger, 121M USD raised, named customers, autoevals at 1000-plus stars, near daily commits across 30-plus repos. The slop is in the word self-hosted, only half the stack actually moves to the customer. |
| future-agi | Fast to a first trace (SDK install plus a few lines of instrumentation code) with a real, screenshot-documented dashboard, but the product is an observability console you watch after the fact, not a console for approving or steering an agent while it works. | Not slop. A real, large, actively developed product (2,939 frontend files, 1,187 backend test files, 52 e2e specs, a live security bounty) built by a small named team since an April 2026 open-sourcing of an already 2024-founded, funded platform, but its marketing outruns its code in specific, checkable places: two of six routing strategies are broken or unwired, a benchmark directory the README links to twice does not exist in the repo, and these gaps are already the subject of open GitHub issues filed by an outside user on the same day as this review. |
| harbor | The CLI (harbor run, hr/hb aliases) gets a first trial running with no account in one command, but the useful cross-model comparison view only lives in the separate apps/viewer web app, not in CLI output. | Real and actively maintained, 28 releases in six months, commits as recent as 2026-09-28, official successor harness to Terminal-Bench, backed by the well-funded Laude Institute. The commercial Hub layer sits under a separate for-profit, HarborCo Inc., with no published pricing, worth watching for an open-core split. |
| helicone | Two lines of code and a base URL swap get a first logged request in minutes; the dashboard, sessions view and playground are materially ahead of our htmx console's current state. | Real and used in anger (16,000 orgs, 14.2 trillion tokens over three years, per the founders), but Mintlify acquired the company in March 2026 and put it into maintenance mode (security and bug fixes only), so treat anything beyond what already exists in the repo as unlikely to arrive. |
| laminar | Fast to first trace (SDK install, two lines of code, or one docker compose up) and its trace and debugger UI is far richer than our console's flat kanban, but it is a dashboard to look at, not a place to approve or steer a running agent. | Real and used in anger, 3287 stars, 29 contributors, near-daily commits, YC-backed with named customers (Browser Use, OpenHands), but the README's checked-off "Signals" box ships as a closed-source no-op in the public tree, disclosed only in source comments, not in the README. |
| langfuse | A mature, well-documented dashboard for humans watching traces and evaluations, plus one genuinely strong pattern, an SSE-tailed and approval-aware live agent run, that our own console has nowhere at all. | Real and used in anger: 2,000+ paying customers and 26M+ monthly SDK installs per ClickHouse's own acquisition announcement, daily commits, and CVEs (RBAC leak, SSRF, SSO bypass) patched within weeks of disclosure rather than ignored. |
| langsmith | The best trace/replay visualization we have seen in this survey (Trajectory and Details tabs, Insights clustering, Prompt Hub diffing) but zero task queue, approval flow or kanban, because the product answers 'what happened', never 'what should happen next'. | Real and used in anger: SOC 2 Type II since 2024, 12x year-on-year trace volume growth by the vendor's own account, 35% of the Fortune 500 claimed as users, and a 12,000-line client (client.py) doing real async batching, not a stub. Two disclosed CVEs (CVSS 8.8 and 8.5, both patched) and near-universal third-party advice to pick Langfuse instead above modest trace volume temper the marketing claim of being framework-agnostic and effortless. |
| opik | Better trace and evaluation drill-down than our console today (per-span tree, dataset/experiment diffing, prompt playground), but it has no task lane, no approval flow and no kanban, because it solves a different problem than orchestration. | Real and actively used, not a landing page. Apache-2.0 core, near-daily releases, 22k+ stars, named production users (AssemblyAI, Stability AI per Comet's own page, not independently verified). The marketing language ("true open-source project") is honest about the OSS licence but glosses over the missing self-hosted user management, confirmed both in the code and by independent reviews. |
| cedar | A policy authoring tool, not an operator console. Readable syntax, an editor extension, and inside AgentCore a plain English to Cedar generator. Nothing to watch once wired in, a decision returns inline in the same request. | Real. Daily commits, a six week release cadence, CNCF Sandbox status since October 2025, and shipped in anger as the tool call authorization layer of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Policy, general availability March 2026. |
| composio | A five-line SDK quickstart and a hosted connections dashboard, fast for a developer wiring up one more app to one agent, but built around one user's one app connection, not a fleet of concurrent tasks to watch, approve or stop. | Real, funded and used in production (200+ customers including Glean, 100k+ developers claimed, 30.4k stars, a commit the same day as this report), but with a real scar record: a disclosed May 2026 credential-store breach and four independently confirmed CVEs on NVD, including a critical authentication bypass and a 2026 path-validation bypass reachable by prompt injection. Not a README with a landing page, but not a platform to trust with sensitive credentials without its own compensating controls. |
| cycles | Zero-infrastructure onboarding (mock-mode MCP tool, one-command quickstart.sh) beats ours, but there is no verified working dashboard, no kanban-style view, and no human-approval lane, only inline allow/deny/cap and webhook events an operator must build their own UI around. | Real, tested and thoughtfully specified (atomic Lua scripts, property-based concurrency tests, fail-closed design), but effectively a one-founder project padded with an AI commit author, 30 releases and a 20-repo org against 5 GitHub stars, 2 forks and zero independent reviews or discussion. |
| invariant-labs | A code-first rule DSL and a trace viewer for developers, no task or approval concept, so nothing here for a non-technical reviewer to click through. | Real, used code across three repos, including an ACM/IEEE citation, but effectively frozen. The parent folded it into a paid product and shut down the only hosted instance in January 2026. |
| iron-proxy | three commands from clone to a first allow/deny/secret-swap result in the checked-in docker-compose example, but the OSS product has no dashboard, replay or approval surface at all, only a JSON log stream | real and actively maintained (191 commits, 67 releases in under six months, integration tests against live AWS, Vault, 1Password and GCP backends, signed release checksums), but effectively a single-maintainer project under six months old, with the valuable fleet layer pushed into a separate, unconfirmed-licence commercial product |
| ms-agent-governance-toolkit | A pip install and two lines of code get a first policy decision fast, and a CLI (agt doctor, agt verify, agt red-team scan) and a Streamlit demo dashboard exist, but there is no first-party console for watching or intervening in a running multi-step agent task the way our kanban board does. | Real and fast-moving, not a landing page (3,976 commits summed in the top-100 contributor stats alone, 6,362 stars, MIT-licensed, a named multi-org steering committee, 992-test conformance suite, an external red-team review and its own SOC 2 mapping), but one author holds 900 of those commits, dependabot holds 857 more, an account literally named claude has 160, the v5.0.0 tag has no published GitHub Release seven months in, and the project's own compliance docs and an external red-team report both confirm real gaps behind the marketing (partial PII redaction, an in-memory-only audit chain by default, admitted fail-open corners). |
| anthropic-sandbox-runtime | One command (npm install -g, then srt COMMAND) gets a sandboxed process running; watching and intervening is CLI-only (violation log stream on macOS, strace on Linux, control-fd to push policy changes), no console or approval workflow of its own. | Real and used in anger, it is the actual mechanism behind Claude Code's own /sandbox feature, with weekly point releases and 34 contributors, but Anthropic still labels it a Beta Research Preview, and independent researchers found and Anthropic quietly patched at least two real bypasses without a public advisory. |
| daytona | One of the shortest quickstarts checked (account, API key, four lines of SDK code), plus SSH, VNC and a web terminal straight into a live sandbox; but there is no task, no approval lane, no idea an agent is running, only a compute resource a human can poke at. | Real and heavily used (71.7k stars, about 230 contributors, three priced funding rounds, a promptly patched CVE), not a landing-page shell, but its open-source and self-hostable claims that are still all over the internet stopped being true in June 2026. |
| e2b | two files and one command to a running Firecracker sandbox in about two minutes beats anything we offer a newcomer, but it is a generic sandbox demo with no task, approval or diff view because there is no task concept. | real and actively developed (commits today on both repos, 14k/1.6k stars), but its two deployment shapes that matter for a self-operated enterprise (BYOC, private cloud) are Enterprise-only and negotiated, and its own secrets-resolution component is closed source. |
| edera | Infra-ops UX (kubectl, Helm, edera-check reports), not agent UX; no dashboard, no task view, because it solves a different problem than ours | Real, funded, audited (Trail of Bits, zero high/medium findings) and actively shipping (v1.12.7, 36 releases since April 2025), but the interesting core is closed, undisclosed-priced and enterprise-sales-led, and the only performance numbers anywhere trace back to the vendor's own arXiv paper |
| fly-sprites | Fast and pleasant for one machine (CLI, SDKs, MCP, preinstalled agent plugins), but no multi-task oversight view, no diff view, no per-action approval, intervention is "restore a checkpoint." | Real and used in anger, but young and still finding its shape; a founder-admitted "skunkworks" project pivoted into the company's main focus after a funding round, with a rougher-than-average 2026 uptime record for a service meant to hold an agent's only copy of its state. |
| modal-sandboxes | Four lines of Python from pip install to a running sandboxed command, backed by a paid dashboard for logs and tunnels, but there is no task-level UI at all, this is a developer building block, not an agent-facing console. | Real and used in anger, not a landing page. SWE-bench Verified runs behind a --modal flag, Quora/Poe, Codegen and Anthropic's own Claude Managed Agents integration all run production workloads on it, and the company is raising at a reported 15.75B USD valuation. The sandbox engine itself is closed source behind a paid SDK, so none of that activity is independently auditable. |
| bmad-method | Good ergonomics for a human pairing with an agent in their own editor (named personas, numbered menus, hard checkpoints, git-visible plan and ticket files as state) but there is no dashboard, no queue view and no way to watch or steer work you did not start yourself. | Real, actively maintained and clearly used in anger (53.6k stars, near-daily commits, a real test and validation suite, an ecosystem of official add-on modules and third-party products), but architecturally still moving fast, it rewrote itself from an agent-team-file model to the current Jinja-rendered skills model within about a year, and a maintainer-engaged, since-closed GitHub issue (#2003) catalogues real structural contradictions in the prior v6 stable release. |
| openspec | Best-in-class onboarding for injecting a workflow into a tool you already use, but there is no agent-run UI at all because OpenSpec never runs the agent. | Real and actively used, not a landing page, but a one-founder YC company with no enforcement behind its own convention and a recurring independent complaint of spec drift. |
| spec-kit | five to six manual chat turns from init to a built feature, each gated on a human reading the previous artifact, no dashboard, no parallel view. | real and heavily used, but GitHub calls it an experiment not a product, the documents are convention not enforcement, and the CLI has broken past tutorials with flag removals. |
| tessl | Two commands (tessl init, tessl install tessl-labs/spec-driven-development) are a lower floor than anything in our console, but there is no board, no run history and no way to watch or interrupt a task beyond the host agent's own chat, because Tessl is not itself an agent runtime. | Real company and real usage (107 npm releases in a year, $125M raised, named enterprise logos on the homepage, not independently verified), but the specific spec-driven-development-tile that defines this category entry is a secondary, largely dormant artifact (14 commits, none since March 2026) next to a company that pivoted twice in eighteen months, from Framework to Registry to skills-and-governance. |
